# Imperially Compiled Balanced Interpretations of the Zhou Changes

## Juan Eleven · 卷十一

**Commentary on the Images, Upper Part: Qian through Clinging.**

[Chinese source](../source/juan-11.md) · [Translation conventions](README.md) · [Translator's endnotes](#translator-endnotes)

**Voices:** “Original Meaning” is Zhu Xi; “Cheng's Commentary” is Cheng Yi; “Editorial Judgment” belongs to the imperial compilers. Translator's interventions appear separately in the endnotes. Great Images concern whole hexagrams; Small Images explain individual lines. Added line labels identify their places and do not add oracle statements.

## 1. Qian 乾

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4640 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:001 -->

**Commentary on the Images, Upper Part**[^j11-layer]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Images” means the two images, upper and lower, of a hexagram, together with the six lines of those two images to which the Duke of Zhou attached his statements.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:003 -->

### Great Image: Heaven moves with vigor. Thus the noble person strengthens himself without ceasing.[^j11-heaven-motion]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:004 -->

**Original Meaning.** Heaven is the image of Qian. Every doubled trigram takes the meaning of repetition. This one alone does not, because there is only one Heaven. Yet speaking of Heaven's movement shows that it completes one circuit in a day and another circuit the following day, as though there were an image of doubling and repetition. Nothing short of the utmost vigor could accomplish this. The noble person takes it as his model, not allowing human desires to injure the firmness of his heavenly virtue; thus he strengthens himself without ceasing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:005 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Image beneath a hexagram explains the image of the whole figure; the Image beneath a line explains that line's image. All the hexagrams offer images to be taken as models. Qian's image of sheltering and nurturing is so great that only a sage could embody it. Wishing everyone to have a model they could follow, the text therefore takes only Heaven's vigorous movement. The utmost vigor itself suffices to reveal Heaven's Way. The noble person's unceasing self-strengthening models the vigor of Heaven's movement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:006 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:007 -->

**You Zuo:** Utmost sincerity has no interruption: this is Heaven's vigorous movement, exemplified by the purity of King Wen's virtue. Not yet capable of having no interruption, yet refusing to cease, is the noble person's self-strengthening, exemplified by Yanzi's not departing from benevolence for three months.[^j11-unceasing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:008 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** The doubled Qian has Qian both above and below, but one cannot speak of two Heavens. Yesterday's movement is one Heaven; today's movement is also one Heaven. In reality there is one Heaven, moving vigorously without cease, yet presenting an image of doubled Heaven. Hence “Heaven moves with vigor.” The doubled Kun likewise has Kun above and below, but one cannot speak of two Earths. On level ground its compliance is not apparent; only in its successive layers of heights and depths is there an image of doubled Earth. Hence “Earth's configuration is Kun.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:009 -->

**Question:** “Heaven revolves without cease; the noble person likewise strengthens himself without cease.” **Answer:** “This does not mean that because Heaven revolves without cease, we must chase after it, trying to imitate its ceaselessness. Simply preserve this heart continually, and heavenly principle will continually act, circulating without interruption.” He also said: “Heaven's unceasing movement does not concern the four seasons alone. In a day, an hour, or the smallest instant, its movement has never ceased.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:010 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Of the four doubled figures in the Upper Classic, Qian says “Heaven moves,” Kun “Earth's configuration,” Kan “water arrives repeatedly,” and Li “brightness arises twice”: the underlying body precedes its activity. Of the four in the Lower Classic, Zhen says “repeated thunder,” Gen “joined mountains,” Xun “following winds,” and Dui “adjoining lakes”: activity precedes the underlying body. Qian and Kun do not speak of doubling, distinguishing them from the six children. Qian says “vigorous,” rather than “Qian,” distinguishing it from Kun.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:011 -->

**Cai Qing:** After explaining the hexagram names and statements, Confucius added the Great Images because explanations of names and statements are limited, whereas the rightness and principle within the sage's breast are inexhaustible. From “Heaven moves with vigor” to “fire above water: Before Completion,” and from the noble person's unceasing self-strengthening to his carefully distinguishing things and placing them in their proper domains, all unfold the sage's inward understanding through the figures.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:012 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** In elucidating the Changes, the Master had already explained the hexagram names and statements through the Judgment Commentary, the Words of the Text, and other writings. Seeing that the principles of the Changes were inexhaustible, he further combined the images of the two trigrams and composed a commentary to illuminate them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:013 -->

**He Kai:** Vigor without interruption is called Qian. The Doctrine of the Mean's “utmost sincerity has no interruption” relates this to Heaven. Self-strengthening speaks of “not ceasing,” rather than “having no interruption”: learning is the work of modeling oneself on Heaven. One begins by refusing to cease and ends by having no interruption. Thus, after speaking of having no interruption, the Doctrine of the Mean traces its source: “Not ceasing, one endures.” How does one strengthen oneself? By making reverence the governing practice. Through solemn reverence, the noble person grows stronger daily.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:014 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** In explaining a name, the Judgment Commentary may draw upon the hexagram's images, virtues, or structure. The Great Image Commentary establishes its meanings solely through the two images, without invoking virtues or structure. Moreover, the words beneath each Judgment have already specified particular ways of acting in human affairs and responding to the time. The Image Commentary speaks of the affairs of former kings, great persons, rulers, and noble persons. Much of this illuminates the Judgment's meaning, yet some establishes a meaning of its own, beyond the Judgment Commentary. Why? The commentaries on hexagram and line statements explain the writings of King Wen and the Duke of Zhou; the Great Image Commentary shows people the general approach to reading Fuxi's Changes. The structural rules that determine higher and lower rank and distinguish adjacency and correspondence are detailed and exact; we suspect that they became fully developed only with King Wen. When Fuxi first drew the figures, there were simply images of Heaven, Earth, mountain, lake, thunder, wind, water, and fire, as described in the Discussion of the Trigrams. Doubling them likewise involved simply the interweaving of the eight trigrams described there. Their images resemble everything; their meanings encompass everything. Applied to making implements, they yield the examples set out in the Appended Statements. Applied to divination, they can everywhere classify the dispositions of things and comprehend the principles of affairs. The Master recognized this. Accordingly, he based the images on Fuxi, followed Zhou in the names, and determined the meanings himself. It is as though he said: the earlier sage established images to express meaning fully, yet meaning is inexhaustible; the later sage attached statements to express words fully, yet words cannot exhaust it. It rests with students to apprehend its subtle workings and make it clear. Once this distinction is established, students know that there is a book of Fuxi; only then can they read the book of King Wen. This is the Master's intention in transmitting the Great Images.[^j11-images-method]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:015 -->

### First Nine: “Hidden dragon; do not act”: yang is below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:016 -->

**Original Meaning.** Yang refers to the nine; below explains its being hidden.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yang energy is below. The noble person occupies an obscure position and cannot yet be put to use.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:019 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** At the first lines of Qian and Kun, the Master brings forward the two terms yin and yang to illuminate the Changes' great meaning. Qian's first says “yang is below”; Kun's first says “yin first congeals.” The intention to support yang and restrain yin is already apparent in the words.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:020 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “The dragon appears in the field”: virtue's beneficence spreads everywhere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Appearing above the ground, its transforming virtue reaches other beings; its beneficence is already widespread.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:023 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** When yang energy appears in the fields, growing plants benefit the people. When the sage appears in the world, his transforming teaching gradually reaches things. Hence “virtue's beneficence spreads everywhere.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:024 -->

**Liang Yin:** Virtue's beneficence spreading everywhere is precisely what Mencius calls rectifying oneself so that other things become correct. Must beneficence mean generous gifts and relief for multitudes? Everyone stirred to action by hearing of his example receives his virtue's beneficence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:025 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Vigorous throughout the day”: repeatedly practicing the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:026 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Repeatedly” means treading and practicing it again and again.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Advance and retreat, movement and rest, must always accord with the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:029 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The third cultivates itself, hence “repeatedly”; the fourth tests itself, hence advance and retreat.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:030 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Perhaps a leap from the depths”: advancing brings no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:031 -->

**Original Meaning.** One may advance, but need not advance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Assess what is possible before advancing. Meet the proper time, and there is no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:034 -->

**Shi Jie:** “Advancing brings no blame” continues “perhaps a leap from the depths”; it does not settle the uncertainty. It means only that advancing in this way will be without blame.[^j11-qian-four]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:035 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Flying dragon in Heaven”: the great person rises to action.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:036 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Rises” has the sense of coming forth to act.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:037 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The great person's activity is the work of the sage.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:038 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:039 -->

**Xu Ji:** The great person's rising is the sage's coming forth. The dragon takes its place in Heaven through flight, just as the great person occupies his position by coming forth. “Great person” explains dragon; “rises” explains flying.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:040 -->

### Top Nine: “Overreaching dragon; there is regret”: fullness cannot last.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:041 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fullness brings change, and there is regret.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:042 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:042 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:043 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** Overreaching does not refer only to time and circumstances. Only when the person handling them overreaches along with them is it called fullness. “Cannot” is the sage's profound warning to one who occupies fullness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:043 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:044 -->

### Using Nines: Heaven's virtue must not take the lead.[^j11-qian-no-head]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:044 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:045 -->

**Original Meaning.** Yang firmness must not place itself before other things; hence all six yang change, and there is good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:045 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:046 -->

**The Original Meaning continues:** Earlier scholars call the passage beginning “Heaven moves” the Great Image, and those beginning “Hidden dragon” the Small Images. The same applies below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:046 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:047 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Using Nines is Heaven's virtue. Heaven's virtue is yang firmness; to employ further firmness and delight in leading would be excessive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:047 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:048 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:048 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:049 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** A year begins with spring and a month with the new moon: there seems to be a beginning. Yet spring is the outcome of the year's last month, and the new moon the culmination of the old moon's darkness. Whole and undivided, where can one finally know its ending? Follow it out: where is its beginning? Nothing can be established as first. One who uses Nines embodies Heaven's virtue in its entirety, cycling without end. This is how the sage rides Heaven's course.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:049 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4640:050 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** “Cannot be made first” resembles “cannot be made a fixed rule” in its phrasing: it is not an admonition. Suppose it meant that one must not lead for fear of using firmness excessively. “Heaven's virtue” names the utmost purity and refinement, to which nothing can be added. It is not like firmness and yieldingness or benevolence and rightness considered from one partial aspect. To fear that its exercise might be excessive, and therefore forbid its taking the lead, would no longer describe Heaven's virtue. Master Cheng once said: “Movement and stillness have no starting point; yin and yang have no beginning.” This is precisely the meaning of “cannot be made first.” Expressions such as “its first traces cannot be discerned” and “it cannot be compared with a particular thing” have the same intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4640:050 -->

<a id="eee-4641"></a>

## 2. Kun 坤

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4641 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:001 -->

### Great Image: Earth's configuration is receptive. Thus the noble person bears all things through the depth of his virtue.[^j11-earth-depth]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Earth is Kun's image, and it too is only one. Thus the text does not speak of doubling, but of the compliance of its configuration. This reveals its heights and depths following one another without end: utterly compliant and supremely deep, it leaves nothing unsupported.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The greatness of Kun's Way resembles Qian's. Who but a sage could embody it? Earth is deep, and its configuration slopes compliantly. Therefore its compliant depth supplies the image, and the text says that Earth's configuration is Kun. The noble person observes Kun's depth and, with profound virtue, accommodates and bears the multitude of things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:005 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Heights and depths following one another are simply compliance. Yet only because Earth is deep can they continue following one another, so that all one sees is its compliance. If a thin thing had heights and depths following one another, it would collapse and could not continue without limit. The noble person embodies this: only the utmost depth can bear things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:006 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** “Earth's configuration is Kun” means its configuration is compliant. In this its depth is apparent; hence the noble person's bearing things through deep virtue. Kun's image is Earth; doubled, it is again Kun. Thus the land's configuration has heights and depths following one another, rising and falling in succession. This compliance depends upon its depth. Without depth, successive heights and depths would collapse: how could there be such compliance? Only its depth enables it to sustain everything. Thus the noble person deepens his virtue to support the world's beings. How numerous those beings are! With his single person he bears responsibility for the world. The common people rely on him for security; birds, beasts, insects, grasses, and trees likewise rely on him for life. With a narrow heart and meager virtue, how could he accomplish this? The world's hopes in him would be disappointed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:007 -->

### First Six: “Treading frost—hard ice”: yin first congeals. Following its course through habituation, one comes to hard ice.[^j11-kun-frost]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:008 -->

**Original Meaning.** The Records of Wei reads “First Six: treading frost.” That reading should now be followed. “Habituation” means compliant repetition.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yin first congeals into frost; gradually increasing, it reaches hard ice. Though petty people begin obscurely, growth brings them gradually to strength. Hence the warning at the beginning. Habituation means repetition, through which strength is reached. Repetition here means drifting along without correction.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:011 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Habituation means becoming accustomed and compliant, as birds and beasts become tame. Following the Way of yielding yin, repeating it without cease, one arrives at hard ice. To warn in advance of hard ice when one treads on frost is to guard against gradual encroachment and consider the slight beginnings, attending to the ending at the outset.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:012 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Qian's first nine is explained in the Small Image by “yang is below”; Kun's first six by “yin first congeals.” Wishing to make clear that nine and six are yang and yin, the sage says this at Qian's and Kun's first strokes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:013 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The top six says, “Its course is exhausted.” This results simply from the first six's compliantly repeating that course until exhaustion. The two occurrences of “its course” record beginning and end. The Classic's “hard ice arrives” looks toward the ending; the Commentary's “comes to hard ice” traces it from the beginning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: The second six's movement is straight and thereby square. “Without practice, nothing is unfavorable”: Earth's Way shines forth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Receiving Heaven and moving, it is simply straight and square. Straight and square, it is great; their meaning has inexhaustible greatness. Earth's Way shines forth, and its work is compliantly accomplished. Why would benefit depend upon prior practice?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:017 -->

**Wang Anshi:** “The second six's movement” means that its virtue becomes visible only through movement. Bringing things to life according to their natures is its straightness. Completing their forms without altering them is its squareness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:018 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Kun's second six occupies a correct position with compliant virtue. Of the six lines, none fulfills Earth's Way as completely as the second. Hence “Earth's Way shines forth.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:019 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Qian is governed by the fifth nine, Kun by the second six. Within these two figures, only these lines are both centered and correct. Moreover, the fifth is a heavenly line and the second an earthly line, precisely matching Qian's and Kun's original positions. Qian is governed by the fifth nine, so the fifth expresses its great activity, while the second nine speaks only of its virtue's excellence. Kun is governed by the second six, so the second expresses its great activity, while the fifth six speaks only of its virtue's excellence. The second six's straightness is being utterly yielding yet firm in movement; its squareness is being utterly still yet square in virtue. Its greatness is “following, it finds a master and has constancy; containing the myriad things, its transformation shines.” Its “without practice, nothing is unfavorable” is “how compliant is Kun's Way, receiving Heaven and acting with the time!” The second six thus possesses Kun's virtue in full. Confucius feared that people would not understand how it could also possess Qian's straightness, so he explained: “The second six's movement is straight and thereby square.” Kun is firm in movement and can therefore be straight. He also feared that people would not understand how nothing could be unfavorable, so he explained that Earth's Way shines forth. Earth's Way is governed by the second six, just as Qian's fifth nine is said to occupy the position of Heaven's virtue.[^j11-kun-master]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:020 -->

**Cai Qing:** Earth's Way is straightness and squareness. Its shining forth is straightness and squareness becoming great. In being straight, square, and great, there is already nothing unfavorable, without practice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:021 -->

**Ye Erzhan:** In “straight and thereby square,” attend to “thereby.” The second six's movement is square, but it is because what remains within is straight that what appears outside is square.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:022 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Keeping brilliance within, one may remain steadfast”: bringing it forth at the proper time. “Perhaps following in the king's service”: one's understanding is radiant and great.[^j11-contained-brilliance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Master feared that people would cling to the wording without grasping its meaning, so he explained further. A minister's Way below is not to claim accomplishment and goodness as his own. He must contain and conceal his excellence; only then is his conduct correct and enduring. Yet what rightness requires is brought forth at the proper time: he merely does not claim the achievement. Not missing what is fitting is acting with the time. It does not mean concealing things and never acting; containing one's ability without acting would fail to fulfill loyalty. “Perhaps following in the king's service”: the Image quotes only the first clause, but its explanation includes the following words, as elsewhere. To serve the king without claiming achievement yet bring matters to completion is to possess radiant, great understanding. Precisely because understanding is radiant and great, one can contain and conceal one's excellence. A shallow, benighted person fears only that others will not know of any goodness he possesses. How could he contain brilliance?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:025 -->

**Lü Zuqian:** The Commentary says, “Precisely because understanding is radiant and great, one can contain and conceal.” This is deeply meaningful. Ordinary people wishing to conceal their excellence often merely try to root out pride and hide their names and traces. Yet the more they weed, the more pride grows; the more they hide, the more it shows. They have never attended to themselves at the root. Their understanding is not yet radiant and great, and their hearts are shallow and narrow. With the slightest achievement or goodness, they have nowhere to put it. Although they force themselves to suppress it, they ultimately cannot. It is like too much water in a small vessel: however tightly one closes it, it must overflow. If the vessel is large, it naturally does not overflow, and no such wasted effort is needed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:026 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Containing does not mean hiding forever without bringing forth; it means waiting for the time. Serving the king without claiming accomplishment yet completing the task must depend upon radiant, great understanding. The shallow and benighted fear that others will not know of their goodness. How could they contain and conceal it?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:027 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Tie the sack; no blame”: caution keeps one unharmed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With caution such as this, there is no harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:029 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Yellow lower garment; supreme good fortune”: the pattern is within.[^j11-yellow-garment]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** The pattern is within and appears outwardly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The pattern of the yellow center remains within and does not exceed. The utmost beauty is accumulated inwardly while one occupies the lower place; hence supreme good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:033 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** The yellow lower garment is centered virtue manifesting as cultivated government. The Image again traces it to what is within: could pattern be something put on from outside? Patterned virtue truly resides within. The center possessed inwardly is called the yellow center; manifested outwardly, the yellow lower garment. “The pattern is within” is brilliance beneath an unobtrusive appearance, pattern without display: precisely the meaning of beauty residing within.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:034 -->

### Top Six: “Dragons battle in the open country”: its course is exhausted.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When yin's strength reaches its utmost limit, conflict and injury must follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:037 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Qian says, “The overreaching dragon has regret: the calamity of exhaustion.” Kun says, “Dragons battle in the open country: its course is exhausted.” Qian reaches the top and, exhausted, meets calamity; Kun reaches the top and, exhausted, fights. Fighting goes beyond regret.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:038 -->

### Using Sixes: “Enduring steadfastness”: concluding with greatness.[^j11-kun-great-ending]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:039 -->

**Original Meaning.** First yin, afterward yang: hence concluding with greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:040 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** If yin lacks steadfast firmness, it cannot endure to completion. Thus the benefit in Using Sixes lies in becoming great at the end. The ability to end greatly is enduring steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:041 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:042 -->

**Xun Shuang:** Yang seeks to be without a head; yin concludes with greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:042 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:043 -->

**Cheng Jiong:** Qian takes origination as its root, supplying beginnings; Kun takes steadfastness as its governing quality, concluding greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:043 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:044 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Yang is great, yin small. All yin changing into yang is what is called concluding with greatness: beginning small and ending great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:044 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:045 -->

**Yu Yan:** Kun's body is originally small. Changed into Qian, its activity becomes great; hence concluding with greatness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:045 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:046 -->

**Lu Zhenqi:** Although Qian and Kun share origination, success, benefit, and steadfastness, Qian emphasizes origination and takes it as the governing unity; Kun emphasizes steadfastness and finds peace in it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:046 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4641:047 -->

**Cheng Jingcheng:** Yang at its utmost does not become first: it has no beginning. Yin at its utmost concludes with greatness: it has no ending. Endings and beginnings cycle, transformations have no starting point. Such indeed is the subtle working of creation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4641:047 -->

<a id="eee-4642"></a>

## 3. Zhun 屯

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4642 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:001 -->

### Great Image: Cloud and thunder: Zhun. Thus the noble person orders and weaves affairs together.[^j11-zhun-weaving]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Kan is called cloud rather than water to suggest that passage is not yet open. Ordering and weaving are the work of arranging silk: drawing out its threads and putting them in order. An age of initial difficulty is a time for the noble person to act.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Kan is called cloud rather than rain because cloud is rain not yet formed. Its inability to become rain is why there is initial difficulty. The noble person observes this image and orders and weaves together the world's affairs to overcome its difficulties. Setting the warp and weft, arranging and joining the threads, means undertaking the work.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:005 -->

**Li Shunchen:** Kan above Zhen makes Zhun: clouds are rising, accumulated but not yet dispersed. Kan below Zhen makes Release: rain's moisture has poured forth and reaches everywhere. Thunder and rain arising disperse initial difficulty; cloud and thunder just beginning are its onset.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:006 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Ordering establishes the framework; weaving joins things together and completes them. This too contains an image of difficulty. Ordering images thunder's stirring; weaving images the clouds' gathering.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:007 -->

**Feng Yi:** Cloud and thunder are just arising, without rain: an image of blockage and entanglement. The noble person observes it to remedy the age's difficulties, like someone arranging silk, drawing out and ordering the threads to undo their knots and establish a coherent pattern.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:008 -->

**Wu Cheng:** The noble person governs the world as one arranges silk, seeking to untangle confusion. In Zhun's time, he must seek to release its knotted obstructions.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:009 -->

### First Nine: Though lingering, the purpose is to put correctness into practice. The honored lowers himself before the lowly and greatly wins the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A worthy person is below. If the time is not favorable, he lingers, unable to go forth and relieve its difficulties. Yet he possesses both the purpose and the means to relieve them: his purpose is to put correctness into practice. In a time of initial difficulty, the nine is yang that comes to dwell beneath yin, imaging the honored lowering himself before the lowly. Yielding yin cannot preserve itself in such a time; one person of yang-firm ability becomes the focus of everyone's allegiance. If he can further place himself humbly below, he greatly wins the people. Someone may ask: “While struggling below, how is he honored?” To place firm, clear ability beneath yieldingness, and the capacity to relieve difficulty beneath those unable to do so, is precisely the honored lowering himself before the lowly. Moreover, is not yang inherently honored in relation to yin?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:012 -->

**Wang Bi:** It cannot advance, and therefore lingers. This is not restful ease that abandons the completion of affairs. Hence “though lingering, the purpose is to put correctness into practice.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:013 -->

**Yang Wanli:** Does lingering without advancing really mean doing nothing? It remains correct and waits, yet its purpose never ceases to seek the practice of correctness. Thus the Duke of Zhou speaks of abiding in steadfastness, and Confucius of putting correctness into practice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:014 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The first lingers and waits because its ultimate purpose is to practice correctness. In a time of initial difficulty, yielding people cannot preserve themselves. One person of yang-firm ability must become the leader whom the multitude follows. Moreover, the first can lower the honored before the lowly, greatly winning the people's hearts. If those above truly establish him as a lord, the difficulty can be relieved. Hence benefit.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:015 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Qian's and Kun's first lines bring forward yin and yang. Here yang is honored and yin lowly; yang is ruler and yin the people. The distinctions of yin and yang become still more exact.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:016 -->

### Six in the Second Place: The second six's difficulty comes from riding upon firmness. “After ten years, she pledges herself”: returning to the ordinary course.[^j11-zhun-ten]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second six meets initial difficulty and also rides upon firmness, pressed by yang strength: this is its affliction. By ten years, difficulty long endured must find passage. It can then return to the ordinary course and unite with its proper correspondent. Ten is the completion of numbers.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:018 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Pursuing deer without a forester”: because one pursues the quarry. The noble person lets it go; going on brings humiliation and exhaustion.[^j11-zhun-deer]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Recklessly acting when an affair is impossible follows desire. Pursuing deer without a forester comes from greed for the quarry. Acting in initial difficulty when action is inadmissible resembles this: the heart is set on pursuit. The noble person sees the first signs, lets go, and does not follow. Going on would bring humiliation and exhaustion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:020 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:021 -->

**Yang Jian:** One pursuing deer without a forester has his heart fixed on the quarry and is obscured by it. Even without a guide, he ventures heedlessly forward, failing to recognize that he should not. Someone moved by profit and salary, heedlessly seeking them without following the Way, is like this. The noble person lets go; proceeding brings humiliation and exhaustion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:022 -->

**Cai Qing:** “Pursues” carries the emphasis: the heart covets the quarry. Hence the explanatory “because.” This is what is called losing oneself in hunting: surrendering one's person to external things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:023 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Image Commentary sometimes makes a complete explanation from a single word, as here with “exhaustion” and in the next line with “clarity.” This is the first instance of that convention. The same applies in the other hexagrams.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:024 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Seeking, then going: clarity.[^j11-zhun-seeking]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Knowing one's insufficiency, seeking a worthy person to assist oneself, and only then going forth may be called clarity. To occupy a position from which assistance can be obtained, yet simply stop because one cannot manage alone, is the utmost darkness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:027 -->

**Hu Yuan:** One must wait until others seek one, and only then go to respond. Could anyone do this without the noble person's cultivated nature and clear understanding?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:028 -->

**Yu Yan:** They seek me, and I go: then my going may be called clear-sighted. To go without waiting for an invitation would show ignorance of the rightness of approaching and withdrawing. Could that be called clarity?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:029 -->

**Jiang Tisheng:** This refers to following the fifth nine. Generally, retreating downward is coming, and advancing upward is going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:030 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Cheng's Commentary and the Original Meaning both understand oneself to seek another. Hu, Yu, and Jiang all take others to seek oneself, after which one goes to follow them. This also suits the phrasing “seeking, then going.” Moreover, in the Changes, both the fourth six's responding to the first nine and its following the fifth nine can have auspicious meanings. Thus following either the first or the fifth is intelligible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:031 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Its rich moisture is held back”: its beneficence does not yet shine forth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Rich moisture fails to reach below, so virtue's beneficence cannot yet spread with radiance and greatness. This is the ruler's difficulty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:034 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** “Beneficence” should be understood as nourishing moisture. Moisture held back rather than bestowed is precisely its failure to shine forth. It does not mean that it has already been bestowed but merely fails to shine brightly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:035 -->

### Top Six: “Tears of blood stream down”: how could this continue?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Initial difficulty reaches exhaustion, and one does not know what to do; thus one weeps blood. With such distress, how could it continue long? A hexagram represents an affair; a line represents a time within that affair. Dividing into three and doubling them suffices to encompass the many principles. Extend them, develop them through related kinds, and all the world's possible undertakings are covered.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:038 -->

**Yang Jian:** “How could this continue?” asks how one could go on being like this. It not only expresses profound compassion but also hopes for change. With change, passage may become possible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4642:039 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Wherever the Image Commentary asks “how could this continue?”, it means that one should reverse course promptly rather than delay, as Yang explains.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4642:039 -->

<a id="eee-4643"></a>

## 4. Meng 蒙

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4643 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:001 -->

### Great Image: A spring issues beneath the mountain: Meng. Thus the noble person makes his conduct resolute and nurtures his virtue.[^j11-meng-spring]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** A spring is water just emerging. It must flow, yet does so by gradual stages.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A spring emerges beneath the mountain and encounters danger, not yet knowing where to go: the image of unformed understanding, like a young person who does not yet know where to turn. The noble person observes it to make his conduct resolute and nurture his virtue. Seeing water emerge without yet being able to flow freely, he makes his action decisive. Seeing its first emergence without a settled direction, he nurtures his luminous virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:005 -->

**Master Zhou:** “The unknowing child seeks me”: I set him right and make his conduct resolute, as through divination. Divination is an appeal to the spirit. A second and third inquiry become irreverent; to irreverence, no answer is given. “A spring issues beneath the mountain”: still and clear. Disturb it, and it becomes confused; confused, it cannot decide. Take care! Does it not depend upon attaining the mean appropriate to the time?[^j11-meng-zhou]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:006 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** The text says not water beneath the mountain but a spring issuing beneath it. A spring is water's source: what is called pure, single, and unmixed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:007 -->

**Zhen Dexiu:** When a spring first emerges, its slight trickle is blocked by sand and stones. How could it immediately get through? Only because it is resolute in flowing, avoiding no danger, can it eventually become a river. Yet if its source were not deep, resolute flow would readily exhaust it. Gen's image is mountain, its virtue stopping. Precisely because the mountain remains still, the spring's emergence is inexhaustible. Stopping precedes flowing. The noble person observes Meng, making his conduct resolute like water's necessary flow and nurturing his virtue like water's possession of a source.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:008 -->

**Xu Ji:** Unformed understanding does not yet know where to go. One must embody Kan's firm center to make conduct decisive and carry it through. Unformed understanding has not yet suffered injury. One must embody Gen's stillness to nurture virtue and bring it to completion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:009 -->

**Cai Qing:** Resolute conduct and nurtured virtue are the Way in which inner and outer, movement and stillness, nourish one another. The nurturing of unformed understanding does not go beyond this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:010 -->

### First Six: “Punishment is beneficial”: to establish the standard correctly.[^j11-meng-punishment]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:011 -->

**Original Meaning.** At the beginning of opening unformed understanding, the standard must be correct. Admonition and discipline establish it correctly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At the beginning of treating unformed understanding, establish safeguards and limits, make offenses and their penalties clear, and set the standard correctly. Let people follow it and gradually reach transformation. Someone may doubt whether immediately employing punishment at the beginning amounts to execution without teaching. Such a person does not know that establishing laws and regulating punishments are themselves means of teaching. Later discussions of punishment have ceased to recognize the transforming teaching within it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:014 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Punishing a small fault frees someone from a greater one: this is the sage's original intention in using punishment. It establishes the standard, rather than seeking to inflict punishment. Even at the utmost, when an army is used to strike, the action remains defense against raiders rather than raiding. The sage's treatment of unformed understanding is always full of compassion. Is this not why the second nine, embracing the unformed, governs the whole figure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “The son can manage the household”: firmness and yieldingness meet.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:016 -->

**Original Meaning.** This points to the correspondence between the second and fifth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A son able to govern the household enjoys his father's undivided trust. The second's ability to direct the work of enlightening the unformed likewise depends upon the fifth's undivided trust. The dispositions of firmness and yieldingness at the second and fifth meet. Thus the second can practice its Way of firm centeredness and accomplish enlightenment. Without such meeting between above and below, how could even a firm, centered second preside over the affair?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:018 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Do not marry this woman”: her conduct is not compliant.[^j11-meng-shun-shen]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Compliant” should read “careful.” The two graphs were interchangeable in antiquity: Xunzi's “Shun and Mo” is written “Shen and Mo.” Moreover, “conduct is not careful” fits the Classic's intention more closely.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Such a woman's conduct is perverse and uncompliant; she must not be taken in marriage.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:022 -->

**Xiong Liangfu:** The three occurrences of “compliant” in Meng's Small Images all mean the same thing. There is no need to change “not compliant” into “not careful.” The third six's conduct is uncompliant; hence the instruction not to marry her.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:023 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The humiliation of being trapped in unformed understanding: alone, it is far from substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:024 -->

**Original Meaning.** To rhyme, “substance” is read in the departing tone.[^j11-meng-substance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In the time of unformed understanding, yang firmness brings enlightenment. The fourth is yielding yin and farthest from firmness: an ignorant person who does not approach the worthy and has no way to become clear. Its shameful entrapment comes from being alone in its distance from worthy, clear-sighted people. Failing to draw near to the worthy and thereby becoming trapped is the utmost humiliation. Substance means yang firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:027 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Yang governs birth and growth and is therefore called substantial. Yin governs decline and reduction and therefore cannot be called substantial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:028 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The first and third are near the second nine; the fifth is near the top nine. Both the third and fifth also correspond with yang. Only the fourth six has yin both as neighbors and as correspondent. Hence “alone, far from substance.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:029 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Yang is solid, yin open. Alone far from substance means that, of the whole hexagram, it alone cannot approach a worthy person of yang substance. Hence it is trapped in unformed understanding without a way through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:030 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The unknowing child's good fortune: compliance joined to deference.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Setting oneself aside to follow another is compliance. Lowering one's purpose to seek instruction is humble deference. One who can do this has ample capacity for the world's affairs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:033 -->

**Hu Yigui:** Compliance concerns the yielding line; deference concerns its purpose to respond.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:034 -->

### Top Nine: “It is beneficial to ward off raiders”: above and below are compliant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:035 -->

**Original Meaning.** Firmness wards off raiders, and above and below both attain their proper Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4643:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In beneficially warding off raiders, above and below each attain compliance. Those above do not become excessively violent; those below have their ignorance struck away. This is the meaning of warding off raiders.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4643:036 -->

<a id="eee-4644"></a>

## 5. Xu 需

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4644 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:001 -->

### Great Image: Clouds rise into Heaven: Xu. Thus the noble person eats, drinks, and rests in gladness.[^j11-xu-waiting]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Once clouds have risen into Heaven, nothing more is to be done. They await the harmony of yin and yang and then rain of themselves. Affairs that require waiting likewise admit no further intervention. One simply eats, drinks, and rests in gladness, awaiting their arrival of themselves. The slightest intervention would no longer be waiting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Cloud energy steams upward into Heaven and must await the harmonious meeting of yin and yang before becoming rain. The clouds are only rising, not yet raining; hence the meaning of waiting. Yin and yang interact but have not yet produced nourishing rain, like a noble person who has accumulated ability and virtue without yet putting them to use. He observes clouds rising into Heaven and waiting to become rain, cherishes his Way and virtue, and calmly awaits the time. Food and drink nourish his energy and body; restful gladness harmonizes his heart and purpose. This is what is called dwelling in ease while awaiting the mandate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:005 -->

**Kong Yingda:** The text says not “clouds are above Heaven” but “clouds rise into Heaven.” The former would not reveal the prospect of rain. Clouds rising mean that Heaven is about to rain, awaiting the time to let it fall. This illuminates waiting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:006 -->

**Hu Yuan:** Food and drink nourish the body; restful gladness settles the spirit. This is simply delighting in Heaven, understanding the mandate, and dwelling in ease while awaiting the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:007 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Xu means waiting. Eating, drinking, and resting in gladness mean that there is nothing further to do: simply wait. What one awaits must have its time of arrival. Learning the Way is also like this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:008 -->

**Wu Cheng:** Rest means that the body is at ease, with no other undertaking to manage; gladness means that the heart is content, with no other plans or worries. Food and drink accord with one's present station; restful gladness does not hanker after what lies outside it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:009 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** Only after clouds have risen into Heaven may one await rain. Only after the noble person has acted may he await good order. Otherwise, would this not approach idle abandonment?[^j11-xu-reading]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:010 -->

### First Nine: “Waiting in the outskirts”: not venturing into difficulty. “Constancy is beneficial; no blame”: the constant course has not been lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Dwelling in an open, distant place means not venturing into danger and difficulty. Yang is firm and vigorous, advancing upward. The first can wait in a distant place without advancing into danger. It should also remain at ease without losing its constant course; then it can be without blame. One who does not advance but whose purpose remains agitated cannot rest in constancy. When the noble person waits for the time, he quietly preserves himself. Although his purpose awaits something, he remains serene as though he would spend his whole life there. Only then can he employ constancy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:013 -->

**Sun Zhiqing:** Not proceeding into difficulty is the constant course; not losing it is constant virtue. People lack a constant governor within and are driven by their abilities, moved by their impulses, or provoked by circumstances, venturing into difficulty without a backward glance. Thus not losing constancy is most difficult. Eating, drinking, and resting in gladness preserve constancy. With constancy preserved, what further affair is there?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:014 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Waiting on the sand”: spaciousness within the center. Though there is a little talk against one, the ending is auspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:015 -->

**Original Meaning.** Spaciousness means breadth. Dwelling broadly at the center, one does not rush forward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Spaciousness means ample ease. Though the second is near danger, it occupies the center with breadth and ease. Thus, though a little talk reaches it, good fortune comes in the end. It handles the situation well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:018 -->

**Yang Jian:** Spaciousness within the center means an open, level heart. Neither advancement nor petty talk stirs it. Such a person has good fortune in the end because the second nine has attained the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:019 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Waiting in the mud”: calamity lies outside. I bring the raiders upon myself; reverence and caution avert defeat.[^j11-xu-added-prognosis]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:020 -->

**Original Meaning.** Outside means the outer trigram. “Reverence and caution avert defeat” discloses a prognostication beyond the prognostication. How earnest is the sage's intention to guide people!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third closely presses upon the upper trigram's danger and difficulty; hence calamity lies outside. Calamity is a general name for trouble, though distinguished from fault when the two are contrasted. The third brings raiders because it advances and presses upon them; hence “I myself.” Though the raiders are self-incurred, reverence and caution, assessing what is fitting before advancing, can avert loss and defeat. In Xu's time, one waits before advancing. Its meaning is acting with discernment of the time, not a prohibition against ever advancing. It simply requires reverence and caution so that one does not miss what is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:023 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Asked about reverence and caution, he said: “Reverence is broad; caution attends to particulars. A person walking straight along the road exemplifies reverence; guarding against a stumble at a dangerous place ahead exemplifies caution. Caution fears the possibility of error. This resembles thought and consideration: thought pursues a line of thinking; consideration fears that things may not turn out that way.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:024 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Though the raiders are outside, they do not arrive of themselves. They come because I give them cause. If I am reverent, cautious, and without fault, even proximity to them does not entail defeat.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:025 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Kan's danger lies outside and has never pressed upon anyone. People eager to advance press themselves toward danger and thereby bring disaster. The Image's “I myself” makes clear that the cause of calamity does not lie with someone else.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:026 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Waiting amid blood”: compliantly listening.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yielding yin occupies danger and difficulty and cannot hold its place; thus it retreats out of its pit. Yieldingness cannot contend with the time. Unable to remain, it withdraws. This is compliant obedience to the time, which prevents misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:029 -->

**Yang Jian:** The fourth six enters danger and suffers injury, yet neither good fortune nor misfortune is stated. Why? Because it can wait, withdraw, and heed what is required. The Way of the Changes reaches everywhere. Even when the fourth enters danger and is injured, there remains a Way of handling it. Both the six and the fourth position are yielding; hence the image of compliant listening.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:030 -->

**Wu Cheng:** This means the fourth six's yielding compliance in heeding the fifth nine.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:031 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** If the third can be reverent, it avoids defeat even when pressing upon Kan's danger. If the fourth can be compliant, it can emerge even after falling into that danger. Reverence and compliance are indeed Ways of handling danger.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:032 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Wine and food; steadfast correctness brings good fortune”: through centeredness and correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Waiting with wine and food is steadfast and auspicious because the fifth attains centered correctness and fulfills its Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:035 -->

**Liang Yin:** “Through centeredness and correctness” shows that its feasting is not indulgence in pleasure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:036 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Many desires within create the danger of seeking good government too hastily. Only centered and correct virtue makes waiting accord with steadfastness and attain good fortune. Centered correctness means sincerity and steadfastness: this traces the source of the ability to wait.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:037 -->

### Top Six: Unbidden guests arrive; respecting them brings good fortune in the end. Though not appropriately placed, there is no great error.[^j11-xu-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:038 -->

**Original Meaning.** Yin at the top is correctly placed. The meaning of “not appropriately placed” remains unclear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:039 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Not appropriately placed means yin occupying the highest place. The line treats a six in a yin position as a place of ease. The Image further exhausts its meaning, explaining that yin should be below, so occupying the top is inappropriate. Yet reverence and caution prevent yang from overbearing it, and good fortune follows in the end. Though inappropriately placed, it does not reach a great error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:040 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:041 -->

**Lü Zuqian:** The good fortune of Xu's first nine and fifth nine is self-evident. As for its other four lines, the second has a little talk yet good fortune in the end; the third's Image says reverence and caution avert defeat; the fourth's says compliantly listening; and the top says that three unbidden guests arrive and respecting them brings good fortune in the end. Broadly, in the world's affairs, patient accommodation and waiting ultimately leave fewer mistakes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4644:042 -->

**Cai Qing:** Though not appropriately placed means yin occupying danger's utmost limit. It is precisely like Oppression's top six: “Entangled in vines: not yet fitting.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4644:042 -->

<a id="eee-4645"></a>

## 6. Song 訟

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4645 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven and water move in contrary directions: Song. Thus the noble person considers the beginning before undertaking an affair.[^j11-song-beginnings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Heaven above and water below move contrary to one another. Consider the beginning when undertaking affairs, and the origins of contention are cut off.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven above and water below proceed in contrary directions. The two bodies' opposition gives rise to contention. If above and below accorded, how could contention arise? Observing the image, the noble person understands the tendency of human feeling toward dispute. Thus, in whatever he undertakes, he must consider its beginning. Remove the beginnings of contention at the outset, and it has no way to arise. The meaning of considering beginnings is broad: caution in forming relationships and clarity in contracts are examples.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:005 -->

**Wu Cheng:** Water moves downward and Heaven upward. Their movements turn away from one another: this is contrary movement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:006 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Every affair has a beginning, middle, and end. Contention has good fortune in the middle and misfortune at the end. Yet if one considers its beginning and removes the source of dispute, there is no need to speak of middle or end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:007 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Contention does not begin on the day of the dispute, but when the affair is first undertaken. Failure to consider it beforehand gives dispute its origin. Thus the noble person considers the outset: whether the principles of the affair contain obstacles, whether human feelings will be offended, and whether eventual harm may follow. He rules out and does not undertake whatever is unsound and could cause contention. Its beginnings then have nowhere to arise.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:008 -->

### First Six: “Do not prolong the affair”: contention must not be prolonged. Though there is a little argument, the case becomes clear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A yielding, weak six contends from below. Such a situation must not continue long. Prolong it, and defeat and disaster follow. Moreover, at contention's very beginning, the warning is already given that contention is not to be prolonged. Yielding weakness below lacks the ability to contend. Though it does not prolong the affair, contention has begun and some slight trouble must follow; hence a little argument. Yet it does not prolong the affair and has a proper yang-firm correspondent above who clearly distinguishes the case. Thus good fortune comes in the end. Otherwise, could it escape? In Contention, lines in corresponding places may respond and join, so the first obtains a clear defense through the fourth. They may also be at odds and dispute, so the second and fifth become opponents.[^j11-song-correspondence]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:011 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Stopping contention at its beginning is best. Thus its first line already warns that contention must not be prolonged.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:012 -->

**Yu Yan:** The Judgment Commentary says contention must not be carried through: it speaks of contention generally and wishes it not to be completed. The line commentary says it must not be prolonged: the first line is its beginning, and the wish is that it should not grow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:013 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Unable to prevail in contention”: returning to flee and hide. Contending from below against above, one gathers trouble upon oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:014 -->

**Original Meaning.** Gathering means bringing it upon oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its claim cannot stand against the other, and so it cannot prevail. It returns to flee and hide, leaving its place. Contending from below against above goes against rightness and occupies a disadvantaged position. Trouble comes as readily as gathering something up for oneself: this expresses how easily it is obtained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:017 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The first two clauses restate the line; the latter two are the Image Commentary proper. This resembles the phrasing of Xu's top six.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:018 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Recognizing that one's claim cannot prevail, returning, and hiding may still avert disaster. Without turning back upon oneself, trouble arrives as though gathered by one's own hand. The Judgment calls the second “firmness coming and attaining the center,” yet its line has this meaning. The Judgment speaks generally of the figure's structure; the line addresses its application in that particular time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:019 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Living on the benefits of former virtue”: following above brings good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:020 -->

**Original Meaning.** Following above brings good fortune means that following another is auspicious, making clear that taking charge oneself would not bring accomplishment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Preserve one's allotted place. Though following what those above undertake, the initiative is not one's own. Thus one claims no accomplishment and ultimately attains good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:023 -->

**Qiao Zhonghe:** The third lives on former virtue. At the conclusion, all these plans and counsels are credited to our ruler's virtue. Serving the king without claiming accomplishment, what use has one for contention? Hence good fortune in following above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:024 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Return and submit to the mandate; change and rest in steadfast correctness”: there is no error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Able to act thus, one is without error. This is why there is good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:027 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** The second is checked by circumstances; the fourth yields to principle. Hence the second's merit extends only to freedom from calamity, while the fourth's steadfastness is called freedom from error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:028 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Contention; supreme good fortune”: through centeredness and correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:029 -->

**Original Meaning.** Centeredness hears without partiality; correctness judges according to principle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Applied anywhere, how could the Way of centered correctness fail to bring supreme good fortune?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:032 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Centered correctness empties the heart to hear fully from those below, without partiality, and examines the affair's circumstances to judge according to principle. Such is the great person.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:033 -->

### Top Nine: Receiving robes through contention is still unworthy of respect.[^j11-song-honor]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Carry contention to its utmost: even supposing one receives the favor of investiture and robes, this remains unworthy of respect and deserving of contempt. How much more so when disaster follows!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4645:036 -->

**Cai Qing:** “Still unworthy of respect” considers only the acquisition of robes through contention. How much more so when they must ultimately be stripped away! It resembles Increase's top nine: “‘None increases him’ is only a partial statement.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4645:036 -->

<a id="eee-4646"></a>

## 7. Shi 師 — The Army

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4646 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:001 -->

### Great Image: Water within the earth: The Army. Thus the noble person shelters the people and nurtures the multitude.[^j11-army-people]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Water does not stand apart from earth, nor soldiers from the people. Thus one who can nourish the people can obtain the multitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Water within the earth gathers there, imaging the gathering of a multitude: hence The Army. The noble person observes this image to shelter and preserve his people, nurturing and gathering his multitude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:005 -->

**Chen Chen:** Water within the earth is like soldiers within the people. Is this not the image of The Army? Observing it, the noble person must shelter his people and nurture his soldiers. Fields are apportioned among the people, and troops are supplied through their obligations.
