Thus, in peaceful times, he must regulate fields and settlements and teach planting and animal husbandry, so that the people in every neighborhood, ward, clan, district, and township receive sustenance. Once the people are nourished, the men of the military units—from squads and companies to regiments and armies—are all maintained here, ready for future defense and resistance to aggression. If their instruction in ordinary times lacks a proper method, who will serve when urgency comes?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:006 -->

### First Six: “The army goes forth under discipline”: losing discipline brings misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The army should go forth under discipline. Lose discipline, and there is misfortune. Even a fortunate victory would remain a course toward misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:009 -->

**Cai Qing:** Instead of “without sound order, misfortune,” it says “losing discipline brings misfortune,” making clear that “without sound order” means losing discipline.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:010 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “At the army's center, good fortune”: receiving Heaven's favor. “The king thrice bestows his commission”: cherishing the myriad states.[^j11-army-heaven]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Good fortune at the army's center comes from receiving Heaven's favor and trust. Heaven here means the king. Unless a minister receives the ruler's favor and commission, how could he hold independent authority over a campaign and enjoy the good fortune of success? The Image develops this meaning because the second alone presides over the undertaking, differing from the view of ordinary scholars discussed earlier. The king's thrice-bestowed gracious commission commends its achievement and thereby cherishes the myriad states.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:013 -->

**Gan Bao:** Bestowing a commission is not private favoritism; it simply brings peace to the myriad states.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:014 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** A king uses troops only when compelled by necessity. How could delight in killing be his original intention? The threefold commission aims solely to cherish and pacify the myriad states.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:015 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** It says cherishing, not intimidating: this reveals the king's original intention in employing an army.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:016 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “The army may carry corpses”: utterly without achievement.[^j11-army-corpses]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Entrust authority to two or three different hands: how could there be success? This not only fails to achieve anything but brings misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:019 -->

**Yang Jian:** In directing an army, authority belongs to one commander. Let many preside, and one follows a course toward misfortune. Without unity among the multitude, success is impossible. The second nine already acts as commander, while the third six stands above it: an image of authority failing to remain united.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:020 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “The army camps to the left; no blame”: the constant course has not been lost.[^j11-army-left]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** Recognizing difficulty and retreating is the army's constant course.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The constant course of directing an army is to act fittingly according to the time. Thus camping to the left is not an error. The fourth's withdrawal to camp is precisely what is fitting, and therefore without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:024 -->

**Yang Shi:** An army ordinarily gives precedence to the right; camping to the left would depart from that norm. Yet the fourth is yielding and compliant, assessing the enemy before advancing and considering victory before engagement. Its withdrawal to camp on the left has not lost the constant course.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:025 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “The elder son leads the army”: proceeding through the mean. “Younger sons carry corpses”: the appointment is inappropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The elder son means the second. Its centered, correct virtue accords with those above, and it receives the commission to act. If others are also appointed, allowing many to preside over the affair, the appointments are inappropriate. Misfortune is to be expected.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:028 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Proceeding through the mean refers to the second nine's central position. The inappropriate appointment refers to the third six's improper position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:029 -->

### Top Six: “The great ruler gives his commands”: to assess achievement correctly. “Do not employ petty people”: they would surely disorder the state.[^j11-army-rewards]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** Profound indeed is the sage's warning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The great ruler holds authority over favors and rewards, correctly assessing the army's achievements: this is the conclusion of military action. Though achievements are rewarded, petty people must not be appointed merely because they have rendered service. Employ them, and they will surely disorder the state. Antiquity has known petty people who relied upon their achievements to bring disorder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:033 -->

**Yang Jian:** At the army's conclusion, achievement is complete. The great ruler gives commands to reward it. Assessing achievement correctly means that rewards must match service without error. At the founding of states and establishment of estates, petty people should never have been employed from the outset. That the warning appears only here traces the beginning through the occasion of rewarding achievement. Employ petty people as commanders: should they luckily succeed, withholding rewards becomes difficult. Grant them states and estates, and harm reaches the people; they must disorder the state. Removing one who harms the people only to employ another who harms them exchanges one disorder for another. This must never be done.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:034 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The king's threefold commission is given at the beginning of military action; the great ruler's commands come at its end. Cherishing a state and disordering it distinguish great persons from petty people. This indeed expresses the sage's deep concern and far-reaching warning.[^j11-army-elder-variant]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4646:035 -->

**Shao Bao:** Younger sons carrying corpses is a warning at the army's beginning; not employing petty people is a warning at its end. Without younger sons at the beginning, there will be no petty people at the end. Even should such people be present, one may reward them without enfeoffing them, or enfeoff them without assigning them responsibilities. Not entrusting them with office is also not employing them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4646:035 -->

<a id="eee-4647"></a>

## 8. Bi 比 — Holding Together

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4647 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:001 -->

### Great Image: Water upon the earth: Holding Together. Thus the former kings established the myriad states and drew their lords close.[^j11-bi-states]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Water upon the earth adheres to it, admitting no gap. Establishing states and drawing their lords close likewise enabled the former kings to join with the world without separation. The Judgment means others coming to join me; here the meaning is my going to join others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Nothing joins things so closely, without a gap, as water upon the earth: hence Holding Together. The former kings observed this image to establish the myriad states and draw their lords close. Establishing states joined them with the people; affectionately attending to the lords joined them with the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:005 -->

**Zhang Jun:** Water travels upon the earth, small and great streams joining and together returning east. The former kings modeled themselves upon this. Establishing the myriad states joined the lords downward with their people; drawing the lords close joined them upward with their ruler. Like the body directing the arm and the arm the fingers, small and great sustain one another and compliantly heed commands. The institutions have attained their proper Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:006 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Yichuan says that establishing the myriad states joins one with the people. One cannot personally join with every person, so lords are established to draw close to the people. Those whom the Son of Heaven himself draws close are simply the lords. This is the Way of joining with the world.[^j11-bi-text]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:007 -->

**Feng Dangke:** Water upon the earth comes from different sources into a common stream. Field channels and drainage ditches join one another to join the rivers; the nine rivers join one another to join the sea. So the lords of the myriad states, small and great, join together, and regional chiefs lead them into union with the Son of Heaven.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:008 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The Army's sheltering the people and nurturing the multitude is the well-field institution, which allows the people to unite without separation. Holding Together's establishing states and drawing their lords close is the institution of enfeoffment, which allows ruler and people to unite without separation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:009 -->

### First Six: Holding Together's first six has further good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It names Holding Together's first six because the Way of union rests upon the beginning. Sincerity at the beginning ultimately brings further good fortune. Without sincerity at the outset, how could the end be auspicious? The top six's misfortune comes from having no beginning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:012 -->

**Jiang Tisheng:** The line twice begins anew with “there is sincerity,” and includes such words as “filling the earthen vessel.” The Image omits all these and directly names the first six. This shows that Holding Together's essential Way lies at the outset. It illuminates the Judgment's “for the latecomer, misfortune.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:013 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Joining from within”: not losing oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:014 -->

**Original Meaning.** Attain correctness, and one does not lose oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Preserve one's Way of centered correctness and wait to be sought from above: then one does not lose oneself. The Changes' warnings are exact and comprehensive. Though the second is centered and correct, it is yielding in constitution and compliant in character, so it is warned through steadfast good fortune and not losing oneself. Does warning it to preserve itself and await a summons risk the latecomer's misfortune? A man's cultivation of himself is itself the Way of seeking those above. Lowering one's purpose and humiliating one's person is not self-respect. Thus Yi Yin and the Marquis of Wu were earnest in wishing to rescue the world, yet both waited until ritual courtesy had been fulfilled before coming forth.[^j11-bi-summons]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:017 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The second six is yielding, so there is concern that it may lose itself. It dwells within, awaits a request from above, and only then responds. This is joining from within; hence “not losing oneself.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:018 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Joining with the wrong people”: is this not grievous?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** People join others in seeking security and good fortune. Joining the wrong people instead brings regret and humiliation. Is that not grievous? This profoundly warns against a misplaced attachment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:020 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Joining outwardly with the worthy means following above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Joining outwardly means following the fifth. The fifth is worthy, firm, clear, centered, and correct, and occupies the ruler's position. The fourth's joining it both joins the worthy and follows above. Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:022 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Open union's good fortune comes from a correct, central position. Letting the contrary go and taking the compliant explains losing the game ahead. The townspeople need no warning: those above direct through the mean.[^j11-bi-hunt]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** The virtue of those above makes their direction impartial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Open union is auspicious because its position is correct and central. Occupying such a position means proceeding by the Way of correctness and centeredness. Union prizes impartiality, hence “correct and central.” Whenever that order of words appears, it means being correctly placed and attaining the center, as in Holding Together and Following. “Centered and correct” means attaining both centeredness and correctness, as in Contention and Waiting. In the hunting rites, taking those that fail to obey commands amounts to letting the compliant go and taking the contrary: those that heed the command and depart all escape. Holding Together, however, speaks of turning toward and away: those leaving are contrary, those approaching compliant. Thus the lost quarry is the game departing ahead. It means cherishing those who come and not pursuing those who go. No warning is required among those nearby. The ruler directs those below through level impartiality, treating distant and near alike.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:026 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Letting the contrary go means letting go the one yin above: yin riding upon yang is contrary. Taking the compliant means taking the four yin below: yin supporting yang is compliant. Losing the one yin above is therefore called losing the game ahead.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:027 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** In The Army, who makes the inappropriate appointment? The fifth. In Holding Together, who directs through the mean? Again the fifth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:028 -->

### Top Six: “Union without a beginning”: there is no way to conclude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:029 -->

**Original Meaning.** In the image of above and below, it is without a head; in the image of ending and beginning, without an ending. Without a beginning, there is no ending.[^j11-bi-head]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** If union has no beginning, how could it conclude? Even union properly begun may end in estrangement. Begin without the Way: what assurance remains for the end? Hence there is no way to conclude.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:032 -->

**Yang Jian:** Joining from the outset makes union sincere. Not joining at the beginning and only seeking union at the end is neither loyal nor trustworthy; others will not associate with such a person. It is a course toward misfortune. “Head” means beginning. With a beginning, there is an ending; without a beginning, how could one conclude? Hence “no way to conclude.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4647:033 -->

**Jiang Tisheng:** This is precisely the Judgment's meaning of misfortune for the latecomer.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4647:033 -->

<a id="eee-4648"></a>

## 9. Xiaoxu 小畜 — Small Restraint

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4648 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind travels above Heaven: Small Restraint. Thus the noble person refines his cultivated virtue.[^j11-small-cultivation]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wind has energy but no solid substance. It can restrain, but not for long: hence the image of Small Restraint. Refining cultivated virtue means that one cannot yet accumulate deeply and extend beneficence afar.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Qian's firm vigor is restrained by Xun. Only yielding compliance can restrain and check a firm, vigorous nature. Yet though it can check it, it cannot firmly control that vigor; it merely softens and binds it through yielding compliance. Hence Small Restraint. Observing its meaning, the noble person enhances the beauty of his cultivated virtue. Restraining and gathering also mean accumulating within. What the noble person accumulates is, on a great scale, the Way, virtue, and the work of ordering the world; on a small scale, literary accomplishment, abilities, and arts. He observes Small Restraint to refine cultivated virtue, which is small compared with the Way and rightness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:005 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** When a strong wind passes, grasses and trees bend before it. Afterward they quickly return to their former condition. This is restraint that cannot last, because wind has energy but no solid substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:006 -->

### First Nine: “Returning by one's own Way”: its meaning is auspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:007 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yang-firm ability returns by its proper Way, and its meaning is auspicious. The first and fourth are proper correspondents; in a time of restraint, they are the ones that restrain one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:008 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:009 -->

**Zhang Jun:** Able to turn back upon oneself and return to the Way, one's conduct cannot contradict principle. This is the ability to restrain oneself; hence its meaning is auspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:010 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Drawn into returning,” at the center: it too does not lose itself.[^j11-small-return]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:011 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Too” continues the preceding line's meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second occupies and attains the center. In firmness and yieldingness, advancement and retreat, it does not lose the central Way. Yang's return must be forceful. Yet because the second is centered, even vigor in advancing does not become excessive rigidity. Excessive rigidity would mean losing oneself. The line speaks only of being drawn into returning and its good fortune; the Image further discloses the excellence of its central position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:014 -->

**Yang Wanli:** The first returns at ease, hence returning of itself. The second makes an effort to return, hence being drawn into returning. Because it can make this effort, it too is granted the judgment of not losing itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:015 -->

**Yu Yan:** Going without returning cannot avoid losing oneself. Having returned, it too does not lose itself. “Too” continues the previous line's meaning: like the first nine, it does not lose itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:016 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Husband and wife turn against each other”: he cannot set his household right.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:017 -->

**Original Meaning.** Master Cheng says: “The loosened wheel fittings and hostile looks are the third's own doing.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Husband and wife turn against each other because he cannot set his household right. The third does not conduct itself according to the Way, so the fourth can control it and prevent its advancement. This resembles a husband unable to put his household in order, thereby bringing hostile looks between them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:020 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** All three yang in the lower trigram are restrained by Xun. The first nine stops at the outset, returning of itself to the Way without restraint being applied and leaving no fault to repair. This is restraint at its finest. The second nine has already moved before being drawn back. Only when drawn does it return; only when restrained does it stop. Effort has already been applied. Yet because it is centered and has not gone far, it still does not lose the Way. “Too” grants a qualified acceptance. The third nine's firmness has already passed the center before restraint is applied. With the fourth directly above, conflict must follow. It is like someone who has already mounted a carriage before the wheel fittings come loose and he is held back, or a husband whose conduct is incorrect and a wife who looks at him angrily and disputes with him. Hence he cannot set his household right.[^j11-small-household]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:021 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “There is sincerity; fear departs”: joining purpose with those above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Once the fourth has sincerity, the fifth trusts it and shares its purpose. Thus fear can depart and there is no blame. If fear departs, the departure of bloodshed is evident: the lesser matter is named. Once the fifth shares its purpose, all the yang follow it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:024 -->

**Guo Zhongxiao:** Joining purpose above means joining the sincere purpose of the fifth nine. Precisely through that union, it can restrain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:025 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Merely saying that fear departs makes the departure of bloodshed evident. If even fear is escaped, injury must be far away. The lighter matter reveals the heavier.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:026 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Sincerity binds together”: not possessing wealth alone.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Sincerity binds together because neighboring companions are all drawn into following it. It shares the multitude's aims rather than possessing its wealth alone. Amid hardship, the noble person's utmost sincerity wins the assistance of many people's strength and enables him to bring them through together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:028 -->

### Top Nine: “Rain has fallen; there is rest”: virtue has accumulated to fullness. “For the noble person, going forward brings misfortune”: there is cause for apprehension.[^j11-small-doubt]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Rain having fallen and rest having come mean that the Way of restraint has accumulated to fullness and completion. Yin is about to reach its greatest strength; movement by the noble person then brings misfortune. If yin rivals yang, it must diminish yang. If petty people oppose noble people, they must harm them. How could there be no apprehension? Yet if one anticipates the danger, remains vigilant, and seeks a means of controlling it, misfortune need not follow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4648:031 -->

**Yang Jian:** Restraint has already brought passage. To go further and control what restrains is to offend against it, not to follow the Way. “There is rivalry” means a rivalry that does not accord. Kun's top six, “yin rivals yang,” has this same meaning: a course toward misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4648:031 -->

<a id="eee-4649"></a>

## 10. Lü 履 — Treading

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4649 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven above, lake below: Treading. Thus the noble person distinguishes higher and lower and settles the people's purposes.[^j11-treading-order]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Cheng's Commentary gives the full explanation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven above and lake below express the correct principle of higher and lower. Human conduct should be like this, so the image is taken as Treading. Observing it, the noble person distinguishes the divisions of higher and lower to settle the people's purposes. Only when those divisions are clear can their purposes be settled; only then can government be discussed. With unsettled purposes, the world cannot be governed. In antiquity, from dukes, ministers, and great officers downward, each position matched its holder's virtue. Remaining in it throughout life, one attained one's proper share. If position did not match virtue, the ruler raised and advanced the person. Men of service cultivated their learning; when that learning matured, the ruler sought them. None of this was arranged by themselves. Farmers, artisans, and merchants diligently pursued their occupations, and what they enjoyed had its limits. Thus all had settled purposes, and the world's hearts could be united. In later ages, from ordinary men of service to the highest ministers, each day brought ambition for honor. Farmers, artisans, and merchants daily sought wealth and extravagance. Countless hearts raced together after gain. With the world in such confusion, how could it be united? Keeping it from disorder would be difficult. This arises from unsettled purposes above and below. The noble person observes Treading and distinguishes their divisions, letting each attain a proper share and thereby settling the people's hearts and purposes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:005 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “Why is Treading explained throughout in terms of ritual?” Answer: “Ritual gives priority to humility. Treading concerns what one actually practices. If conduct does not follow ritual, it naturally becomes contrary. Hence ‘through Treading, one acts harmoniously.’”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:006 -->

**Wang Yinglin:** “Heaven above, lake below: Treading” is the Changes speaking of ritual. “Thunder comes forth and stirs the earth: Enthusiasm” is the Changes speaking of music. Duke Cheng Lü's explanation rests upon the History of the Han: Heaven above and lake below, spring thunder arising—the former kings observed these images and established ritual and music.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:007 -->

**He Kai:** Heaven is high and Earth low, Heaven honored and Earth humble. The lake lies still lower: the lowliest of the low.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:008 -->

### First Nine: “Going with plain conduct”: simply fulfilling one's aspiration.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going while resting at ease in one's plain condition does not seek mere profit; it simply fulfills one's aspiration. “Simply” means exclusively. If ambition for rank contended within with the wish to practice the Way, how could one rest at ease in plain conduct?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:011 -->

**Li Xinchuan:** Going with plain conduct is what the Doctrine of the Mean calls acting according to one's present station. Simply fulfilling one's aspiration is what it calls not hankering after what lies outside that station.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:012 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “The secluded person's steadfastness brings good fortune”: inwardly, he does not disorder himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Way of Treading rests upon calm stillness. With serenity and correctness within, one's conduct is secure and spacious. If the center were agitated, how could one be at ease in one's course? Thus the secluded person alone can remain firmly steadfast and auspicious: his heart is quiet, and he does not disorder himself through desires for gain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:015 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** The first's plainness is described as fulfilling aspiration; the second's level course as freedom from inner disorder. This shows that their personal conduct proceeds from settled purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:016 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “The one-eyed can see,” but not clearly enough. “The lame can tread,” but not well enough to walk beside others. The misfortune of being bitten comes from an inappropriate position. “The warrior acts as a great ruler”: his purpose is firm.[^j11-treading-warrior]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A yielding person's ability is insufficient: sight lacks clarity and walking cannot go far, yet he insists upon firmness. With such conduct, could he escape harm? Yieldingness in the third does not tread correctly; hence disaster, a bite, and misfortune. The warrior supplies the comparison because the line occupies yang: weak in ability, firm in purpose. Firm purpose becomes reckless action, and conduct departs from the Way, like a warrior acting as a great ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:019 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** The third is obscure in constitution and weak in ability, originally insufficient for action. Yet in Treading's time, one yin governs and happens to meet its moment. Thus it disregards its inappropriate place, boldly acting and stepping into danger and calamity. Only a warrior may use this course in the king's service, advancing single-mindedly to fulfill a firm purpose. Hence the line says this after the misfortune of the bite. Each application has its fitting place.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:020 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Fearful vigilance; good fortune in the end”: the purpose is carried through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fearful vigilance brings eventual good fortune because the purpose is to move on, not remain. Leaving danger brings good fortune. Yang firmness can act; occupying yieldingness conducts itself compliantly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:023 -->

**Li Guo:** Apprehension and fear are the means of carrying one's purpose through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:024 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Both the third and fourth tread upon the tiger's tail. Why is the third inauspicious and the fourth auspicious? The third is yielding but firm in purpose, boldly acting without fear. The fourth is firm but yielding in purpose, acting carefully and knowing what to fear. Fear enables precautions; hence good fortune in the end. That good fortune is the fulfillment of its purpose to advance upward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:025 -->

**Shen Yiguan:** Taken together, Qian is the tiger. Taken line by line, only the fifth is the tiger. Thus the fourth nine also has the image of treading the tiger's tail. A nine in the fourth is precisely opposite to a six in the third; hence its purpose can be carried through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:026 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Decisive treading; steadfastness brings danger”: the position is correct and fitting.[^j11-treading-correct-danger]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** It is injured by that upon which it relies.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The warning against decisive treading comes precisely because it properly occupies the honored position. At the highest rank, possessing power to decide alone, it trusts its own firmness and decision and ceases to be apprehensive. Even granting correctness, this remains a dangerous course.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:029 -->

### Top Nine: Supreme good fortune at the top: great blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** If supreme good fortune is attained, there is great blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The top is Treading's ending. A person's conduct is good and auspicious, and at its conclusion every turn remains without defect: such a person has great blessing. Human conduct prizes completion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4649:033 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** At the top means Treading's ending. To attain supreme good fortune at that ending brings great blessing. “At the top” explains why there is supreme good fortune; “great blessing” directly explains supreme good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4649:033 -->

<a id="eee-4650"></a>

## 11. Tai 泰 — Peace

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4650 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven and Earth interact: Peace. Thus the ruler shapes and completes Heaven and Earth's Way, assists what is fitting in Heaven and Earth, and thereby guides and supports the people.[^j11-peace-assisting]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Shaping and completing restrain excess; assisting supplies deficiency.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven and Earth interact, yin and yang harmonize, and the myriad things flourish: hence Peace. The ruler should embody their free interaction, shaping and completing their Way and assisting what is fitting in them to guide and support living people. Shaping and completing means embodying the Way of their interaction and establishing appropriate forms for its application. When Heaven and Earth interact, things flourish; the ruler embodies this in institutions. He enables the people to use Heaven's seasons and Earth's advantages, assisting the work of transformation and nurture and completing its rich benefits. When spring energy brings things forth, he establishes methods for planting; when autumn energy brings things to maturity, methods for gathering and storing. This assists what is fitting in Heaven and Earth and thereby supports the people. Their lives depend upon rulers establishing institutions to teach, lead, and assist them, so that life and nourishment reach fulfillment. This is guiding and supporting them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:005 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Shaping and completing means cutting into sections; assisting means helping it. Earth's transforming activity continues as an undivided whole; the sage distinguishes sections within it. For example, the transformation of energy completes a yearly circuit, and the sage divides it into spring, summer, autumn, and winter.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:006 -->

**Cai Yuan:** Transforming energy circulates in unbroken continuity; the sage regulates it by distinguishing the four seasons' divisions. Earth's terrain stretches far, with its lines crossing in every direction; the sage regulates it by distinguishing east, west, south, and north. This shapes and completes Heaven and Earth's Way. Spring's generation and autumn's killing are the spontaneous course of the seasons. Millet on high ground and rice on low ground accord with the terrain. The sage assists by having people plow in spring and harvest in autumn, plant millet above and rice below. This assists what is fitting in Heaven and Earth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:007 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Heaven and Earth interact, yin and yang harmonize, and the myriad things flourish: hence Peace. The ruler models this, shaping their Way and assisting their fitting conditions. Thus nothing between Heaven and Earth fails to find peace.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:008 -->

### First Nine: “Pulling up couch grass; going forward brings good fortune”: the purpose is directed outward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When peace is approaching, the assembled worthy people all wish to advance upward. The three yang share that purpose; hence couch grass whose connected roots advance together. Purpose directed outward means upward advancement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:011 -->

**Yang Wanli:** The noble person's purpose concerns the world, not his single person. Hence it is directed outward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:012 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Embracing the uncultivated, one gains accord with the central course”: through radiance and greatness.[^j11-peace-embracing]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Image quotes “embracing the uncultivated” while explaining all four qualities. Possessing them, one accords with the virtue of the central course, and one's Way is radiant and manifestly great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:014 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Commentary's quoting only “embracing the uncultivated” is not an abbreviation standing for what follows. Embracing the uncultivated is the foundation of government. Yet it accords with the central Way because it is upright, great, radiant, and clear, with impartial discernment. Thus the resolution of crossing a river on foot, the illumination that does not neglect the distant, and the impartiality of leaving companions behind complement that embrace, bringing everything into accord with the central Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:015 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Nothing goes without returning”: Heaven and Earth's meeting point.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Nothing going without returning speaks of Heaven and Earth's interaction. Yang descending below must return above; yin rising above must return below. This is the constant principle of contraction and extension, departure and return. Through Heaven and Earth's meeting, the text illuminates the inconstancy of obstruction and peace as a warning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:017 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Heaven and Earth's meeting point simply means where Qian and Kun meet. Viewed as a whole figure, the outer trigram is yin going outward. Viewed line by line, the outer trigram is also yin coming back.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:018 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Fluttering, without wealth”: all have lost substance. “Without admonition, there is sincerity”: the heart's own wish.[^j11-peace-substance]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:019 -->

**Original Meaning.** Yin originally belongs below; above, it has lost its substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fluttering is rapid descent. That neighbors follow without waiting for wealth is because all three yin above have lost their substance. Yin belongs below, yet now dwells above: this is losing substance. They join sincerely without awaiting admonition because it is their hearts' wish. What principle requires is Heaven; what all share is the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:022 -->

**Li Jian:** The line says without wealth; the Image says losing substance. Both mean not using wealth and rank to lord it over others, but having an open center without self. Neighbors are companions: the fifth and top. Thus the fourth and fifth are both described as carrying out a wish. At the lower trigram's beginning, companions join with those above; at the upper trigram's beginning, neighbors join with those below. Above and below interact, and their purposes unite.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:023 -->

**Yu Yan:** Losing substance resembles Meng's fourth six being far from substance: both take yang as substantial. Yin follows yang as poverty relies upon wealth. Now the three yin are outside and have lost their support; hence all have lost substance. Their wish is for above and below to interact and their purposes to unite. In Peace, neither side distrusts or resents the other. This comes from their original hearts; hence the heart's own wish.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:024 -->

**He Kai:** Losing substance simply means being without wealth. Though without wealth, neighbors follow because all three lines lack wealth and wish to draw upon yang. Without prior agreement, they trust one another, each acting from the wish of its heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:025 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Wang Bi takes yin above as losing substance, and Cheng and Zhu follow him. Yet throughout the Changes, openness and substance are distinguished by yin and yang, not by upper and lower positions. Yang lines are substantial and wealthy; yin lines open and unwealthy. Thus losing substance clearly explains being without wealth. It is like “possessing substance yet seeming empty.” The fourth and fifth both open their centers to associate below, treating position, power, ability, and virtue as though they possessed none. This is the Great Learning's “having no other skill” and Mencius's forgetting one's power. The explanations of Li, Yu, and He accord with the Classic's intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:026 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Blessing and supreme good fortune”: carrying out the wish through the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Blessing and supreme good fortune are attained because one unites through the central Way and carries out one's wish. Centered virtue enables the employment of a worthy person of firm centeredness. What one heeds and follows is entirely one's own wish. Without wishing it, could one follow?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:029 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Carrying out the wish through the mean means employing yielding centered virtue to fulfill the wish of uniting with those below. Thus blessing and supreme good fortune are received. This is above and below interacting with a shared purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:030 -->

### Top Six: “The wall falls back into the moat”: its commands are disordered.[^j11-peace-commands]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:031 -->

**Original Meaning.** Disordered commands bring a return to Obstruction. Proclamations are the means of addressing it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4650:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The wall falls back into the moat. Its commands are disordered, and this cannot be stopped.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4650:032 -->

<a id="eee-4651"></a>

## 12. Pi 否 — Obstruction

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4651 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven and Earth do not interact: Obstruction. Thus the noble person restrains the display of his virtue to avoid difficulty; salary and rank cannot confer glory upon him.[^j11-obstruction-withdrawal]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** He gathers his virtue inward rather than displaying it, avoiding the trouble caused by petty people. Others cannot glorify him with salary and position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven and Earth do not communicate, hence Obstruction. In a time of blockage, the noble person's Way declines. He should observe this image, restrain his virtue, and avoid disaster; he must not occupy a glorious position for its salary. Obstruction is when petty people gain their aims. If the noble person occupies a conspicuously honored place, disaster must reach him. Thus he should remain obscure in straitened simplicity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:004 -->

### First Six: “Pulling up couch grass; steadfastness brings good fortune”: the purpose is directed toward the ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:005 -->

**Original Meaning.** A petty person who changes into a noble person can think of cherishing the ruler rather than calculating private advantage.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:006 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The line uses a six preserving itself below to illuminate how a noble person lives in a low position. The Image further reveals his heart. Firmly keeping his integrity below does not mean that he delights in refusing advancement and perfecting himself alone. The Way is blocked and advancement impossible, so he rests content. Yet his heart has never ceased to concern the world. His purpose is always to find a ruler and advance in order to bring peace and aid to the world. Hence purpose directed toward the ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:007 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:008 -->

**Wang Bi:** Purpose directed toward the ruler prevents heedless advancement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:009 -->

**Hu Yuan:** The noble person's purpose has never ceased to be guiding the ruler and benefiting the people. Even in this blocked time, withdrawing, preserving correctness, refusing heedless advancement, and waiting for the time still express only that purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:010 -->

**Guo Yong:** My late father said: “The late great officer had these words: high in the temple court, one worries for the people; far away among rivers and lakes, one worries for the ruler.” This expresses Peace's purpose directed outward and Obstruction's purpose directed toward the ruler. The whole figure regards the inner trigram as petty people, yet the line regards the first as a noble person: what Yichuan calls taking meanings according to the time, changing without a fixed rule. Purpose directed toward the ruler—does a noble person who restrains virtue to avoid trouble forget his ruler? How could the rightness of ruler and minister be discarded? Thus the sage did not endorse the ways of the basket-bearing recluse and his like.[^j11-obstruction-allusions]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:011 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** The time is blocked, so companions preserve correctness together below. If Obstruction reverses into Peace, they too advance together like Peace's first nine. Hence that first nine's Image says purpose directed outward, and this first six's Image says purpose directed toward the ruler. Acting or stopping depends upon the time, but the noble person's purpose concerning the ruler remains present everywhere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:012 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Original Meaning takes this line chiefly as a petty person and therefore wishes him to think of cherishing the ruler. Yet although the whole figure distinguishes great and small, its line statements attach meanings concerning noble people. Zhu Xi once wrote to Chen Liang: “In one's lack of recognition, perfect oneself alone, making great rightness clear to the world, so that all people know the correctness of the Way and rightness and preserve it while awaiting commands from above. This too repays a favor that has received no return. Must one necessarily advance into action and take the world in hand?” This is precisely the Image Commentary's intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:013 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “The great person has passage amid obstruction”: not becoming confused with the crowd.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:014 -->

**Original Meaning.** This means not becoming mixed into the crowd of petty people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In a time of obstruction, the great person preserves upright integrity and does not mix with petty people. Though his person is obstructed, his Way has passage; hence passage amid obstruction. Personal success gained without the Way would instead obstruct the Way. The text says great person rather than noble person because one capable of this has a great Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:017 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** The second six meets a time when above and below do not interact. Though the fifth is its proper correspondent, there is no means of communication. Encompassing and supporting is the ordinary behavior of petty people. A great person, however, does not seek association contrary to the Way. Though personally obstructed, his Way has passage. How could he busy himself with accommodating and supporting others, mixing unnoticed into the crowd?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:018 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Containing shame”: the position is inappropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yielding yin in Obstruction lacks both centeredness and correctness. Its shameful conduct arises from inappropriate placement. Occupying an improper place means acting without the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:020 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:021 -->

**Wang Bi:** It uses a petty course to support those above, yet its position is inappropriate. Hence it contains shame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “There is a commission; no blame”: the purpose is carried through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With the ruler's commission, there is no blame. One can then remedy obstruction and carry one's purpose through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: The great person's good fortune: the position is correct and fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Possessing a great person's virtue and attaining the correct, highest position, one can bring the world's obstruction to rest. Hence good fortune. Without the position, what could one do even with the Way? Thus the sage's position is called a great treasure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:026 -->

### Top Nine: When obstruction ends, it is overturned. How could it continue?[^j11-obstruction-action]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At its end, obstruction must be overturned. What principle could allow endless obstruction? Reversal at the utmost is a constant principle. Yet turning danger into security and disorder into order requires yang-firm ability. Thus Obstruction's top nine can overturn obstruction, whereas Zhun's top six cannot transform initial difficulty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4651:029 -->

**He Kai:** The word “then” must be brought back to human action: when obstruction reaches its limit, one should consider how to overturn it. How could one let it continue? The intention is precisely to hold people responsible for accomplishing this.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4651:029 -->

<a id="eee-4652"></a>

## 13. Tongren 同人 — Fellowship

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4652 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven together with fire: Fellowship. Thus the noble person groups kindred people and distinguishes things.[^j11-fellowship-difference]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Heaven is above and fire flames upward: their natures agree. Grouping kindred people and distinguishing things examines differences so as to attain fellowship.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The text does not say fire beneath Heaven, or Heaven having fire below, but Heaven together with fire. Heaven is above and fire tends upward: fire agrees with Heaven, hence Fellowship. The noble person observes this image to group kindred people and distinguish things, assigning each to its kind and discerning similarities and differences. The parties of noble and petty people, principles of good and evil or right and wrong, affinities and separations in the dispositions of things, similarities and differences in the principles of affairs—he clearly distinguishes all such differences and agreements. Thus in dealing with things he does not lose the proper method.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:005 -->

**Yu Fan:** Directions gather according to kind, and things divide according to their groups. The noble person harmonizes without merely agreeing. Hence, in Fellowship, he groups kindred people and distinguishes things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:006 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Grouping kindred people concerns people; distinguishing things concerns things. Some principles under Heaven cannot all be identical, so one distinguishes them according to their respective categories.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:007 -->

### First Nine: Going out through the gate into fellowship—who could blame this?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going out through the gate to join others outside makes fellowship broad, without private partiality. Differences in closeness and distance or generosity and meagerness give rise to faults. With no partisan attachment, who could blame one?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:010 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Going out through the gate into fellowship explains “fellowship at the gate,” making clear that at the gate means going out. One goes outside to join others, without a private bond. Neither losing oneself within nor losing others outside, who could blame one?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:011 -->

**He Kai:** The Commentary explains “fellowship at the gate” as going out through it. Adding the single word “out” makes the intention clearer.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Fellowship within the clan”: a course toward humiliation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Other hexagrams esteem centered, correct correspondence, yet in Fellowship it is humiliating. Thus the fifth does not take the ruler's meaning: private attachment is not a ruler's Way. Fellowship through private interests is humiliating.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:015 -->

**Jiang Bao:** Only going out through the gate brings no blame. Fellowship within the clan includes only those inside the gate; hence humiliation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:016 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Although the fifth nine and second six are proper correspondents, the Changes often warns the second six, as in Holding Together's not losing oneself and Gathering's purpose not yet changed. In Fellowship, its correspondence is especially exclusive, hence a humiliating course. If one agrees through exclusive affection rather than correct principle, the course is humiliating. This is a warning prompted by the prognostication, not a departure from the hexagram's meaning. The whole figure speaks generally of responding to all the yang, not exclusively to the fifth nine. The individual line speaks specifically of correspondence with the fifth and uses this to disclose complete impartiality. These meanings do not conflict.[^j11-fellowship-clan]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Hiding weapons in the thicket”: the opponent is firm. “Not rising for three years”: how could one act?[^j11-fellowship-opponent]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:018 -->

**Original Meaning.** This says that one cannot act.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The opponent is the fifth, both firm and correct. Could it be deprived of what is its own? Thus one fears it and hides. Not rising for three years—how could one ultimately act?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:020 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** “Opponent” means a corresponding partner. Gen speaks of opposing correspondents and Inner Sincerity of obtaining a counterpart; both refer to corresponding lines.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:021 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Mounting the wall”: rightness does not permit prevailing. Its good fortune comes from being brought to an impasse and returning to the rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:022 -->

**Original Meaning.** It has mounted the wall, so the difficulty is not insufficient strength. It refrains from attacking only because rightness does not permit victory. Able to decide by rightness, it reaches an impasse and returns to the proper standard; hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It mounts the wall yet cannot attack because rightness does not permit prevailing. Wrong attacking right cannot prevail in rightness. Its good fortune comes from this failure, which brings it to an impasse and back to the proper rule. The second is desired by all the yang. Why do only the third and fourth have the meaning of contention and seizure? They lie between the second and fifth. The first and top are distant, so their meanings differ.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Fellowship's beginning comes from straightness within. Great armies meet: this speaks of overcoming one another.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:025 -->

**Original Meaning.** Straightness means that rightness is on one's side.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At first it cries and wails because sincerity is within and its claim is right; it cannot contain the urgency of its indignation. Though the enemy is strong enough to require great armies, rightness and principle prevail, and it can ultimately overcome. Thus the text speaks of overcoming one another. This means the ability to prevail and shows the strength of the two yang opponents.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:028 -->

**Dong Zhu:** Although great armies must overcome before the meeting, this too is because their rightness and principle agree: intervening things cannot ultimately keep them apart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:029 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** In the Changes, cries express the heart and pour forth sincerity. Hence straightness within means utmost sincerity accumulated inwardly. In Fellowship's time, the second and fifth properly correspond. That they must overcome before meeting arises from the outer trigram's image of reversing difference and returning to agreement. No additional explanation need be sought elsewhere.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:030 -->

### Top Nine: “Fellowship in the outskirts”: the purpose has not been attained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Dwelling far away, with no one to join, it ultimately has nothing to regret. Yet in the Way of Fellowship, its purpose of seeking union remains unfulfilled. Though without regret, it has not handled the situation well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:032 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:033 -->

**Cai Yuan:** It has not reached the open country and has not fulfilled the Way of great fellowship. Hence its purpose is unattained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4652:034 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Outside the figure lies the image of open country, where fellowship brings success. This line says only no regret, showing that the outskirts remain one step short of open country and the purpose of great fellowship remains unattained. Confucius may indeed be called a skillful reader of the Duke of Zhou's words.[^j11-fellowship-outskirts]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4652:034 -->

<a id="eee-4653"></a>

## 14. Dayou 大有 — Great Possession

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4653 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire above Heaven: Great Possession. Thus the noble person checks evil and promotes good, complying with Heaven's beneficent mandate.[^j11-great-possession-mandate]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Fire above Heaven illuminates widely, giving the image of Great Possession. With possessions already great, failure to govern them allows seeds of discord to emerge. Heaven's mandate contains good, not evil. Thus checking evil and promoting good accords with Heaven. Turning this back upon oneself requires exactly the same.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Fire high above Heaven illuminates the multitude of things; hence Great Possession, which means abundance and variety. The noble person observes it to suppress the many evils and bring good people to prominence, honoring Heaven's beautiful, beneficent mandate. Among the multitude of things are distinctions of good and evil. Enjoying abundant possession, the noble person should carry out Heaven's work, governing and nurturing every kind. Governing the multitude consists simply in checking evil and promoting good. Punishing evil and encouraging goodness follow Heaven's mandate and bring security to living beings.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** Great Possession is an image of comprehensive inclusion. Thus one checks evil and promotes good, completing the excellence of things and complying with Heaven's virtuous mandate to benefit them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:006 -->

**Sima Guang:** Fire above Heaven is utmost clarity. Before utmost clarity, neither good nor evil can hide. Raise the good and restrain the evil; let rewards and punishments be fitting. Only then can one preserve possession of the four quarters. This complies with Heaven's beneficent mandate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:007 -->

**Yang Wanli:** Heaven punishes the guilty; I restrain them through Heaven. Heaven commissions the virtuous; I promote them through Heaven. What private part do I have in this? This is Shun and Yu possessing the world without treating it as their own affair. Hence complying with Heaven's beneficent mandate. In Fellowship, Li is below and does not dare monopolize authority, so its work stops at classification and distinction. In Great Possession, Li is above and authority issues from oneself, so its work extends to restraint and promotion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:008 -->

### First Nine: Great Possession's first nine has no involvement in harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At Great Possession's beginning, keep difficulty in mind, and arrogance and excess have no way to arise. Thus one is not involved in harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:011 -->

**Lu Zhenqi:** Preserving the ending requires care at the beginning. Only attention to difficulty at the outset permits Heaven's assistance at the end. Thus the first says “Great Possession's first nine” and the top “Great Possession's auspicious top”: the name appears only at root and culmination.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:012 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** A nine at the beginning retains its original heart unchanged. Without involvement, there is no harm. Once beyond this, involvement brings harm. How could one fail to attend to the ending as carefully as the beginning, always approaching it as difficult?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:013 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “A great wagon for carrying”: what is loaded within does not break it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A sturdy, great wagon carries a heavy load without damage, like the second nine's strong ability bearing the responsibility of Great Possession.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:016 -->

**Guo Yong:** With the Way accumulated within, nothing undertaken is unfavorable, just as the great wagon cannot be broken by its load.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:017 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** What is loaded within not breaking it resembles the Odes' “Do not spill your load.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:018 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “A duke presents offerings to the Son of Heaven”: a petty person brings harm.[^j11-great-possession-offering]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A duke should employ his resources in service to the Son of Heaven. If a petty person occupies the position, harm follows. From antiquity, lords who preserve ministerial integrity and loyally serve above nourish their people to become the king's protecting screen, and increase their resources to meet levies from above. A petty person does not know this Way of ministerial service, treating everything as private property. With numerous people and abundant resources, he instead monopolizes wealth and power and becomes increasingly uncompliant. Thus great possession by a petty person brings harm, and great possession also harms the petty person himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:020 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:021 -->

**Fang Yingxiang:** The line says the petty person cannot accomplish it; the Commentary says he brings harm. Inability to accomplish it must lead to harm.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Not displaying its abundance; no blame”: bright, penetrating discernment.[^j11-great-possession-peng]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Penetrating” describes brightness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The ability not to rest in one's abundance, and thus to avoid blame, comes from discerning wisdom. “Penetrating” means clear wisdom. Worthy and wise people discern the principles of things. In the midst of flourishing, they recognize approaching blame and therefore reduce and restrain themselves, not daring to reach utter fullness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:026 -->

**Liang Yin:** To call it discernment and further call it penetrating shows wisdom at its utmost clarity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:027 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Its sincerity joins with others”: trust awakens purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:028 -->

**Original Meaning.** One person's trustworthiness suffices to awaken the purposes of those above and below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:029 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place, continued: The good fortune of dignified authority: ease without precautions.[^j11-great-possession-authority]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:030 -->

**Original Meaning.** Excessive yieldingness makes people take one lightly, without a heart of apprehension or preparedness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:031 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The purposes of those below follow those above. If those above approach the people with sincerity and trust, the people likewise serve them sincerely. Hence sincerity joins with others. Sincerity above awakens sincere purpose below. The people's response resembles an echo answering a sound. Dignified authority is auspicious because, without it, those below would become disrespectful and unprepared, lacking reverence and readiness toward those above. Preparedness means readiness to meet their demands and responsibilities.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:032 -->

**Supplement.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:033 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Trust awakening purpose explains sincerity joining with others. Through one's own sincere trust, one arouses their purposes, so above and below respond and enter into relationship. Ease without precautions explains dignified authority's good fortune. Such authority comes from having no private partiality toward things, simply proceeding with ease and taking no defensive precautions. Things naturally stand in awe. Hence ease without precautions.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:034 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Kong's explanation also has reason. Speaking of dignified authority might suggest mutual guardedness above and below, so “ease without precautions” further explains it. This makes clear that checking evil and promoting good follow principle, rather than arising from defensive suspicion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:035 -->

### Top Nine: Great Possession's auspicious top: assistance from Heaven.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:036 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At Great Possession's top, possession has reached its limit and should change.
