Yet its conduct follows Heaven and accords with the Way, so Heaven assists it and there is good fortune. The noble person is full without overflowing: this is Heaven's assistance. The Appended Statements further explains: “Heaven assists the compliant; people assist the trustworthy. Treading upon trust, considering compliance, and also honoring the worthy, one receives Heaven's assistance: good fortune, nothing unfavorable.” Treading upon trust means treading upon the fifth, whose open center is trustworthy. Considering compliance means withdrawing humbly without claiming position. Honoring the worthy means directing one's purpose toward the fifth. Amid Great Possession, it is unfitting to add further fullness to abundance. Of the six lines, all delight in holding power and position except the first and top, which do not occupy offices. Thus the first nine has no blame and the top nine nothing unfavorable. At the top, it treads upon trust and considers compliance; hence it is auspicious above, through Heaven's assistance.[^j11-great-possession-top]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:037 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:038 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The Image says “Great Possession's auspicious top,” showing that the affair concerns the whole figure, not merely the top line. It resembles The Army's top six, where military affairs reach their conclusion. The great ruler named there is the fifth six, not the top six acting as ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4653:039 -->

**Zhao Yansu:** The fifth can honor what is above. This is why Great Possession is auspicious at the top. The ruler's great possession reaches its fullest expression in honoring the worthy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4653:039 -->

<a id="eee-4654"></a>

## 15. Qian 謙 — Modesty

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4654 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:001 -->

### Great Image: A mountain within the earth: Modesty. Thus the noble person diminishes the abundant and increases the scant, weighing things to distribute evenly.[^j11-modesty-leveling]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Lowliness containing height is Modesty's image. Diminishing abundance and increasing scarcity weigh what is fitting for things and make distribution even. Lowering the high and raising the low toward balance also expresses modesty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Earth's body is lowly, yet the mountain's height and greatness lie within it. Outward lowliness containing inward greatness gives Modesty. The text says not a mountain in the earth but a mountain within the earth: lowliness contains what is lofty. Reversing the emphasis to loftiness contained within lowliness would not fit the wording's intention. This holds for the other Images, as examination shows. The noble person observes Modesty to diminish abundance and increase scarcity, weighing things to distribute evenly. A mountain beneath the earth lowers what is high and raises what is low, expressing the restraint of elevation and lifting of lowliness, the reduction of excess and increase of deficiency. Applied to affairs, one takes from the abundant and adds to the scant, measuring their amounts to make distribution equitable and attain balance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:005 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “Diminishing abundance and increasing scarcity lower the high toward the low until things are just right; it is not simply going ever lower.” Answer: “People generally see what belongs to themselves as high and what belongs to others as low. Modesty restrains one's own height and humbles oneself before others. That is balance.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:006 -->

**Feng Yi:** Every Great Image establishes a distinct meaning, teaching how to use the Changes. Diminishing abundance and increasing scarcity, weighing things and distributing evenly, bring small and great, long and short into balance. This is not the image of the noble person's own modest virtue, but of his governing an age so as to make it modest. Neither the Judgment nor the six lines contains this meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:007 -->

**Cai Qing:** Lowliness containing height is Modesty's image. This differs from the earlier Original Meaning, where the mountain is supremely high and Earth supremely low, yet the mountain bends and rests beneath it. There, modesty centers on the mountain: high yet able to lower itself. Here it centers on Earth: low, yet containing height within, inwardly full and outwardly self-effacing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:008 -->

**Yang Qixin:** In ordinary human feeling, self-exaltation is abundant and the wish to place oneself below others scant. Without reducing one and increasing the other, one places oneself too high and others too low, losing balance between self and things. Restrain the heart that belittles the age and despises others, so the abundant is no longer excessive; increase humble, deferential compliance, so the scant is no longer deficient. Reducing abundance, one sees no surplus in oneself; increasing scarcity, one sees no deficiency in others. What one extends to self and others then finds its proper balance. Is this not the noble person's modesty?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:009 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** These explanations all return to modesty's fundamental meaning. Feng alone treats it as an extension into another field; his account too can complement the others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:010 -->

### First Six: The modest, modest noble person nurtures himself through humility.[^j11-modesty-self-nurture]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** “Modest, modest” is modesty at its utmost. The noble person tends himself through humble modesty. Tending oneself means conducting oneself, as in the Odes' “from the pasture, a gift of reeds.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:013 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Tending means nourishing. This explains the modest, modest noble person: he continually nourishes his virtue through humility.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:014 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Modesty is a lowly virtue, and the first a lowly position. The ground of nurturing virtue must begin in the lowest place. When that nurture reaches its utmost, increasing humility becomes increasingly free of baseness. This is the method of self-nurture.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:015 -->

**Zhang Shi:** The modest, modest noble person tends himself through humility, as one tends cattle or sheep until they are tractable. Only then can one speak of modesty. People now often mistake pride for nurturing their vital energy. This is merely passing, borrowed spirit, not the vast, overflowing energy of righteousness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:016 -->

**Yu Yan:** The line places a pause after “modest, modest”; its Commentary attaches “noble person” to those words, saying that utmost modesty is the noble person's virtue. Someone who is not a noble person cannot possess such modesty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:017 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Modesty sounding forth; steadfastness brings good fortune”: attained within the heart.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second's modest virtue comes from utmost sincerity accumulated within and therefore issues in sound. It is attained for itself within the heart, not forced.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:020 -->

**Hu Yuan:** Attainment within the heart means that everything the noble person does is first attained inwardly and then expressed outside. Thus this modesty comes entirely from the heart, until its reputation spreads among people and it obtains the good fortune of utmost correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:021 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The noble person modest in his labor wins the allegiance of all the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A noble person capable of laboring modestly is honored and followed by all the people. The Appended Statements says: “Laboring without boasting, achieving without claiming virtue: the utmost depth. This speaks of lowering oneself before others despite one's accomplishments. Virtue is called flourishing; ritual is called reverent. Modesty brings reverence to its utmost and thereby preserves one's position.” To have labored without boasting and achieved without claiming merit is the utmost breadth and depth of virtue. It means humbling oneself before others despite one's work and accomplishment. In terms of virtue, one is supremely flourishing; in the ritual manner of conducting oneself, supremely reverent. This is modesty. Modesty brings reverent deference to its utmost to preserve one's position. Thus one is high without danger and full without overflowing, and can be auspicious to the end. Yet modest conduct is the noble person's ordinary practice, not something undertaken to preserve office. Saying it preserves his position means that utmost reverence enables this preservation: such is modesty's Way. Similarly, saying that goodness brings a fine name does not mean that the noble person does good for reputation. The fine name comes because of goodness.[^j11-modesty-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:024 -->

**Wu Cheng:** All the people's allegiance means carrying things through to the end with good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:025 -->

**Yu Yan:** The line originally pauses after laboring modestly. The Commentary again attaches the words “noble person”: laboring yet remaining modest is his virtue. One who is not a noble person cannot act thus.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:026 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Nothing unfavorable; putting modesty into practice”: not departing from the rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** This says it is not excessive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Modesty has fitting occasions and must not exceed them, as when the fifth six may undertake an attack. The fourth alone occupies a place near the ruler and above a minister of achievement. Thus everything it does benefits from modesty. Only in this way does it meet the proper standard. Hence “not departing from the rule,” meaning attaining what is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:030 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Not departing from the rule means not violating the proper standard. Putting modesty into practice is what should be done here, not something excessive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:031 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “It is beneficial to invade and attack”: campaigning against the disobedient.[^j11-modesty-force]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** One campaigns against those whom cultivated virtue and modest deference cannot win over. If virtue cannot secure their allegiance and force is not used, how could the world be pacified? That would not be the ruler's central Way but an excess of modesty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:034 -->

**He Kai:** The invasion is not militaristic aggression. Because they will not submit, one campaigns only when compelled. This explains how campaigning can employ modesty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:035 -->

### Top Six: “Modesty sounding forth”: the purpose is not yet attained. “One may dispatch armies”: campaigning against one's own town and domain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:036 -->

**Original Meaning.** Yielding yin without office lacks sufficient ability and strength. Thus its purpose is unattained and it comes to dispatching an army, yet this suffices only to govern its private domain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:037 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** At modesty's utmost while occupying the top, the purpose of being humble remains unfulfilled. Its urgency becomes so intense that it sounds forth. Though not appropriately placed, modesty has reached excess; firm, martial means should therefore govern its own affairs. Hence benefit in dispatching armies against its own town and domain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:038 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:039 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second six's sounding modesty is explained through the heart; the top six's through purpose. Enthusiasm's first six, sounding enthusiasm, is likewise explained through purpose. Thus sounding always concerns purpose. Purpose has grief and joy, both expressed in sound. In Enthusiasm, people delight in following above; in Modesty, their purpose lies below and they do not delight in being above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:040 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** At the top, modesty sounds outwardly as reputation, yet the heart remains self-effacing and unsatisfied, its purpose still unattained. This unattained purpose is precisely its modesty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:041 -->

**He Kai:** Purpose unattained means that, at modesty's utmost, one still regards oneself as lacking and considers one's modesty insufficient. This resembles Yi's praise to Yu concerning fullness bringing loss and modesty receiving increase.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4654:042 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Image means that the top six's sounding modesty comes from an inward purpose that remains self-effacing and unsatisfied. Thus, though it may dispatch armies, it campaigns only against its own town and domain: governing oneself from beginning to end. Similarly, Fellowship's top has an unattained purpose because it has not fulfilled the heart's great fellowship; it too remains self-effacing and unsatisfied. Without the heart of Fellowship's top, the breadth of great fellowship is incomplete. Without the heart of Modesty's top, modest virtue's openness is incomplete. Gu and He alone perceive the principal meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4654:042 -->

<a id="eee-4655"></a>

## 16. Yu 豫 — Enthusiasm

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4655 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder comes forth and stirs the earth: Enthusiasm. Thus the former kings made music to exalt virtue, presenting it in abundance to the Lord on High and associating their ancestors with the offering.[^j11-enthusiasm-music]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder coming forth to stir the earth is harmony at its utmost. In making music, the former kings both modeled its sound and adopted its meaning. “In abundance” means magnificently.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder is yang energy stirring forth, with yin and yang pressing upon one another to produce sound. Yang is initially hidden and enclosed within the earth. When it moves, it emerges and shakes the earth. At first confined and pent up, it then bursts forth into free passage and harmonious delight: hence Enthusiasm. Kun is compliant and Zhen issues forth. Harmony and compliance accumulate within and emerge in sound: the image of music. The former kings observed thunder emerging from the earth and harmonious release sounding forth, and made music to praise and exalt achievement and virtue. Its magnificence extended to presentation to the Lord on High, with ancestors associated in the offering. “Abundant” means magnificent, as in the ritual expression for a grand offering. Presenting to the Lord on High and associating one's ancestors is magnificence at its utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:005 -->

**Xun Shuang:** Music is how the sage gives measure to people's delight, nourishing its correctness and guarding against its wrongfulness. Its harmony can move spirits; how much more human beings!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:006 -->

**Zheng Kangcheng:** Stirring means movement. Thunder moves above the earth, and the myriad things find delight. At utmost joy, people's hands wish to beat time and their feet to dance. Kings made music when achievements were complete. Those who gained rule through cultivated virtue established flute dances; those who gained it through force established martial dances. Each gave full expression to its virtue in the prescribed forms. Sacrificing to Heaven's Lord with ancestors associated lets them share with Heaven in enjoying the achievement. Thus the Classic of Filial Piety speaks of sacrificing to Houji at the suburban altar in association with Heaven, and to King Wen in the Hall of Light in association with the Lord on High.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:007 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The Original Meaning's “modeled its sound” means that music's sound takes thunder's sound as its model. “Adopted its meaning” concerns Enthusiasm's harmony. Thunder manifests the work of transformation and stirs Heaven and Earth's harmony; music manifests achievement and virtue and summons harmony between spirits and human beings.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:008 -->

### First Six: “Enthusiasm sounding forth”: the purpose reaches its limit—misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:009 -->

**Original Meaning.** Reaching its limit means fullness carried to the utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It names the first six because yielding yin is below yet its purpose reaches excessive fullness, unable to contain its delight and thus sounding forth. Arrogant license must bring misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:012 -->

**Yang Jian:** Its low position has not reached an extreme. Yet delight sounding forth shows that its purpose has already done so.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:013 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** The position is only at the beginning, and time and circumstances are not yet exhausted. Such competitive agitation means that purpose has reached its limit ahead of them, bringing misfortune upon itself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Not to the end of the day; steadfastness brings good fortune”: through centeredness and correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The ability to act before the day ends, with steadfastness and good fortune, comes from centered, correct virtue. That virtue makes its preservation firm, its discernment early, and its departure swift. The line's account of how the second six handles Enthusiasm has a profound teaching intention.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:017 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** Centered correctness is the meaning of firmness like a rock. This traces the reason why it does not wait until the day ends.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:018 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Gazing upward in enthusiasm; there is regret”: the position is inappropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its situation is inappropriate, lacking centeredness and correctness. Thus both advance and retreat bring regret.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:020 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:021 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** This line is the opposite of the second six. Looking upward, it cannot be firm as a rock; delaying, it cannot act before the day ends. The difference is centered correctness and its absence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “The source of enthusiasm; great attainment”: the purpose is carried through greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Through oneself, the world reaches delight and ease. Hence great attainment means that one's purpose is widely realized.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:025 -->

**Qiao Zhonghe:** Firmness receives response and purpose proceeds because the fourth's yang firmness is answered by all the yin. Thus its purpose can be carried through greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:026 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Persistently ill”: riding upon firmness. “Enduring without dying”: the center has not been lost.[^j11-enthusiasm-illness]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Persistence in illness comes from riding upon firmness and being pressed by it. Enduring without dying means that the honored central position has not been lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:029 -->

**Yang Shi:** In Enthusiasm's time, lacking firm and vigorous ability, it sinks into pleasure. Mencius says that a state with neither upright families and correcting ministers within nor rival states and external troubles without ordinarily perishes. The fifth six's riding upon firmness means having such correcting ministers and external adversaries. Because those around it rescue and correct it, it experiences correctness as an illness. Though it cannot yet hold to the mean, the center has not been lost, so it does not die in ease and pleasure. Hence enduring without dying.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:030 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** The second and fifth alone do not mention enthusiasm. The second remains still and obscure and does not indulge; the fifth rides upon firmness and dares not indulge. It resembles a person with a chronic ailment: though displeasing, it can lengthen life by maintaining a cautious heart. Thus it does not ultimately perish but continues to endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:031 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** Enthusiasm most readily engulfs people. The second six is yielding, centered, and correct, and can leave it before the day ends. The fifth six is yielding yin and incorrect, unable to avoid immersion in pleasure. It still avoids death because the center is not lost. People live through worry and hardship and die through ease and pleasure. The fifth's centeredness barely allows it to avoid death. Thus the first's sounding delight, the third's upward gaze, and the top's darkness, all lacking centeredness, are not paths of life.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:032 -->

### Top Six: “Darkened enthusiasm” at the top—how could this continue?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Darkened by delight at its utmost limit, one is about to meet disaster and blame. How could one remain like this? One must change promptly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:035 -->

**Hu Yuan:** How could this continue means that pleasure has become so excessive that feelings run loose, nature is darkened, and one does not know where to stop. How could such a state continue? The point is that change can still bring freedom from blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4655:036 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** At the top, enthusiasm has reached its limit. The extreme cannot last. Prompt change is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4655:036 -->

<a id="eee-4656"></a>

## 17. Sui 隨 — Following

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4656 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder within the lake: Following. Thus the noble person, as darkness approaches, goes indoors to rest.[^j11-following-rest]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder is hidden within the lake, resting according to the time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder shakes within the lake, and the lake follows its stirring: Following's image. The noble person observes it to act according to the time. All affairs follow what the time requires; the text chooses the clearest and nearest example. As darkness approaches, he goes indoors to rest. By day he strengthens himself without ceasing; toward dusk he withdraws within and rests to settle his body. Rising and resting follow the time and meet what is fitting. Ritual prescribes that the noble person not remain indoors by day or outdoors by night: the Way of following the time.[^j11-following-readings]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:005 -->

**Di Xuan:** Darkness means obscurity. Thunder is yang energy, active in spring and summer. Its presence within the lake indicates autumn and winter, so the noble person models this. At sunrise he attends to affairs; as darkness approaches, he withdraws to the sleeping chamber and rests.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:006 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “Master Cheng says the lake follows thunder's movement, and the noble person should rest according to the time. Is that right?” Answer: “Having said thunder moves, why does the text not say that the noble person acts, but instead that he rests? The figure has Zhen below and Dui above, an image of thunder entering the earth. Thunder hides according to the time; hence the noble person goes indoors to rest as darkness approaches.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:007 -->

### First Nine: “The office changes”: following correctness brings good fortune. “Going out through the gate to associate brings achievement”: no error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Once following brings change, what one follows must be correct for good fortune. Incorrect following brings regret and humiliation. Going outside the gate to associate is not constrained by private interests, so the association must be correct. Correctness brings achievement without error.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:010 -->

**Yu Yan:** The figure takes a yang line as its governor. A governor should not follow others, nor should yang follow yin. Yet when correctness follows correctness, this is what Following's Way requires.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:011 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Bound to the small boy”: one cannot associate with both.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In following, attaining correctness distances wrongfulness; following what is wrong loses what is right. One cannot follow both. If the second binds itself to the first, it loses the fifth. This warns that following correctness must be single-minded.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The fifth nine and second six correspond in the same way throughout. In Holding Together and Gathering this is auspicious; in Fellowship, humiliating. In Following, the second binds itself to the small boy, and its humiliation is evident. These differences arise from the meanings of the hexagrams. Here firmness lowers itself before yieldingness, and yieldingness must become attached to it. Extend this into the line's meaning, and one knows why it cannot associate with both.[^j11-following-choice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:014 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Bound to the grown man”: the purpose abandons what is below.[^j11-following-man]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Having followed above, its purpose abandons what is below and no longer follows it. Leaving below for above means leaving the low for the high. In Following, this is good.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:017 -->

**Huang Chunyao:** A person's choice and rejection depend upon purpose. The third's purpose is already bound to the fourth, so what it abandons must be the first. At the second, attachment reveals the inability to have both; at the third, abandonment strengthens the attachment it has chosen.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** How do we know that its purpose abandons below? No firm line comes down to it, so it must follow firmness above. The fourth is near and the first distant. The whole figure means firmness lowering itself before yieldingness, while this line means yieldingness following firmness. This does not match the particular meaning of the time, yet it does not lose the constant principle of yang initiating and yin following. Thus the sage still commends its purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:019 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Following brings gain”: its meaning is inauspicious. “Sincerity in the Way”: the achievement of clarity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying a position near the ruler and gaining followers is inherently inauspicious. Sincerity in the Way can avert blame: this is the achievement of clear wisdom.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:022 -->

**Yuan Shu:** Its inauspicious meaning is that there is a principle tending toward misfortune. Handle it according to the Way described afterward, and there is no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:023 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Meaning here means the hexagram's meaning. Firmness lowers itself before yieldingness, yet the fourth's firmness is followed by yieldingness. Moreover, near the ruler, it is especially liable to suspicion of gathering adherents. Hence its meaning is inauspicious.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Trust in the excellent; good fortune”: the position is correct and central.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Occupying a correct, central position and proceeding by that Way, its sincere following is correct and central: this is the excellent. Its good fortune is evident. The excellence it trusts is the second six. Following esteems attaining the center and guards against excess. When the heart delights in following, it fails to notice its excesses.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:026 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** In Following's time, one occupying the honored position with correct, central virtue trusts only what is good. Both the first and fifth speak of good fortune, but the fifth is especially auspicious because it is correct and central.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:027 -->

### Top Six: “Seizing and binding it”: exhaustion at the top.[^j11-following-binding]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:028 -->

**Original Meaning.** Exhaustion means reaching the utmost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Following is so firm that it resembles being seized, bound, and held fast: the utmost of Following's Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4656:030 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Exhaustion at the top suggests aloof elevation, like a person who cuts himself off from the age, leaves human company, and departs without returning. All the figure's yin lines speak of binding; only the top six says “seizing and binding it.” Thus the Master explains that this arises because the top six is not easily bound.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4656:030 -->

<a id="eee-4657"></a>

## 18. Gu 蠱 — Decay

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4657 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind beneath the mountain: Decay. Thus the noble person rouses the people and nurtures virtue.[^j11-decay-nurture]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wind beneath the mountain brings deterioration and work to be done. No work is greater than these two: the Ways of governing oneself and governing others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wind beneath the mountain encounters it and turns back, scattering and disordering things. Hence an image of work requiring attention. The noble person observes it to rouse and aid the people and nurture virtue. In himself, he nurtures virtue; in the world, he aids the people. Nothing he undertakes is greater than these two.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:005 -->

**Li Shunchen:** Wind beneath the mountain means wind stripping the mountain. Its trees are broken and shed: an image of decay and ruin. Repairing decay requires something that can rouse and restore. Rousing the people resembles Xun's wind stirring as commands; nurturing virtue resembles Gen's mountain developing ability and strength. In the Changes, nurturing virtue often takes its image from mountains, so Meng also speaks of resolute conduct and nurtured virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:006 -->

**Yang Wenhuan:** Nothing rouses the myriad things like wind; nothing nurtures them like mountains.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:007 -->

**Li Jian:** Wind beneath the mountain is an image of stirring things. In Decay, the people's virtue has deteriorated. To nurture it after deterioration, one must stir them to leave their former habits, just as wind's bending of things can be the means of nurturing them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:008 -->

**Yu Yan:** Small Restraint's wind is above Heaven, Viewing's above the earth, and Dispersion's above water. All are unobstructed, so all speak of traveling. Decay's wind stops beneath the mountain, obstructed and unable to spread freely. Thus it says wind is present, not that it travels.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:009 -->

**Shen Yiguan:** Wind encounters the mountain and turns back, disturbing all things: an image of work to be done. The noble person rouses the people's hearts and nurtures their virtue, renewing the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:010 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** These commentators take both rousing the people and nurturing virtue as governing others, unlike Cheng and Zhu. Examining the wording, their reading seems right. Governing oneself should not follow governing others; Meng's resolute conduct and nurtured virtue also concern the unformed person being taught. By contrast, Gradual Progress's abiding in worthy virtue and improving customs govern oneself and others in clearly marked order. Moreover, abiding and nurturing are distinct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:011 -->

### First Six: “Setting right the father's decay”: the intention is to carry on the father's work.[^j11-decay-intention]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:012 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A son's Way of repairing his father's decay has the intention of taking responsibility for the father's affairs. Thus he reverently attends to them to place the father beyond blame. Constant apprehension and vigilance ultimately bring good fortune. Complete sincerity toward the father's affairs is a path to good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:013 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:014 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Setting right the father's decay may outwardly appear uncompliant, yet the intention carries on his work. Outward practices change with the time and acquire defects through duration. How could they simply be continued? A filial son need only preserve the intention of loyal affection toward his father.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:015 -->

**Yang Jian:** Compelled to set right the father's decay, the son has never lacked a compliant intention to continue him. Intention can be continued; the actual practice cannot. Continuing the practice would leave the decay unremoved and expose the father's wrongdoing. That would not be filial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:016 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** Not continuing his affairs but continuing his intention is skillful continuation of the father's purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:017 -->

**Yang Qixin:** A predecessor whose errors caused decay may nevertheless have regretted them. Setting right the father's decay carries on that father's intention and places him beyond fault. The sage thus credits the son's worthiness and goodness to the father. How great is this teaching!

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** “The intention carries on the father” explains why the father is without blame, as Yang says.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:019 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Setting right the mother's decay”: attaining the central Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The second attains the central Way without excessive firmness: a good way of setting right the mother's decay.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:022 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** The line says one must not insist upon steadfastness, warning the diviner. The Commentary says it attains the central Way, describing the line's own image and its ability not to reach rigid insistence. Such insistence would not be the central Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:023 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Setting right the father's decay”: no blame in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** With the third's ability, repairing the father's decay may bring a little regret but no great blame in the end. Firm decision enables repair; correctness is preserved and compliance present. Hence no blame in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:026 -->

**Cai Qing:** It says not no great blame but simply no blame: more than avoidance of great blame is intended. This encourages further advancement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:027 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Indulging the father's decay”: going forward has not attained its aim.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fourth's ability could preserve ordinary practice in a time of ample ease. Yet if it wishes to go forward, it cannot attain the aim. Increase its responsibilities, and it cannot bear them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:030 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** The advance of doubled yieldingness cannot fulfill its purpose of attending to affairs. This is the humiliation of one dealing with decay.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:031 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Setting right the father, gaining praise”: sustaining through virtue.[^j11-decay-virtue]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Repairing the father's decay brings a fine reputation because a worthy person below supports and assists it with the virtue of firm centeredness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:034 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The fifth six attains the honored position and practices great centeredness, covering the predecessor's decay with a fine name. Hence setting right the father and gaining praise means sustaining through virtue: repair is accomplished through virtue rather than mere ability.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:035 -->

**Zheng Weiyue:** Once there is decay, what virtue can be continued? The text lets people speak not of continuing defects but of continuing virtue, as though unaware that the decay belonged to the predecessor.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:036 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Cheng understands the second nine to support through firm centered virtue. Yet references to continuing here all concern succession between father and son. Thus the first's intention to carry on the father resembles this sustaining through virtue. One continues the father through intention, not through his practices; through virtue, not through his affairs. When the father's virtue becomes manifest, praise also becomes clear. Sustaining through virtue directly explains gaining praise.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:037 -->

### Top Nine: “Not serving kings or lords”: the purpose may be taken as a rule.[^j11-decay-nonservice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:038 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The top nine stands outside affairs, unencumbered by worldly business, and does not serve kings and lords as a minister. Its advance and retreat follow the Way; its being employed or set aside follows the time. Could anyone but a worthy person do this? Its abiding purpose may be taken as a rule.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:039 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4657:040 -->

**Lu Quan:** What is the occupation of a man of service? To exalt his aspiration. A purpose that may be taken as a rule is precisely “holding his undertaking high.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4657:040 -->

<a id="eee-4658"></a>

## 19. Lin 臨 — Approach

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4658 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:001 -->

### Great Image: Earth above the lake: Approach. Thus the noble person's thought for teaching is inexhaustible, and his sheltering care for the people boundless.[^j11-approach-teaching]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Earth overlooking the lake is above approaching below. Both activities concern attending to those below. Inexhaustible teaching belongs to Dui; boundless accommodation to Kun.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Earth above the lake is its bank, the edge of the water. Nothing approaches and contains another thing like earth and water. Thus earth above the lake makes Approach. Observing the image of drawing near, the noble person has inexhaustible thought for teaching. Coming personally to the people, he has the intention to instruct and guide them. Inexhaustibility is utmost sincerity without weariness. Observing containment and accommodation, he has a heart to shelter and preserve them. Boundlessness is unrestricted breadth. Accommodation contains a meaning of greatness, hence inexhaustibility and boundlessness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** Nothing is better for approaching others than joy and compliance. Without relying on coercive authority, one gains their sincerity, so they do not oppose one. Thus the noble person's thought for teaching is inexhaustible and his sheltering care boundless.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:006 -->

**Liu Mu:** The bank is higher than the lake and overlooks it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:007 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** It says not merely teaching but thought for teaching, with an intention as deep as Dui's lake. It says not merely preserving the people but sheltering and preserving them, with a capacity as great as Kun's earth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:008 -->

**Yu Yan:** Approach has two meanings. In the lines' yin and yang, the great approaches the small; in earth and lake as images, above approaches below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:009 -->

**Cai Qing:** Thought for teaching means the intention to educate people and bring them to fulfillment. Teaching others goodness is called loyalty. Savor that word loyalty, and only then will one perceive the thought for teaching meant here.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:010 -->

**He also said:** Encourage them, bring them forward, correct and straighten them, support and assist them, let them attain understanding for themselves, then further rouse their virtue: here one sees the noble person's inexhaustible thought for teaching. The people are my siblings. Widowers, widows, orphans, and the solitary are my brothers and sisters in distress, with no one to whom they can appeal. All must be enabled to enjoy what brings them joy and benefit from what benefits them. Here one sees boundless sheltering care.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:011 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Approach means greatness. Earth above the lake suggests a lake filling until its surface almost meets the ground: greatness. Inexhaustible teaching and boundless care speak of the abundance of royal beneficence, penetrating deeply and spreading widely.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:012 -->

### First Nine: “Approaching through influence; steadfastness brings good fortune”: the purpose is to practice correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Steadfast good fortune means that the nine's purpose lies in practicing correctness. A nine in a yang position also responds to the fourth's correctness; its purpose is correct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:015 -->

**Wu Yueshen:** There is preserving correctness and there is practicing correctness. Approach's first is precisely like Zhun's in this respect.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:016 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Approaching through influence; good fortune, nothing unfavorable”: not simply obeying the command.[^j11-approach-command]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:017 -->

**Original Meaning.** The meaning remains unclear.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** “Not” here is a word denying something directly. Someone asked Mencius whether he had encouraged Qi to attack Yan; he answered, “No.” Elsewhere he asks whether the grain Zhongzi eats was grown by Boyi or by Robber Zhi, and says this cannot be known. In the Records of the Historian, Hou Ying says that people are certainly not easy to know. Such is the ancient use of this word. People now generally contrast it with “already,” so its meaning seems different, though it is not. The second nine and fifth influence one another to approach those below. Firm virtue grows and attains the center; their utmost sincerity moves one another. This does not arise from obeying a superior's command, and therefore brings good fortune with nothing unfavorable. The fifth belongs to compliance, the second to joy, and yin and yang correspond, so the Image specifically makes clear that mere pleasing compliance is not the source.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:019 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The growth of the noble person's Way is Heaven's mandate. Yet the mandate is not constant, so the Judgment speaks of misfortune by the eighth month and its Commentary of decline not being far away. In this situation, the noble person knows only to hold fullness as though empty. What is called “great success through correctness: Heaven's Way” therefore follows the Way, not the mandate. The second governs firmness's growth and hence the hexagram, so this meaning is disclosed to accord with the Judgment. Heaven's mandate consists in waning and waxing. During growth, one does not merely follow or receive the mandate, knowing that fullness cannot last and advancement cannot be relied upon. During decline, one's purpose does not abandon the mandate, knowing that things cannot be exhausted and what goes must return. The Changes' great meaning lies entirely here.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Sweet approach”: the position is inappropriate. Once troubled by it, blame does not persist.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A yielding person lacks centeredness and correctness, occupies the top of the lower trigram, and rides upon two yang. This is inappropriate placement. Once able to fear and become troubled by it, one must exert oneself to change. Thus fault and blame do not persist.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:023 -->

**Li Jian:** The third six is neither centered nor correct and occupies an improper position. Though it delights in that position, how could this suffice for approach? If it recognizes the fault, becomes concerned, and strives to reform itself, blame does not persist.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:024 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The third's line position is inappropriate and the fourth's appropriate, so their virtues differ in goodness. Yet the third occupies a high, powerful place, which should not be enjoyed complacently. Its doing so is what makes it inappropriate. The fourth's position gives the closest contact with those below, and it can make that contact complete: this makes it appropriate. Formal appropriateness of a line thus illuminates appropriate handling of one's actual position. This is a convention of the Changes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:025 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Complete approach; no blame”: the position is appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Being near the ruler gives it responsibility; yin in the fourth gives correctness; correspondence with the first means humbling itself before the worthy. Hence no blame, arising from appropriate placement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:028 -->

**Zheng Ruxie:** Its place is where above and below meet, the closest point of approach. In approaching those below, the chief danger is distance without communication. The fourth is already near them and occupies an entirely fitting place. Hence no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:029 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Fitting for a great ruler”: practicing the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Ruler and minister accord in the Way because kindred dispositions seek one another. The fifth possesses centered virtue and can therefore rely upon a worthy person of firm centeredness. It attains what suits a great ruler and accomplishes wise approach through practicing that virtue. Unless a ruler shares the Way and virtue of worthy talent, how could he employ it?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:032 -->

**Shen Gai:** It can use its wisdom to practice the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:033 -->

### Top Six: The good fortune of generous approach: the purpose is directed inward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Purpose directed inward responds to the first and second. It follows yang firmness with generous earnestness, so its good fortune is evident.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:036 -->

**Zhang Zhenyuan:** Purpose directed inward is the intention of taking the myriad things as one body. Thus it can be generous. Set the world and state outside one's concern, and even some small beneficence remains shallow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4658:037 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** View this inward purpose alongside the outward purpose of Peace's first. Both concern the world and state. From the first's standpoint, they are outside; from the top's, inside. Yi Yin farming while taking responsibility for the world's weight exemplifies purpose directed outward. Yao and Shun, advanced in age and weary of labor yet never forgetting the people, exemplify purpose directed inward.[^j11-approach-inward]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4658:037 -->

<a id="eee-4659"></a>

## 20. Guan 觀 — Viewing

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4659 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:001 -->

### Great Image: Wind travels over the earth: Viewing. Thus the former kings toured the regions, observed the people, and established teaching.[^j11-viewing-two-directions]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Touring the regions serves to observe the people; establishing teaching serves to become their example.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Wind travels over the earth and reaches every kind of thing: an image of journeying and comprehensive observation. The former kings embodied it in the rites of regional inspection, observing customs and establishing government and teaching. The Son of Heaven toured the four quarters, observed the people's customs, and framed his instruction accordingly. Extravagance was checked through frugality; excessive frugality was guided through ritual. Touring the regions means observing the people; establishing teaching means becoming what the people observe.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:005 -->

**The Changes of the Nine Schools:** When wind travels over the earth, grasses and trees must bend. Thus the ruler examines the four quarters, observes the people's customs, and establishes teaching.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:006 -->

**Liu Mu:** Wind over the earth reaches everywhere. One gathers songs and poems throughout the myriad states and examines their customs. Where customs differ, teaching brings them into accord.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:007 -->

### First Six: “Childlike viewing”: the petty person's Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:008 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Viewing without clarity, like a child, belongs to the petty person's station. Hence the petty person's Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:009 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:010 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Lowliness without far-sighted understanding is excusable in ordinary people but shameful in a noble person.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:011 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Peering through a door; a woman's steadfastness”: this too can be shameful.[^j11-viewing-woman]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** In a grown man, it is shameful.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Unable to perceive the great Way of yang firmness and centered correctness, the noble person catches only a glimpse of its outline. Though he can follow compliantly, this merely equals a woman's steadfastness and is shameful for him.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:015 -->

**Guo Zhongxiao:** Good fortune and misfortune differ for women and men. Thus Constancy says, “For a woman, good fortune; for a man, misfortune.” From this one knows that what benefits a woman's steadfastness can indeed be shameful for a man.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:016 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Viewing my life, advancing and retreating”: the Way has not been lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Observing what issues from oneself and advancing or retreating according to what is fitting prevent the loss of the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:018 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Way here is the Way of advancement and retreat. Assess before entering, and that Way will not be lost.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:019 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Viewing the state's radiance”: esteeming the guest.[^j11-viewing-guest]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:020 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The noble person bears ability and accomplishment, with the purpose of benefiting the world as well as himself. Some nevertheless fold these away and preserve themselves because the time lacks an enlightened ruler who can employ their Way. This is necessity, not their aspiration. Hence Mencius says that standing at the world's center and bringing security to the people within the four seas delights the noble person. Once he sees a state's flourishing virtue and radiance, he has what the ancients called an extraordinary encounter. He therefore aspires to advance into the royal court to practice his Way. Hence viewing the state's radiance means esteeming the guest's position. Esteeming means aspiring: his purpose longs to be a guest at the royal court.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:021 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:022 -->

**Yang Jian:** This says that the state esteems and honors worthy guests, making advancement possible.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:023 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “Viewing my life”: observing the people.[^j11-viewing-self-people]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:024 -->

**Original Meaning.** Here the Master speaks through the principle involved, making clear that a ruler observing his own conduct must not consider only his personal gains and losses. He should also observe whether the people's virtue is good or bad, using this to examine himself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** My life means what issues from myself. A ruler wishing to know whether his conduct is good should observe the people. Good customs indicate good government and transformation. Wang Bi rightly says: “Observe the people to examine one's own Way.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:027 -->

**Hu Yuan:** Observe a stream to know its source, a shadow to know what casts it, and the people to know the merits and failures of one's government.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:028 -->

### Top Nine: “Viewing his life”: the purpose is not yet at peace.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:029 -->

**Original Meaning.** Purpose not yet at peace means that, though without office, one must not forget vigilance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Though not in office, one has virtue that people observe and take as a model. Thus careful self-examination is required. Observe what issues from oneself and never fall short of the noble person, so that people's expectations are not disappointed and they may be transformed. Lack of office is no reason to relax, indulge oneself, and attend to nothing. The purpose cannot rest at ease; hence it is not yet at peace. Peace means settled ease.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4659:032 -->

**Lu Xisheng:** The people's goodness and badness depend upon my virtue's transforming influence. Purpose not yet at peace is concern that they have not yet been transformed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4659:032 -->

<a id="eee-4660"></a>

## 21. Shihe 噬嗑 — Biting Through

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4660 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder and lightning: Biting Through. Thus the former kings made punishments clear and put the laws in order.[^j11-biting-order]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder and lightning should read lightning and thunder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Images do not reverse the order of their trigrams, so these words may have been transposed. Thunder and lightning require one another and appear together, also giving an image of joining. Lightning illuminates and thunder awes. The former kings observed them, modeling their clarity and authority to make punishments clear and regulate laws and commands. Laws clarify the principles of affairs and establish safeguards accordingly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:005 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** Thunder stirs things and lightning illuminates them. When they shake and shine, nothing can harbor wrongfulness. The former kings therefore modeled them in clarifying punishments and ordering laws, displaying these to all.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:006 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Yin and yang biting together produce thunder as sound and lightning as light. These two join through biting; hence thunder and lightning make Biting Through.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:007 -->

**Xu Ji:** Clarifying punishments shows people what to avoid; ordering laws guards them and teaches what to fear. This is the former kings' loyal, generous intention. It has not yet reached deciding cases and imposing sentences, so it differs from Abundance's Image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:008 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** The Stone Classics associated with Cai Yong read lightning and thunder.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:009 -->

**Cai Qing:** The former kings' clarifying punishments and ordering laws concern establishing law, hence former kings. Abundance's deciding cases and imposing punishments concern applying law, hence the noble person.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:010 -->

**Xue Xuan:** Biting Through, Adornment, Abundance, and The Wanderer all discuss punishment through Li's fire, showing that law prizes clarity. Biting Through and Abundance interchange fire and thunder: law values authority and clarity together. Adornment and The Wanderer interchange mountain and fire: law values clarity joined with caution.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:011 -->

### First Nine: “Feet in the stocks, toes concealed”: not proceeding.[^j11-biting-toes]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** Concealed toes also image not advancing into wrongdoing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Stocks cover and injure the toes, teaching the person to heed correction and not dare extend his wrongdoing. Hence not proceeding. The ancients prescribed stocks for lesser offenses to restrain movement and prevent advancement into evil.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:015 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The lower trigram is Zhen. Concealing the toes prevents its daring to move like Zhen, for such movement would advance into wrongdoing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:016 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Biting through soft flesh, nose concealed”: riding upon firmness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Biting deeply enough to conceal the nose comes from riding upon firmness. This means applying punishment to a strong, resistant person, which must be penetrating and severe. Such severity is fitting and thus is what is called the mean.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:019 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Riding upon firmness explains biting through flesh and concealing the nose. Because it rides upon firmness, its use of punishment is deep.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Encountering poison”: the position is inappropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A six in the third is yin in yang, inappropriately placed. Because its own situation is improper, the punished person is hard to subdue and turns poison against it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:022 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Here too, inappropriate line position illuminates the difficulty of its actual situation. It does not mean that its conduct is improper. Improper conduct in administering punishment would be a great failure. How could there be no blame, or only a little humiliation?[^j11-biting-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:023 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Difficulty and steadfastness are beneficial; good fortune”: not yet radiant.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Not yet radiant means that its Way has not become radiant and great. The warning to value difficulty and steadfastness addresses its deficiency: it has not attained centeredness and correctness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:026 -->

**Fang Yingxiang:** Concern that the judge's heart may not yet be clear prompts the warning to value difficulty and steadfastness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:027 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Steadfastness amid danger; no blame”: attaining what is fitting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It can be without blame because its actions are fitting. This means occupying the center, employing firmness, preserving correctness, and considering danger.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:030 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** The Judgment Commentary calls its position inappropriate; here it attains what is fitting. There, position is at issue; here, action. The fifth six administers justice through yieldingness, acting with correctness and apprehension. It is without blame because it finds the fitting way to handle cases.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:031 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Attaining what is fitting means finding the proper Way of punishment, not a statement about line position. If central position were intended, it would explain obtaining yellow metal, not steadfastness amid danger and freedom from blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:032 -->

### Top Nine: “Wearing the cangue, ears concealed”: hearing lacks discernment.[^j11-biting-ears]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:033 -->

**Original Meaning.** Concealing the ears punishes the failure to listen discerningly. Careful listening and early consideration would prevent this misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Deaf in understanding and unable to awaken, a person accumulates offenses to the utmost. The ancients prescribed the cangue for great offenses because failure to hear and understand allowed evil to accumulate. The cangue covers and injures the ears, warning against undiscerning hearing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:036 -->

**Hu Bingwen:** The upper trigram is Li. Concealed ears mean that one has failed to be clear like Li.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4660:037 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Used alone, discerning hearing includes clarity. When the two are named together, hearing is the underlying capacity and clarity its activity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4660:037 -->

<a id="eee-4661"></a>

## 22. Bi 賁 — Adornment

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4661 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:001 -->

### Great Image: Fire beneath the mountain: Adornment. Thus the noble person brings clarity to the many affairs of government but does not dare decide criminal cases.[^j11-adornment-judging]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Fire beneath the mountain does not illuminate far. Clarifying governmental affairs is a lesser undertaking; deciding cases a greater one. Li's clarity is within and Gen's restraint outside, hence this image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Mountains are where grasses, trees, and the many kinds of things gather and grow. Fire below shines upward, bathing them in light: an image of adornment. The noble person observes this illumination to cultivate and clarify the affairs of government and bring about cultivated, enlightened rule. Yet he does not dare rush to decide cases. Adjudication demands a ruler's utmost caution. How could he rely upon his clarity and lightly trust his own judgment? Such is the sage's heart, and profound is the warning. The image takes fire beneath the mountain illuminating things and warns about the use of clarity. Adornment itself also contains the meaning of not daring to decide cases. Cases depend entirely upon the true circumstances. Embellishment obscures those circumstances, so one must not dare use ornamented argument to decide them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** In Adornment's time, one regulates things through cultivated clarity, not punitive force. Thus the noble person clarifies governmental affairs but does not dare decide cases.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:006 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “Clarifying governmental affairs without daring to decide cases?” Answer: “This and The Wanderer both concern justice. Their difference is simply whether Gen and Li are within or outside, so their explanations are opposite. Restraint outside and clarity within clarify government without daring to decide cases. Restraint within and clarity outside apply punishments clearly and cautiously without leaving cases unresolved. In present prefectural and county courts, detention, investigation, examination, and review have their prescribed stages. Going beyond these without deciding is leaving a case unresolved; deciding before completing them is daring to adjudicate prematurely. The Documents allows ten days or a season for examining prisoners: the process must take a certain time. Its intention agrees with the Offices of Zhou's Autumn Offices.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:007 -->

**Cai Yuan:** A mountain's materials illuminated by fire display their brilliance outwardly: the image of adornment. Clarifying the many affairs of government images Li's clarity. Governmental arrangements are instruments of order and properly receive cultivation and refinement. Not daring to decide cases images Gen's restraint. Adjudication prizes actual circumstances; adornment would obscure them.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:008 -->

**He Kai:** The Punishments of Lü says: “Not clever argument but goodness decides cases.” Rely upon sharp discernment and embellish arguments until the facts are concealed, and people will harbor unredressed wrongs.
