Thus harsh legal scrutiny is called deep textual elaboration, manufacturing a case textual contrivance, law a web of writing, and manipulating law dancing with words. Judicial injustice has always arisen from such textual manipulation. All this is the error of a presumptuous heart.[^j11-adornment-legal-words]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:009 -->

### First Nine: “Leaving the carriage and walking”: rightness does not permit riding.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:010 -->

**Original Meaning.** The noble person's acceptance and rejection are decided by rightness alone.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Leaving the carriage to walk means that rightness forbids riding. The first's response to the fourth is correct; following the second is not. Abandoning the ease of the nearby second to follow the distant fourth's difficulty is leaving the carriage and walking. The noble person's adornment consists simply in preserving rightness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Adorning the beard”: rising together with what is above.[^j11-adornment-beard]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The beard images rising together with what is above. It moves with the upper part; movement and rest depend entirely upon its support. Similarly, ornament is added according to a thing's substance, and its goodness or badness rests in that substance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:015 -->

**Hou Xingguo:** From the third to the top is an image of jaws. The second lies beneath them, imaging the beard. It has no correspondent above, and the third likewise has none. If it can support the third above and share its virtue, the two, though without correspondents, can rise together.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:016 -->

**Yuan Shu:** Yin cannot illuminate itself; only with yang does it become bright. Yieldingness cannot stand by itself; only with firmness does it stand. The lower cannot raise itself; only with the upper does it rise.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The good fortune of enduring steadfastness: ultimately none can overbear it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Adornment without constancy or correctness invites contempt and encroachment. Hence the warning that enduring correctness brings good fortune. With adornment both constant and correct, who could overbear it?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:020 -->

**Cai Yuan:** Overbearing means insulting. If the third remains enduringly steadfast, the two yielding lines may draw close and moisten it, yet ultimately cannot overbear it or cause its immersion in indulgence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:021 -->

**Shen Yiguan:** The three lower lines all take Li's meaning. At the third, cultivated clarity reaches its utmost and may submerge substance. Enduring steadfastness supplies Gen's restraint; hence good fortune and freedom from encroachment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:022 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: Its position gives rise to doubt. “Not raiders, but seeking marriage”: ultimately no grievance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** Its position gives rise to doubt means that the place it occupies is doubtful. Ultimately no grievance means that, if it preserves correctness and does not join the intruder, there will be no other trouble.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fourth and first are far apart, with the third between them: their position gives rise to doubt. Though this hostile third separates them and they cannot yet unite in marriage, they properly correspond, with principle and rightness on their side. They must ultimately unite, hence no grievance. Grievance means resentment. Ultimately adorning one another, they have no resentment or reproach.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:026 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** Pure whiteness without falsity—who could come between them? Initial doubt ends in union; hence no grievance in the end.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:027 -->

**Guo Yong:** Though the fourth adorns itself, it also possesses a plain, white substance, like adornment in hills and gardens: an image of emptying oneself to await others. The first adorns its toes and comes, like the winged horse. Firmness lowers itself before yieldingness and comes to respond: not raiders, but marriage. Though the fourth has doubts, what grievance could ultimately remain?[^j11-adornment-white-horse]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:028 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The fifth six's good fortune: there is happiness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Able to follow another and accomplish adornment, it enjoys auspicious excellence: this is happiness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:030 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:031 -->

**Fang Yingxiang:** In a time when ornament prevails, adornment in hills and gardens is surely delightful. It is not personal delight alone, but delight for the world's Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:032 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Commentary often speaks of blessing in the fifth position. Blessing is great, happiness smaller. This line is honored yet returns to simplicity and esteems frugality, which can also change customs. Its mere mention of happiness concerns, for now, freedom from personal fault. As in Without Falsity's fifth, Decrease's fourth, and Joy's fourth, freedom from ailment constitutes happiness. Extend its application, and it transforms and completes the world: blessing is contained within it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:033 -->

### Top Nine: “White adornment; no blame”: at the top, the purpose is attained.[^j11-adornment-white]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:034 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** White adornment is without blame because the top attains its purpose. The top nine attains its purpose by standing above and adorning yieldingness, completing adornment's work. The ruler at the fifth six also receives its adornment. Thus, though without an official position, it actually presides over the work and attains its purpose, unlike extremes in other figures. Already above and successful at adornment's limit, it risks ornamental falsity and loss of substance. Hence the warning that plain substance brings no blame. Adornment must not be excessive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:035 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:036 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “What is attaining the purpose?” Answer: “At the top of the figure, outside affairs, one does not borrow embellishment but possesses a natural pattern. This is unhurried ease and self-possession.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:037 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second six's yieldingness comes to adorn firmness and governs the inner trigram's pattern. There, ornament serves as ornament; hence adorning the beard. A beard attached to the body is the most unsubstantial ornament. Yet without flourishing yang energy, the beard cannot be adorned, so it rises together with what is above. The second and top exchange to form the figure, and the second takes the top as its governor, as a beard takes yang as its governor. This profoundly shows that pattern and substance are never separate. Thus no good fortune or misfortune is stated: both depend on substance. The top nine is firmness apportioned upward to adorn yieldingness, governing the outer trigram's pattern. There, substance serves as ornament; hence white adornment. White is not ordinarily what produces ornament. Yet ornament must begin in substance, so white precedes the many colors; at ornament's utmost, it must end in substance, so white also follows them. White therefore completes adornment's ending and establishes its beginning. Hence the top attains its purpose. At the figure's end, governing adornment's completion, it realizes its purpose of earnest substance. This profoundly shows that substance and pattern do not conflict. Thus no blame is stated: conduct contrary to the time might otherwise seem blameworthy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4661:038 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Xiang takes rising together with above to refer to the top nine. Referring it to the third nine is more appropriate.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4661:038 -->

<a id="eee-4662"></a>

## 23. Bo 剝 — Splitting Apart

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4662 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:001 -->

### Great Image: The mountain clings to the earth: Splitting Apart. Thus those above strengthen those below to secure their own dwelling.[^j11-splitting-foundation]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:002 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Gen doubled above Kun is a mountain clinging to the earth. A mountain rises high from the ground, yet here it falls back against it: an image of crumbling. Those above means rulers and all who stand over others. They observe splitting apart and strengthen the foundation below to secure their dwelling. The lower is the upper's root. Nothing with a secure foundation can be stripped away. Thus the collapse above must begin below; when the lower is stripped away, the upper is endangered. Knowing this, those above nourish and settle the people, making their root substantial and thereby securing their own abode. The Documents says: “The people are the state's root. With the root firm, the state is secure.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:003 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:004 -->

**Yu Fan:** A mountain stands high above the earth. Its now clinging to the earth shows that it has been stripped away. A ruler must give generously to those below before his dwelling can be secure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:005 -->

**Liu Mu:** A mountain takes earth as its foundation. Thicken the earth, and the mountain preserves its height. A ruler takes the people as his root. Strengthen those below, and he is secure above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:006 -->

**Sima Guang:** A thin foundation makes a wall fall; weakness below endangers what is above. Thus the noble person strengthens those below to secure his own dwelling.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:007 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Precisely because the earth is deep, the mountain rests securely without shaking. A ruler who strengthens those below and wins the people likewise has an unshaken position: the root firm, the state secure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:008 -->

### First Six: “Stripping the bed at its feet”: destroying what is below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The bed's feet supply the image because yin encroaches upon yang from below. Destroying means submerging: the correct Way is eroded from below upward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:011 -->

**Yu Fan:** The bed gives a person rest. At the bottom, its feet are named. Stripping begins below and gradually reaches above; hence destroying what is below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:012 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Stripping the bed at its frame”: not yet having an ally.[^j11-splitting-ally]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:013 -->

**Original Meaning.** This says that the stripping is not yet overwhelmingly strong.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yin's encroachment upon yang grows until the bed's frame is stripped because yang does not yet have a responding ally. When petty people attack a noble person, allies would enable him to defeat them and prevent harm. Without allies, he is worn away and meets misfortune. Amid decline, how could he preserve himself without companions? “Not yet having an ally” also means that stripping is not yet overwhelming: with allies, it could still be defeated. The intention shown to people is profound.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:016 -->

**Cui Jing:** The frame lies between the bed's surface and feet; it is its supporting rail. “Not yet having an ally” means that only at the third is there a correspondent. Thus the second does not yet have one.[^j11-splitting-frame]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:017 -->

**Wu Cheng:** The third six's stripping away is different precisely because it has an ally.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:018 -->

**Gong Huan:** The second six is yielding yin, centered and correct. If it had a yang-firm partner above, it would certainly respond and assist rather than strip away. Without one, it mixes among the yin and takes part in stripping. The third has an ally, so, though less centered and correct than the second, it is without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:019 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Cui, Wu, and Gong all grasp the wording. The third six lacks centeredness and correctness yet receives a better statement than the second. Thus the sage explains through “not yet having an ally” and “losing above and below.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Stripping away; no blame”: losing above and below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** Above and below mean the other four yin.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The third is without blame amid stripping because its situation differs from the yin above and below. It parts from its own kind, becoming blameless in handling Splitting Apart, like Lü Qiang of the Eastern Han.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:024 -->

**Wang Bi:** Two yin lie above the third and two below, while it alone responds to yang. Thus it loses above and below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:025 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Above means the fourth and fifth; below the first and second. Departing from these four yin and following firmness alone is losing above and below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:026 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Stripping the bed to the skin”: calamity is close at hand.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth is the ruler's place. Stripping has reached the fourth; in a person, it has reached the skin. Once skin is stripped, the person stands at the edge of destruction. Disaster is close at hand.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:028 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Favor through the palace women”: ultimately no fault.[^j11-splitting-palace]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:029 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The gathered yin have stripped yang nearly to the limit. If the fifth six can lead them in an orderly row, turning instead toward favor from yang, there will ultimately be no fault. To disclose this meaning again near the end of stripping expresses the sage's most earnest encouragement to turn toward goodness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:030 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Yin in the honored fifth gives the image of a queen or consort. Leading a string of fish and gaining favor through the palace women would leave no fault of jealousy, injury, or disorder that strips away the ruler.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:031 -->

### Top Nine: “The noble person gains a carriage”: borne by the people. “The petty person strips away his shelter”: ultimately unusable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When the correct Way has been stripped to its utmost, people again long for order. Thus the yang-firm noble person is borne by the people. A petty person at stripping's utmost merely reaches his own exhaustion and is ultimately unusable. This does not identify the nine as a petty person; it says what happens to petty people in that time.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4662:034 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Only noble people can shelter petty people; petty people must depend upon them for preservation. If they strip the noble people away, those people perish and the petty people have nowhere to shelter, like stripping away their own roofs. Look at those who have tried to destroy noble people through history. Once they had destroyed them all, the state fell and their households perished. Did the petty people themselves survive? Hence the sage says: “The noble person gains a carriage: borne by the people. The petty person strips away his shelter: ultimately unusable.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4662:034 -->

<a id="eee-4663"></a>

## 24. Fu 復 — Return

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4663 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder within the earth: Return. Thus the former kings closed the passes on the solstice; merchants and travelers did not journey, and the ruler did not tour the regions.[^j11-return-solstice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Quiet repose nourishes the slight yang. The Monthly Ordinances prescribes purification and abstinence in this month, withdrawing the person to await the settling of yin and yang.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder is sound produced by yin and yang pressing upon one another. While yang is slight, it cannot yet issue forth. Thunder within the earth is the time of yang's first return. Born below and still very slight, yang grows only through quiet repose. The former kings followed Heaven's Way: on the solstice, when yang first arises, they nurtured it through stillness. Thus the passes were closed, merchants and travelers prevented from journeying, and the ruler refrained from inspecting the regions. They observed Return to accord with Heaven. The same applies to one's person: quiet repose should nurture its yang.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:005 -->

**Liu Tui:** Thunder within the earth rumbles deeply. Yang comes and returns: with its return, will not the world become luminous and harmonious?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:006 -->

**Su Shunqin:** “In Return, do we not see Heaven and Earth's heart?” Wang Bi explains: “Return means returning to the root. Heaven and Earth take the root as their heart. Silent, utterly without being: this is their root. Thus when movement rests within the earth, Heaven and Earth's heart appears.” I find this doubtful. Return takes its name from one yang first arising. The Commentary says firmness returns and firmness grows. How could this mean silence and utter nonbeing, or the cessation of movement? The Image says thunder within the earth. Thunder is yang and movement. Its being within the earth therefore images yang stirring. Fusi, failing to apprehend the figure's structure, takes Return as silence and utter nonbeing. This loses its meaning. He also says winter solstice is yin's return and summer solstice yang's return. How can winter and summer, yin and yang, be so confused?[^j11-return-wang-bi]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:007 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Question: “When yang first arises it is slight, and grows through quiet repose. Hence Return's Image says that the former kings closed the passes on the solstice. The beginnings of goodness in a person are likewise slight and must be reverently nurtured before growing. Otherwise they are lost again.” Answer: “Yes.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:008 -->

**Question:** “The month of pure Kun may be called utmost stillness. Yesterday's stillness nurtures today's movement: one yang's return is nurtured out of pure yin. In a person, making stillness primary precedes the return of goodness.” **Answer:** “That meaning is indeed present, but it is not this figure's principal meaning. The Great Image's closing the passes on the solstice means that, after movement has already begun, quiet is needed to nurture it.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:009 -->

**Yang Qixin:** Closing the passes uses stillness to nourish yang; issuing commands uses movement to regulate yin. The ruler's intention in Return and Coming to Meet is profound.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:010 -->

### First Nine: “Returning from no great distance”: cultivating oneself.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Returning before going far is the noble person's Way of self-cultivation. Learning has no other essential method: recognize what is not good, promptly change it, and follow goodness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:013 -->

**Wang Bi:** Returning swiftly, without going far, is possible because one can cultivate and correct oneself, changing whenever a fault occurs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: The good fortune of beautiful return: humbling oneself before benevolence.[^j11-return-benevolence]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its return is beautiful and auspicious because it can humble itself before benevolence. Benevolence is the world's impartial principle and the root of goodness. The first returns to benevolence, and the second can draw near and place itself below it. Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:017 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Yang practices benevolence. One who stands above it yet attaches to and follows it lowers oneself before benevolence. Hence good fortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:018 -->

**Zhang Shi:** The Changes' 384 lines do not mention benevolence. Here alone it appears, with a profound intention. Overcoming oneself and returning to ritual is benevolence: overcome the private heart and return to heavenly principle. The second is not far from the first and has no binding correspondent above; it can follow the first in returning, hence lowering itself before benevolence. At the fourth, the text says only following the Way, not benevolence. The Way names the general principle; benevolence is more intimately pertinent.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:019 -->

**Yu Yan:** Benevolence is the heart's virtue and goodness's root. The first nine cultivates itself, returns to the root, and recovers goodness: it may be called benevolent. The second's good fortune comes from drawing near to that first nine.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: The danger of repeated return: in rightness, no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Repeatedly returning and repeatedly losing the way are dangerous. Yet the rightness of returning to goodness is without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:022 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “Walking among others, returning alone”: following the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its solitary return is praised because it follows the good Way of the yang-firm noble person.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:024 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:025 -->

**Guo Yong:** Splitting Apart's third six becomes Return's fourth six when the figure is reversed, and their meanings resemble one another. There, it loses above and below to respond to yang; here, it returns alone to follow the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:026 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: “Generous return; no regret”: completing oneself through the mean.[^j11-return-examining]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** “Completing” means bringing to fulfillment.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** One completes oneself through the central Way. Yin at the honored fifth occupies the center and belongs to compliance. If it can make its purpose generous and earnest, completing itself through the mean, it can be without regret. Completing oneself means completing its centered, compliant virtue.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:030 -->

**Wang Anshi:** Able to examine oneself through the central Way, one's movements do not depart from the center.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:031 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** The second and fourth depend upon the first to return, hence lowering oneself before benevolence and following the Way. The fifth returns without waiting upon the first, hence self-completion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:032 -->

**Li Jian:** Without a nature originally endowed with the mean, one could not complete this virtue through the center.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:033 -->

**Liang Yin:** Examining oneself through the mean means that centered virtue enables one to examine one's goodness and its absence.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:034 -->

### Top Six: The misfortune of losing the way in return: contrary to the ruler's Way.[^j11-return-heart-ruler]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:035 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Return accords with the Way; being lost in returning opposes it, and misfortune is evident. The line's misfortune for the ruler of the state means opposing the ruler's Way. A ruler above, governing the multitude, should follow the world's goodness. To be lost in returning is contrary to that duty. Nor does this concern rulers alone: anyone lost in returning opposes the Way and meets misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:036 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4663:037 -->

**Yang Qixin:** The heart is the heavenly ruler. The ruler should direct the many movements, yet instead is directed by them: this runs contrary to the heart's Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4663:037 -->

<a id="eee-4664"></a>

## 25. Wuwang 无妄 — Without Falsity

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4664 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder travels beneath Heaven; all things receive freedom from falsity. Thus the former kings, fully meeting the seasons, nurtured the myriad things.[^j11-without-falsity-nature]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Thunder beneath Heaven stirs things into life, and each receives its proper nature and allotment. This is granting freedom from falsity to every thing. The former kings modeled it, meeting the seasons and nurturing things according to their natures, without private contrivance.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Thunder travels beneath Heaven. Yin and yang harmonize and press upon one another to make sound, awakening what hibernates, stirring shoots, and bringing the myriad things to life. Great and small, high and low, all receive their correct natures and allotments without deviation or falsity. This is all things receiving freedom from falsity. The former kings observed this image of thunder generating and bestowing, and fully met Heaven's seasons to nurture things, letting each attain what is fitting, as Heaven grants freedom from falsity. “Fully” means abundantly, like expressions for flourishing activity or prolonged utterance. Meeting the seasons means according with Heaven's times. Heaven gives life to all things, each attaining its nature and allotment without falsity. The king embodies this Way, nurturing people and extending care to insects, grasses, and trees, letting each attain what is fitting. This is meeting the seasons to nurture things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:005 -->

**The Changes of the Nine Schools:** Thunder beneath Heaven spreads yang energy everywhere, leaving nothing without its share. Hence all things receive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:006 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Asked about the differing explanations of all things receiving freedom from falsity, Wenwei said: “It means each attaining its correct nature and allotment.” Zhu answered: “Yes. Each thing is granted its own freedom from falsity.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:007 -->

**Yu Yan:** Heaven has its times. The former kings could not hasten or delay them, but simply meet and follow them. Things have their lives. The former kings could not diminish or augment these, but nurture and complete them. What the Doctrine of the Mean calls sincerity is what the Changes calls freedom from falsity. The Doctrine of the Mean says: “Only utmost sincerity can fully realize one's nature. Realizing one's nature, one can realize other people's natures; realizing theirs, one can realize the natures of things; realizing those, one can assist Heaven and Earth's transforming nurture; assisting that nurture, one can stand as a third with Heaven and Earth.” Zisi's teaching is rooted here.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:008 -->

**Cai Qing:** All things receiving freedom from falsity means each attaining its correct nature and allotment. Meeting the seasons and nurturing things follow their natures without private intervention: the sage fully realizes the natures of things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:009 -->

### First Nine: “Going without falsity”: attaining the purpose.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going without falsity always attains its purpose. Sincerity can move all things. Applied to self-cultivation, it rectifies the person; to affairs, it brings them into principle; to attending upon people, it moves and transforms them. Wherever one goes, purpose is attained.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:011 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Not plowing for the harvest”: not for wealth.[^j11-without-falsity-harvest]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:012 -->

**Original Meaning.** Wealth has the sense it has in “not to enrich oneself with the world”: one does not calculate profit before acting.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:013 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** “Not” denies a necessary intention, as in Approach's “not obeying the command.” Not plowing for the harvest and not breaking ground for established fields follow what the affair naturally requires. Once plowed, there must be a harvest; once broken, ground must become established fields. Yet one need not act for their wealth. To begin plowing or breaking ground with the heart already set on harvest and established fields is to act for wealth. Action motivated by desire is falsity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:015 -->

**Feng Yinchu:** “Not yet” here means not. Wealth means profit. While laboring at plowing, one does not immediately entertain a heart expecting the harvest. This is the humane person's refusal to calculate achievement and scheme for gain, leaving heavenly virtue whole. Thus going is beneficial.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:016 -->

### Six in the Third Place: The traveler gains the ox; the townspeople suffer calamity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:017 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The traveler's gain is the townspeople's calamity. With gain comes loss. How could this be sufficient to count as gain?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:018 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:019 -->

**Feng Yinchu:** The townspeople's calamity is what is called an undeserved calamity. Someone falsely accused without cause can examine himself without shame. The noble person seeks only freedom from falsity; misfortune and blessing are left to Heaven and set outside his calculations.[^j11-without-falsity-calamity]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:020 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “One may be steadfast; no blame”: firmly possessing it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** Possessing here means preserving.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Preserve it steadfastly and firmly, and there is no blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:024 -->

**Su Shi:** Firmly possessing it means having it as one's nature from birth, not seizing and acquiring it from outside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:025 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Correctness is human nature, not something melted into me from outside. It is originally mine. Follow what is originally possessed and do not lose it; hence one may be steadfast without blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:026 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: Medicine for what is without falsity must not be tried.[^j11-without-falsity-medicine]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:027 -->

**Original Meaning.** Already free of falsity, to medicate it further would instead introduce falsity and produce illness. Trying means tasting even a little.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:028 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** When a person has falsity, correction is necessary. Already without falsity, applying medicine to cure it instead becomes falsity. Could it be used? Hence it must not be tried. Trying means temporary use, like tasting a little.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:029 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:030 -->

**Lin Xiyuan:** Already without falsity, applying medicine would try a medicine against an illness arising without falsity, thereby introducing falsity and producing illness. If one's conduct already accords with principle, how could an unforeseen event justify changing one's plans?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:031 -->

**Qian Zhili:** The fifth nine is yang-firm, centered, and correct. It has no course tending toward illness, yet illness occurs: an illness without falsity. Preserve correctness and rest in constancy, and the illness will depart of itself. To try medicine would deem one's ordinary course wrong and reverse it completely, bringing a succession of new illnesses. Hence medicine for what is without falsity must not be tried.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:032 -->

### Top Nine: Going without falsity meets the calamity of exhaustion.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:033 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Freedom from falsity has reached its utmost. Further advancement becomes falsity, bringing calamity through extremity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:034 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:035 -->

**Zhao Yuquan:** Going without falsity should be free of calamity. Yet at the time's exhaustion, one possesses the virtue without possessing the favorable time. Thus calamity occurs.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4664:036 -->

**He Kai:** Going without falsity is like the Judgment Commentary's proceeding without falsity. The top nine is Qian exhausted, like its overreaching dragon. Thus the two Small Images have the same meaning. Because intention insists upon action, it is called fault; because time and position cause it, calamity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4664:036 -->

<a id="eee-4665"></a>

## 26. Daxu 大畜 — Great Restraint

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4665 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:001 -->

### Great Image: Heaven within the mountain: Great Restraint. Thus the noble person learns much of former words and past deeds, thereby accumulating his virtue.[^j11-great-restraint-learning]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Heaven within the mountain need not be an actual occurrence. The statement speaks simply through the image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Heaven is supremely great, yet lies within the mountain: an image of supremely great accumulation. The noble person observes it to enlarge what he stores within. This accumulation grows through learning: hearing widely the words and deeds of former sages and worthies, examining their traces to understand their application, and studying their words to seek their hearts. Recognizing and attaining these, he accumulates and completes his virtue. This is Great Restraint's meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:005 -->

**Yang Shi:** The noble person's wide knowledge of former words and deeds does not merely furnish information; it accumulates virtue. Accumulate virtue, and what is accumulated is great. Scholars who boast of quantity and vie in ornamental display merely furnish their knowledge. What use is such learning?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:006 -->

**Qiu Fuguo:** Great Restraint speaks of accumulating virtue; Small Restraint of refining cultivated virtue. Both concern virtue, but cultivated accomplishment is its smaller aspect.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:007 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** Heaven within the mountain means storing its energy. Thunder, rain, clouds, and wind within mountains are all Heaven.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:008 -->

### First Nine: “Danger is present; stopping is beneficial”: not venturing into calamity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Where danger exists, one should stop rather than advance into calamity. Going forward without assessing circumstances must bring disaster.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:010 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “The wagon's axle fastenings are loosened”: centeredness brings no fault.[^j11-great-restraint-wagon]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The wagon is unfastened and does not proceed because the line occupies the central Way. Its movement does not miss what is fitting, so there is no fault. Nothing is better than firm centeredness. Yielding centeredness merely avoids excessive yieldingness; firm centeredness joins the mean with ability. The first nine lacks the center and is therefore warned to stop when danger exists. The second is centered, so its advancing and stopping naturally avoid error. Thus the text says only that the wagon is unfastened, meaning it can refrain from proceeding. Refraining brings no fault. The first and second possess Qian's firm vigor yet cannot advance; the fourth and fifth are yielding yin yet can restrain them. Students of the Changes should deeply understand time's flourishing and decline and circumstances' strength and weakness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:013 -->

**Lü Zuqian:** Firm and centered, the second assesses what is fitting. Seeing that action is impossible, it loosens its own wagon's fastenings and does not proceed. Hence centeredness brings no fault.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:014 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “It is beneficial to have somewhere to go”: joining purpose with above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:015 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going is beneficial because purpose is shared with what is above. The top nine's yang nature advances upward, and restraint has reached its utmost. Thus it does not restrain the third below, but joins its purpose in advancing upward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:016 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:017 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** In other hexagrams, yin and yang correspondence is an attainment; here it is restraint. In other hexagrams, opposing yin or yang do not join; here they unite.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:018 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The fourth six's supreme good fortune: there is happiness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:019 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Restrain evil only after it becomes strong, and those above labor at prohibitions while those below suffer punishment. Restrain it while still slight, before it develops, and there is great goodness and good fortune: neither toil nor injury, and therefore happiness. This is the fourth restraining the first. Restraint from above works in the same way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:020 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The fifth six's good fortune: there is blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** If those above do not know how to restrain evil, but use severe punishments to oppose people's desires, injury is great and achievement absent. Know its root and regulate it through the Way, and customs change without toil or injury. This is blessing for the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:022 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:023 -->

**Lü Dalin:** Both the fourth and fifth six restrain firmness through yieldingness and check vigor. An ox's vigor lies in its horns; a boar's in its tusks. The first nine is at vigor's beginning, not yet manifest, like a young ox. Restrain it before expression with a horn-board, and as it grows it has no opportunity to employ its violence. It becomes not merely harmless but content in tractability, fit for harness and service. Hence happiness. The second nine is at vigor's center, its strength already developed, like a boar whose tusks are becoming uncontrollable. The fifth six is honored and holds the center, using yielding methods to diminish violent energy, like a castrated boar. Though its tusks remain strong, it cannot become violent and may be kept without concern. Hence blessing.[^j11-great-restraint-animals]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:024 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Happiness concerns oneself; blessing extends happiness to others. The fifth occupies the ruler's place and therefore reaches others. Yet in restraining things, control at the beginning is greatest goodness. Thus the fourth has supreme good fortune, while the fifth has good fortune alone.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:025 -->

**Cai Qing:** The fifth lacks the fourth's ease because their times differ. The fourth lacks the fifth's breadth of benefit because their positions differ.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:026 -->

### Top Nine: “What a highway of Heaven!” The Way proceeds greatly.[^j11-great-restraint-highway]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:027 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Why is it called a highway of Heaven? Because nothing blocks it: the road opens into great passage. A highway of Heaven is an unusual expression, so the Image expressly asks what it means. The road's great openness supplies the image of spacious freedom. Because the Image contains “why,” that word was mistakenly added to the line statement as well.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:028 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:029 -->

**You Zuo:** When accumulation's Way is complete, the road for the worthy reaches in all directions from oneself. Hence “What a highway of Heaven—success!” The Judgment says firmness rises and the worthy are honored, so Great Restraint centers on the top nine. Raising outstanding people into the many offices, opening the path of the worthy, and ensuring that no worthy person remains eating at home are its responsibilities. When no worthy person is left without employment, what greater manifestation could there be of the Way's broad activity?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:030 -->

**Shen Gai:** The highway of Heaven means honoring the worthy. In Great Restraint, the top alone stands above, honored by the yielding fifth. Accumulating abundant virtue in the highest place, it gathers the worthy at the royal court and takes the highway's free passage as its responsibility. At this point the worthy person's road is unobstructed and the Way flourishes. Hence the Way proceeds greatly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:031 -->

**Lü Zuqian:** Accumulation at its utmost disperses. Yi Yin delighted in the Way of Yao and Shun while dwelling among the fields: his accumulation was great. He had to assist Tang to bring forth what he stored within. Attaining the time was like reaching Heaven's highway. Thus the Way proceeds: one finds the time and puts the Way into action.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4665:032 -->

**He Kai:** Fully present in oneself, it is virtue; reaching the age, it is the Way. When the Way can proceed widely, success is evident. This is the Judgment's good fortune in not eating at home and benefit in crossing the great river.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4665:032 -->

<a id="eee-4666"></a>

## 27. Yi 頤 — Nourishment

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4666 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:001 -->

### Great Image: Thunder beneath the mountain: Nourishment. Thus the noble person is careful in speech and measured in food and drink.[^j11-nourishment-speech]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** These are the urgent tasks of nurturing virtue and nourishing the person.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** In terms of the two trigrams, thunder lies beneath the mountain. Its shaking stirs the roots of everything growing there and brings forth their shoots: an image of nourishment. In terms of upper and lower, Gen stops and Zhen moves: stillness above and movement below image the jaws. In terms of the figure's shape, two yang above and below enclose four yin, solid outside and open within: an image of jaws and mouth. The mouth nourishes the person. Thus the noble person observes it to nourish himself, taking care in speech to nurture virtue and moderating food and drink to sustain the body. Nourishment is not drawn from the mouth alone. Among the nearest affairs with the greatest consequences, none exceed speech and nourishment. In the person, speech is speech; in the world, it includes all commands, policies, and teachings issuing from oneself. Care makes them fitting and free of error. In the person, food and drink nourish; in the world, all goods, resources, and expenditures that support people do so. Measure makes them appropriate and harmless. Extend nourishment's Way to virtue and to the world: the same principle applies throughout.[^j11-nourishment-gen]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:005 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Someone remarked: “The proverb ‘Disaster comes out through the mouth; illness comes in through the mouth’ is excellent.” Zhu replied: “Earlier scholars used it to explain Nourishment's Image: careful speech and measured food and drink.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:006 -->

**Feng Yi:** Model thunder's movement to take care in what goes out; model the mountain's restraint to regulate what comes in.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:007 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Thunder's sound is speech; the mountain's nurture is food and drink. Both pass out through and into the jaws.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:008 -->

**Yu Yan:** Nourishment images the mouth and cheeks, so it speaks of what bears directly upon them: careful speech and measured food and drink. Extend speech's category, and all commands, policies, and teachings issuing from oneself require care and must not go forth wrongly. Extend food and drink's category, and all goods, wealth, and taxes coming to those above require measure and must not be taken wrongly.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:009 -->

### First Nine: “Looking at me, jaws working”: this too is unworthy of esteem.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The nine belongs to movement. Working jaws mean that delight in yin stirs its purpose. Once moved by desire, even firm vigor and discerning ability must ultimately lose themselves; thus the ability is unworthy of esteem. Firmness is valued because it can stand without yielding to desire. Clarity is valued because it illuminates without losing correctness. Confused by desire and deprived of correctness, what firmness or clarity remains? It deserves contempt.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:012 -->

**Yang Jian:** This makes clear that something genuinely honorable was originally present. Now, looking at another with working jaws, it loses what was honorable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:013 -->

**Yu Yan:** Mencius says that nourishing the greater part makes a great person, nourishing the smaller a petty person. He also says that people despise someone given to food and drink. The first nine originally possesses yang virtue's greatness and an estimable substance. Abandoning its own greatness within to gaze upon smallness outside, would it not be despised? Hence this too is unworthy of esteem.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:014 -->

### Six in the Second Place: The second six's misfortune in going forward: acting outside its proper kind.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:015 -->

**Original Meaning.** Neither the first nor the top belongs to its proper kind.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Going to follow the top is inauspicious because it is not a fitting companion. Seeking outwardly while losing one's proper association naturally brings misfortune. Acting means going.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:017 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Do not act for ten years”: the Way is greatly opposed.[^j11-nourishment-steadfastness]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The warning against employing it to the end comes from its course's profound opposition to rightness and principle.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:020 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The words “opposing nourishment's correctness” should be read together. The hexagram statement says Nourishment's steadfast correctness brings good fortune; the third line says opposing nourishment's correctness brings misfortune. This line alone opposes the hexagram's meaning, hence the Way is greatly opposed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:021 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: The good fortune of inverted nourishment: beneficence from above shines forth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Seeking nourishment in an inverted direction is auspicious because yang firmness's response assists its undertaking. Thus one's virtuous beneficence from above shines throughout the world. What good fortune could be greater?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:023 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:024 -->

**Gu Jiajie:** Nourishment reaching below makes beneficence above shine. This is nourishing the worthy to reach the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:025 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The good fortune of abiding in steadfastness: compliantly following above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:026 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** This good fortune means firmly and compliantly following the worthy top nine to nourish the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:027 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:028 -->

**Zhang Qingzi:** The fifth can yield and comply in following the worthy top nine, relying upon it to nourish the world. This truly is the sage's nourishing the worthy to reach all the people.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:029 -->

### Top Nine: “The source of nourishment; danger, good fortune”: great blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The top nine bears such great responsibility and can maintain such vigilance. The world receives its virtuous beneficence: great blessing indeed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:032 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Through Enthusiasm's fourth nine, the world finds delight; hence great attainment. Through Nourishment's top nine, the world receives nurture; hence great blessing.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4666:033 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Both the fifth six and top nine should be understood through the Small Images. The fifth's abiding in steadfastness is not merely preserving itself, but remaining steadfast in following above. Hence its good fortune comes from compliant following. The top's good fortune amid danger is not achieved by itself, but through receiving the fifth's commission. Hence the source of nourishment, with danger and good fortune, brings great blessing.[^j11-nourishment-cooperation]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4666:033 -->

<a id="eee-4667"></a>

## 28. Daguo 大過 — Great Excess

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4667 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:001 -->

### Great Image: The lake submerges the trees: Great Excess. Thus the noble person stands alone without fear and withdraws from the world without distress.[^j11-great-excess-alone]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** The lake submerging the trees is Great Excess's image. Freedom from fear and distress is its conduct.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A lake moistens and nourishes trees, yet here it reaches the point of submerging them: excessive indeed. Hence Great Excess. The noble person observes it to establish conduct far surpassing ordinary people. He surpasses them by standing alone without fear and withdrawing from the world without distress. Though the whole world condemns him, he does not turn aside: standing alone without fear. Though no one in the age recognizes him, he does not regret: withdrawing without distress. Only thus can he preserve himself, and thereby greatly surpass others.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:005 -->

**Liu Mu:** When employed, he stands alone without fear; when set aside, he withdraws without distress.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:006 -->

**Zhao Rumei:** Standing alone resembles Xun's wood; freedom from distress resembles Dui's joy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:007 -->

**Li Jian:** Advancing, the noble person accomplishes great things and may stand alone without fear. Withdrawing into straitened solitude, he remains steadfast without shifting and may leave the world without distress. Both belong to Great Excess.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:008 -->

### First Six: “White couch grass as a mat”: yieldingness below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:009 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yielding yin in a humble place should exceed only in reverence and caution. Yieldingness below images grass laid beneath an object: the Way of reverent care.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:011 -->

**Qian Zhili:** In the figure's image, the first responds to the fourth from below and forms its root. A weak root supported upon grass shows the utmost care in handling the situation. Thus the fourth's ridgepole does not collapse.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:012 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Height rests upon what is low; firmness takes yieldingness as its root. Yieldingness below answers to firmness above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:013 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “An old man and a young wife”: joining beyond ordinary measure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The old man's delight in the young woman and her compliance toward him exceed ordinary measure. The harmony of yin and yang between the second nine and first six goes beyond the usual.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:016 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Though an old man with a young wife exceeds the ordinary, yin and yang join to accomplish generation. Thus nothing is unfavorable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: The misfortune of the sagging ridgepole: it cannot have support.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Excessive strength cannot accept help from others, nor can others approach and assist it. Like a ridgepole bending and breaking, it cannot be propped up. The ridgepole occupies the house's center, where no further support can be added: thus it cannot have support.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:020 -->

**Yang Shi:** A ridgepole stands centrally with other timbers supporting it. The third nine is firmness in a firm place, excessive and uncentered. Such firmness cannot have support: hence the sagging ridgepole.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:021 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The whole figure images a sagging ridgepole, yet the third nine alone receives that image. The whole figure favors going forward, yet the second nine alone receives that benefit. In a time of excessive firmness, the second alone occupies yieldingness and employs the mean. Of the six lines, it alone is without excess; hence nothing unfavorable. The whole figure's center is too strong and its root and tip weak, producing a sag. The third nine is firmness in firmness and, among the six lines, alone represents excess. Thus the ridgepole sags still more and cannot be supported.[^j11-great-excess-lines]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:022 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: The good fortune of the raised ridgepole: not bending downward.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:023 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A raised ridgepole is auspicious because it does not bend toward what is below. This means not becoming attached downward to the first.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:024 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “A withered poplar flowers”—how could this last? “An old woman and a young husband”: this too is shameful.[^j11-great-excess-flowering]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** A withered poplar grows flowers instead of roots and soon withers again. How could it endure? An old woman obtaining a young husband cannot accomplish generation. This too is shameful.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:027 -->

**Su Shi:** A new shoot grows again after collapse, returning to the beginning. Flowering expends everything at fullness, hastening the ending.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:028 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** The second and fifth both lack proper correspondents and go beyond the ordinary to join yin. The second joins the first, which is the root; hence a shoot. A shoot is a new bud growing from a tree's root. Going beyond and producing a fresh shoot contains a principle of successful advance. The fifth joins the top, which is the tip; hence flowers. An already overextended tree producing flowers has no principle of lasting life.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:029 -->

**Wang Shenzi:** Flowers on withered wood hasten its withering. The old woman and young husband exceed the ordinary through delusion by yielding wrongfulness.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:030 -->

**He Kai:** Flourishing has reached its limit and is about to wither, yet produces flowers that further exhaust it. It cannot last. The second is firmness in yieldingness and the first yieldingness in firmness; their excess is not extreme. Moreover, they lie at the figure's beginning, so their extraordinary union can generate life. The fifth is firmness in firmness and the top yieldingness in yieldingness: both excessive. At the figure's end, their yin-yang proximity merely becomes shameful. Such is the contrast.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:031 -->

### Top Six: The misfortune of crossing too far: one cannot blame it.[^j11-great-excess-blame]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:032 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Crossing too far until submerged is one's own doing; one cannot assign blame elsewhere. This means having no one to resent or blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:033 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4667:034 -->

**Su Shi:** Crossing until the head is submerged is an attempt to rescue something. The situation cannot be rescued, and one merely exposes oneself to harm: hence misfortune. Yet in rightness, one cannot be blamed.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4667:034 -->

<a id="eee-4668"></a>

## 29. Kan 習坎 — Repeated Danger

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4668 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:001 -->

### Great Image: Water comes again and again: Repeated Danger. Thus the noble person makes his virtuous conduct constant and repeatedly practices the work of instruction.[^j11-danger-practice]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Governing oneself and governing others both require repeated practice before one becomes familiar with them and rests at ease.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Kan is water, arriving in successive flows. Two Kan trigrams repeated image this successive arrival. Water grows from droplets to depths measured in fathoms and then to rivers and seas, accumulating through repetition rather than suddenly. Following the terrain downward, it is trustworthy and constant. The noble person observes its constancy to make his virtuous conduct endure. Inconstant conduct is false, so it should resemble water's constancy. Observing the repeated reception of one flow after another, he thoroughly practices the work of teaching and commands. Government and instruction must become familiar to people's hearing before they can follow them. Thus commands are given and reiterated many times. Announce them suddenly, before people understand, then immediately demand obedience: even severe punishments cannot compel it. Hence one should model water's repetition.[^j11-danger-noble-person]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:005 -->

**Sima Guang:** Water flows repeatedly without ceasing and becomes a great river. People learn repeatedly without stopping and become great worthies. Thus the noble person makes conduct constant and practices the work of instruction.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:006 -->

**Su Shi:** Affairs that require teaching before one can accomplish them are the work of instruction. In ordinary life, the noble person makes conduct constant, so danger does not change him; he practices what must be learned, so danger finds him able to respond.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:007 -->

**Lu Dian:** Li says brightness arises twice; Kan says water arrives repeatedly. What rises upward is said to arise; what tends downward is said to arrive.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:008 -->

**Wang Zongchuan:** Kan is water's hollow; hence repeated arrival images Repeated Danger. Once the upper hollow fills, water reaches the lower. This is Mencius's filling each hollow before proceeding. Such proceeding never abandons the work of day and night. The noble person's conduct values constancy and instruction values practiced familiarity: this too never abandons the work of day and night.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:009 -->

**Yu Yan:** Constant virtuous conduct means conduct that endures unchanged; practicing instruction means training in it without interruption.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:010 -->

### First Six: “In repeated danger, entering the pit”: losing the Way—misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:011 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** From repeated danger to entering a deeper pit is loss of the Way, hence misfortune. Being able to emerge from danger would preserve the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:012 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:013 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The noble person must handle danger through the correct Way before he can emerge. The first six is incorrect and cannot emerge; it loses the Way and meets misfortune.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:014 -->

**Qian Zhili:** Traversing danger without losing trustworthiness is the Way out. Petty people venturing into danger in the hope of luck meet the misfortune of the first and top six, which lose the Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:015 -->

### Nine in the Second Place: “Seeking a small gain”: not yet out of the center.[^j11-danger-center]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:016 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Trapped between two yin in a dangerous place, its firm centered ability prevents descent into a deeper danger. This is the small gain it seeks, yet it has not emerged from Kan's central danger.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:017 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:018 -->

**Guo Yong:** Once one leaves the center, one loses it. Hence the Image says not yet out of the center.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:019 -->

**Xu Wenzhi:** The noble person is not defeated by danger, not because he can immediately emerge beyond it, but because his heart can rest at ease within it. Someone in danger who thinks only of escaping overnight seeks a great gain. The noble person instead seeks what is small. This is how sincerity gives the heart free passage.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:020 -->

### Six in the Third Place: “Coming or going, pit upon pit”: ultimately no achievement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:021 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Both advance and retreat are dangerous, and remaining is insecure. Employ this course, and one enters still deeper danger. How could achievement follow? Yielding yin without centeredness or correctness would bring regret even on easy ground; how much more in danger! People wish to leave danger, but must find the Way before they can. Seeking escape by losing the Way only increases distress. Thus the sage warns against employing the third's situation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:022 -->

### Six in the Fourth Place: “A jar of wine, two bowls”: firmness and yieldingness meet.[^j11-danger-two-bowls]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:023 -->

**Original Meaning.** Master Chao says that Lu's Textual Explanations lacks the word “two.” That reading is followed here.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:024 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The Image quotes only the opening phrase, as often occurs. A jar of wine and two bowls express utmost plain sincerity. If firmness and yieldingness can meet in this way, freedom from blame may be preserved to the end. What makes the relationship of ruler and minister firm and enduring is sincerity alone. Firmness and yieldingness mean the fourth and fifth, their meeting the encounter of ruler and minister.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:025 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:026 -->

**Wang Bi:** Firmness and yieldingness adjoining and drawing close are what meeting means.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:027 -->

**Jiang Bao:** Confucius's Small Image takes “a jar of wine, two bowls” as a clause. Thus Chao's proposal to join “two” with “use earthenware” is wrong.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:028 -->

### Nine in the Fifth Place: “The pit is not full”: centeredness is not yet great.[^j11-danger-fullness]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:029 -->

**Original Meaning.** It possesses centered virtue, but that virtue is not yet great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The fifth nine has firm centered ability and the honored position. It should relieve the world's difficulties. Yet the pit is still unfilled and the dangers not yet leveled: its firm centered Way has not become radiant and great. In dangerous times, could ruler and minister accomplish the task without joint effort? The fifth's Way is not great because it lacks a minister. A ruler unable to relieve the world's dangers remains insufficiently great and unequal to his position.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:031 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:032 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Water in a hollow can only reach level; it cannot itself rise beyond fullness. Hence not full. Fullness means rising high. Not yet great in the center means that levelness attains the center, while lack of overflowing fullness is not yet great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:033 -->

**Xiang Anshi:** Water flowing without fullness means not stopping. The pit not being full means not yet filled. Not stopping gives trustworthiness; not being filled means centeredness is not yet great. Other things stop when full, but water, once full, flows still more. Thus a hollow has its time of fullness, whereas water never remains full.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:034 -->

**Lu Zhenqi:** Understand the second's small gain, and one understands the fifth's not yet being great.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:035 -->

**Chen Renxi:** Flowing water does not swell to excess; once excessive, it floods outward. The fifth says not full, and the Image says not yet great. Possessing centered virtue, it does not extravagantly magnify itself. Not great explains why it does not overflow.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:036 -->

### Top Six: The top six loses the Way: misfortune for three years.[^j11-danger-three-years]
