<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:037 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Yielding yin places itself at danger's utmost: this loses the Way. Hence misfortune reaches three years. So long without release expresses misfortune to the end. Duration is sometimes expressed as ten and sometimes three, according to the affair. Imprisonment for three years is the utmost of prolonged confinement. Other hexagrams' years likewise suit their particular affairs, as in not rising for three years or pledging oneself after ten.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:038 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4668:039 -->

**Zhu Zhen:** The top six lacks ability to escape danger and occupies its utmost time. Like a person confined in prison, if it sits and examines its faults, even a grave offense permits release within three years. Reckless movement in search of escape brings deeper entrapment. Even after three years, how could it get out?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4668:039 -->

<a id="eee-4669"></a>

## 30. Li 離 — Clinging

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4669 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:001 -->

### Great Image: Brightness arises twice: Clinging. Thus the great person continues the light and illuminates the four quarters.[^j11-clinging-successive-light]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:002 -->

**Original Meaning.** Arising means coming forth.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:003 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Saying two brightnesses would name two lights without revealing succession. Thus it says brightness doubled: brightness repeated, meaning continuation. Brightness doubled makes Li, expressing light continuing light. Zhen and Xun likewise take repetition and following as their meanings, yet Li places special weight upon this. A great person in virtue is a sage; in position, a king. Observing Li's successive brightness, he continues luminous virtue from generation to generation, illuminating the four quarters. Broadly, all light following light is continued brightness. The greatest instance is chosen, so the text speaks of hereditary succession in illumination.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:004 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:005 -->

**Wang Bi:** Continuing means not breaking off. Illumination follows illumination without interruption or vacancy.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:006 -->

**Kong Yingda:** Continuing one's brightness enables illumination of the four quarters. Without continuity, illumination could not endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:007 -->

**Recorded Conversations of Master Zhu.** Brightness arising twice resembles water arriving repeatedly. Today it is bright, and the next day bright again. Brightness directly refers to the sun: simply one brightness arising twice.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:008 -->

**Xu Zaihan:** Continued brightness means no time without clarity; illuminating the four quarters means no place left unilluminated. Because clarity is uninterrupted in time, it reaches everywhere. This is making luminous virtue bright throughout the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:009 -->

### First Nine: Reverence amid tangled steps serves to avoid blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:010 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Steps are entangled and movement is desired, yet one knows to be reverent and cautious and does not dare advance. This seeks to avoid fault. Dwelling in clarity and possessing firmness enable recognition and avoidance. Without them, movement would be reckless.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:011 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:012 -->

**Xu Zaihan:** Reverence straightening the inner self is Kun's virtue. Reverence amid tangled steps embodies it and is what is meant by good fortune in nurturing a cow. Blame recedes without being deliberately pushed away; hence avoiding blame.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:013 -->

### Six in the Second Place: “Yellow clinging; supreme good fortune”: attaining the central Way.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:014 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Supreme good fortune comes from attaining the central Way. Correctness is not separately named because Li gives priority to the center. Cultivated clarity is accomplished through centeredness, and correctness lies within it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:015 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:016 -->

**Guo Zhongxiao:** Clinging succeeds because yieldingness clings to centered correctness. Yellow clinging is supremely auspicious because cultivated clarity employs the mean. Does not the second six alone exhaust the figure's excellence?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:017 -->

### Nine in the Third Place: “Clinging in the declining sun”—how could this last?[^j11-clinging-decline]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:018 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Once the sun declines, can its brightness endure? A clear-sighted person understands this, seeks someone to continue his affairs, and withdraws to rest his person. Resting in the ordinary course and dwelling compliantly, why should there be misfortune?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:019 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The declining sun images darkness in the heart's virtue. Clear virtue continually renews its light; darkened virtue cannot endure.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:020 -->

### Nine in the Fourth Place: “Suddenly, it comes”: nowhere to be received.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:021 -->

**Original Meaning.** Nowhere to be received means burning, dying, and being cast aside.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:022 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** It overbears its ruler above and does not comply with the one it supports. Hated and abandoned by others, it is not accommodated by the world.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:023 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Sudden arrival is what the Documents calls benighted violence. It is not that others refuse it room; it acts as though no place could contain it.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:024 -->

### Six in the Fifth Place: The fifth six's good fortune: clinging to the place of kings and dukes.[^j11-clinging-royal-position]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:025 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** Its good fortune comes from attaching to the proper position of kings and dukes. Possessing authority above, it clearly examines the principles of affairs and maintains that position through apprehension and concern. Thus it can be auspicious. Otherwise, how could it remain secure?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:026 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:027 -->

**Zhao Yansu:** Utmost clarity brings deep concern; deep concern averts calamity. Its meaning attaches to the honored position, hence the statement.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:028 -->

**Cai Qing:** Savor “clinging to kings and dukes,” and one sees that the honored fifth position in the hexagrams need not always mean the Son of Heaven. Lords ruling their individual states can also occupy that meaning.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:029 -->

### Top Nine: “The king employs it in going forth to campaign”: to set the state right.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4669:030 -->

**Cheng's Commentary.** The king employs this top nine's virtue, clear in illumination and firm in decision, to discern and remove the world's evils and thereby set his state in order. This is the Way of firmness and clarity above.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4669:030 -->

<a id="translator-endnotes"></a>

## Translator's Endnotes

The base is [Chinese Juan Eleven](../source/juan-11.md) in the repository, read at commit `35556fbac2fe43f8078756284578f78ac510dfbc`. “Parallel transcription” below means the *Siku Quanshu* text at [Wikisource, Juan Eleven, revision 528597](https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=御纂周易折中_(四庫全書本)/卷11&oldid=528597), consulted selectively on September 26, 2026. This is not a newly constituted critical edition: no systematic comparison of historical page images or independent bilingual review has been performed. A correction described as contextual is a translator's inference, not a reading verified in that parallel transcription. Where another transmission supplies evidence, it is identified separately. The Chinese source remains unchanged.

In the main text, **Original Meaning** identifies Zhu Xi, **Cheng's Commentary** identifies Cheng Yi, and **Editorial Judgment** identifies the imperial compilers. These notes alone are the translator's voice. The added Great Image and line headings aid navigation; the source's order and paragraph divisions remain intact. Historical claims about authorship, social hierarchy, cosmology, punishment, and divination are translated as the speakers' claims, not endorsed as established facts or present-day instructions.

[^j11-layer]: **Which layer is being translated.** 象上傳 contains the Great Images of the thirty Upper Classic hexagrams and their Small, line-by-line Images, followed throughout by the compilation's commentaries. These are not additional oracle statements. Zhu's opening reference to the Duke of Zhou concerns the underlying line statements; it does not assign the Image Commentary itself to him. The source presents 213 primary-text blocks: thirty Great Images, 180 ordinary line Images, the two special “Using” Images, and one additional block because Great Possession's fifth-line Image is divided in two. Traditional attribution to Confucius is retained as the compilation's attribution, not a conclusion independently established here.

[^j11-heaven-motion]: **Heaven's daily circuit.** The commentators explain the doubled Qian through successive daily revolutions of one Heaven, not two physical heavens. Their cosmological account is preserved rather than rewritten into modern astronomy. “Strengthens himself” translates 自強: cultivation of one's capacity and virtue, not coercive strength over others. Their ensuing argument expressly rejects trying to imitate celestial motion by mere incessant activity.

[^j11-unceasing]: **Not ceasing and having no interruption.** 不息 describes refusing to cease; 無息 describes an activity in which no interruption arises. You Zuo and He Kai distinguish sustained cultivation from its unforced fulfillment. The distinction would disappear if both expressions were uniformly rendered “unceasing.” Yanzi here is Yan Hui; his three months without departing from benevolence is the example cited by the commentator, not a measurement introduced by the translator.

[^j11-images-method]: **Images, virtues, and structure.** 卦象 concerns such images as Heaven, water, and mountain; 卦德 concerns capacities such as vigor, compliance, movement, and stopping; 卦體 concerns the figure's constitution and relations among lines. The compilers distinguish the Great Image's interpretation of paired images from the Judgment Commentary's broader methods. Their reconstruction of Fuxi's and King Wen's contributions is explicitly tentative in 疑, “we suspect.” The English preserves that qualification and their claim that the Great Images sometimes establish meanings beyond those of the Judgments.

[^j11-qian-four]: **Permission is not an injunction.** 可以進而不必進 permits advancement without requiring it. Shi Jie further insists that the Image does not resolve the uncertainty expressed by 或, “perhaps.” “Advancing brings no blame” is therefore conditional on the manner and timing of the advance, not an unconditional instruction to proceed. The spatial expression 在淵 is left “in the depths” here, rather than making every commentator commit to a leap out of them.

[^j11-qian-no-head]: **A prohibition against leading, or a cycle without a first point.** Zhu and Cheng read 不可為首 as warning against self-assertive precedence. Gu Jiajie and the compilers instead deny that Heaven's complete, cyclical virtue can have an identifiable first point. The primary translation follows the admonitory construction, while the compilers' “cannot be made first” preserves their explicit rejection of it. Neither account has been made to agree with the other. “Using Nines” is the special statement concerning Qian as a whole, not a seventh ordinary line.

[^j11-earth-depth]: **Depth that can bear things.** 厚 denotes thickness, depth, generosity, and substantial capacity. Here its concrete force matters: layered terrain remains supported because Earth has depth. The ethical image is therefore more demanding than being agreeable or kind; it is the capacity to bear responsibility for beings. “Receptive” in the Great Image renders 坤's force in this context, while the commentaries themselves often explain it as 順, compliance.

[^j11-kun-frost]: **The opening words are disputed within the source.** The base reads 履霜堅冰, literally “treading frost, hard ice.” Zhu proposes 初六履霜, “First Six: treading frost,” on the authority of the *Records of Wei*. His proposal is translated in the commentary, not silently substituted into the primary text. 馴 is then explained through habituation: small beginnings become established through continued acquiescence. This is the commentators' ethical warning, not an assertion that every minor fault inevitably produces disaster.

[^j11-kun-master]: **An attested correction: 王 → 主.** Xiang Anshi's quotation in the base has 後得王而有常. The parallel transcription reads 後得主而有常, hence “following, it finds a master and has constancy.” This also agrees with the phrase quoted in the book's treatment of Kun. The English adopts 主; the Chinese repository file has not been altered. The correction should not be confused with changing the separate occurrences of “king” elsewhere in the passage.

[^j11-contained-brilliance]: **Concealing credit is not withholding service.** Cheng expressly rejects construing 含章 as never acting. One brings forth what rightness requires, while not claiming the accomplishment as one's private possession. Lü Zuqian's large and small vessels explain why genuine breadth of understanding, rather than repeated suppression of visible pride, makes this possible. The political and ethical argument is retained; it is not replaced by a general recommendation to avoid recognition.

[^j11-yellow-garment]: **An inward center made visible.** The source associates yellow with the center and the lower garment with a subordinate position. 文 can mean pattern, cultivated expression, or civil refinement; it does not mean words or literary decoration alone. Gu Jiajie's distinction between 黃中 and 黃裳 is the distinction between centered virtue possessed inwardly and its outward expression, not between two separate virtues.

[^j11-kun-great-ending]: **“Great” is also a line category.** In Zhu's reading, “small” and “great” identify yin and yang, and concluding greatly means that the yin lines change into yang. Cheng's explanation instead emphasizes enduring firmness and a strong completion. The translation preserves both. The technical designation of a line as “small” does not, by itself, establish the personal worth of a woman or a yielding person. The source's own hierarchical applications are left visible wherever it actually makes them.

[^j11-zhun-weaving]: **The silk-working metaphor.** 經綸 joins drawing out or arranging threads with ordering and weaving them together. “Orders and weaves affairs together” keeps the concrete origin audible while allowing the administrative meaning. The several commentators differ over which operation corresponds to thunder and which to cloud; the English does not impose one technical account of weaving upon all of them.

[^j11-zhun-ten]: **Ten years and completion.** Cheng explains ten as the completion of numbers. The text also retains a concrete marriage situation and a delay before reunion with the proper correspondent. The numerical explanation does not justify replacing “ten years” with an exact modern calendar forecast, nor does it require erasing the historical image into an entirely psychological allegory.

[^j11-zhun-deer]: **The forester and the desire for quarry.** 虞 is the guide or forester required for the hunt. It is not translated as intuition or providence. Cai Qing reads 從 as the heart's covetous pursuit, while the compilers discuss the Image's compressed grammar: words such as “exhaustion” and “clarity” can themselves supply a complete explanation. The English gives complete sentences without deleting those grammatical observations.

[^j11-zhun-seeking]: **Who seeks whom?** Cheng takes the fourth to recognize its insufficiency and seek a worthy helper before going forth. Hu Yuan and Yu Yan instead take it to be sought by another and then respond; Jiang Tisheng points to following the fifth. The compilers permit more than one account of the line's relations. “Seeking, then going” leaves the subject open in the primary text so that these are genuine alternatives rather than apparent contradictions created by an over-specific translation.

[^j11-meng-spring]: **Resolute action and a continuing source.** 果行 is decisive or resolute conduct; 育德 is nurturing virtue. Zhen Dexiu explicitly warns that vigorous flow without a deep source runs dry. The argument therefore joins action and sustained cultivation rather than ranking action above stillness. The spring remains a physical image throughout, even where it illuminates an unformed person's education.

[^j11-meng-zhou]: **A compact sentence from Zhou Dunyi.** The quotation can be compared with [*Tongshu*, chapter 40, “Meng and Gen”](https://zh.wikisource.org/wiki/通書). 我正果行 is compressed. The English takes the teacher as setting the pupil right and making conduct resolute; it can also invite emphasis on the teacher's own correct and resolute conduct. No altered Chinese reading is claimed. 筮 is a consultation of the oracle, and 叩神 an appeal to the spirit, not an ordinary question-and-answer lesson with all religious content removed.

[^j11-meng-punishment]: **Historical instruction through penalties.** Cheng defends the establishment of laws and punishments as a form of teaching; Xiang stresses preventing greater harm by correcting small faults. Their argument belongs to the text's historical conception of education and government. It is not advice to punish children physically. 法 includes a normative standard as well as a law, so “establish the standard correctly” retains a wider scope than codifying criminal statutes alone.

[^j11-meng-shun-shen]: **順 or 慎.** Zhu proposes reading “not compliant” as “not careful,” citing an interchange of graphs in a name associated with Xunzi. Xiong Liangfu rejects the change because the Small Images use 順 consistently elsewhere. The primary text retains the base's 順, and both positions remain explicit. Zhu's historical textual argument is being reported; the proposed interchange has not been used as permission to replace 順 indiscriminately throughout the chapter.

[^j11-meng-substance]: **Solid lines and substantial virtue.** 實 means substance or solidity and here also identifies the yang lines capable of enlightening others. The fourth is distant from such lines through both adjacency and correspondence. Zhu's instruction to read 實 in a different tone concerns a traditional rhyme explanation. It is preserved as his phonological claim, not represented as an independently established reconstruction of ancient pronunciation.

[^j11-xu-waiting]: **Waiting after preparation.** The commentators' restful eating and drinking are not a warrant for abandoning work. Gu Jiajie expressly says that prior activity makes waiting possible, just as clouds must first rise before rain can be awaited. Other voices emphasize not forcing what has its own proper time. These qualifications remain attached to the concrete image rather than being converted into a uniform doctrine of either passivity or ceaseless effort.

[^j11-xu-reading]: **An attested correction: 冶 → 治.** Gu Jiajie's base text says 待冶; the parallel transcription has 待治. The English therefore reads “await good order.” This is a correction of the object of waiting, not an expansion invented to soften the passage's apparent inactivity. The same paragraph already requires the noble person to have acted beforehand.

[^j11-xu-added-prognosis]: **A prognostication beyond the prognostication.** 占外之占 is Zhu's description of the Image's additional possibility: reverent caution can avert defeat even after danger has been approached. It does not cancel the original warning about self-incurred raiders. 敬 and 慎 are kept distinct as reverence and caution because Zhu's recorded conversation explicitly distinguishes a sustained orientation from attention to particular risks.

[^j11-xu-position]: **Correctly placed, yet “not appropriately placed.”** The top six is yin in an even position, formally correct. Zhu acknowledges that the Image's contrary description remains unclear. Cheng appeals instead to yin's occupancy of the highest place; Cai to the extreme of danger. None has been silently made the official explanation. The example demonstrates why positional correctness and the ethical or situational appropriateness of an action cannot always be treated as identical.

[^j11-song-beginnings]: **Preventing contention before it becomes a case.** 謀始 includes examining relationships, terms of agreement, foreseeable obstructions, and the consequences of undertaking an affair. The legal examples are the commentators' own. The Great Image's concern is broader than winning a lawsuit, and these historical observations are not presented as jurisdiction-specific legal advice.

[^j11-song-correspondence]: **Corresponding places do not guarantee agreement.** Cheng distinguishes relations between the first and fourth from those between the second and fifth. “Corresponding places” renders the positional relation without asserting that every pair has complementary yin and yang or a harmonious moral bond. In this hexagram the same broad spatial relation can support a defense or constitute opposition, depending on the lines and the situation.

[^j11-song-honor]: **Robes conferred through contention.** 服 denotes the insignia or robes of rank, not merely ordinary clothing. The Image evaluates the means of acquiring honor even before considering the later loss of that honor. Cai's comparison with Increase explains an incomplete statement: one objection is already sufficient, though a further objection remains. The English preserves this order of argument rather than reducing the passage to a warning that success may be temporary.

[^j11-army-people]: **The people and the military are not separate populations.** Zhu makes this analogy directly from water within Earth. Chen Chen expands it through land allocation, local communities, and military groupings. The several levels of organization are retained in English without pretending that their administrative sizes and functions remained unchanged in every period. The passage describes an idealized historical system, not a contemporary conscription proposal.

[^j11-army-heaven]: **“Heaven” explicitly means the king here.** Cheng glosses 天 with 王 in explaining the commander's commission. The English therefore preserves “Heaven” in the Image and the political identification in the commentary. Replacing every occurrence of Heaven in this work with “ruler” would be as misleading as insisting upon a separate divine agent in this particular sentence.

[^j11-army-corpses]: **Corpses or competing commanders.** 輿尸 retains the literal image of carrying corpses in the primary translation. Cheng and Yang Jian explain it through divided command, reading the same expression as several persons presiding over the undertaking. This is the same substantive disagreement recorded in Juan One. Their interpretation is not paraphrased back into a literal account of the dead, nor is the physical image silently removed from the canonical passage.

[^j11-army-left]: **Withdrawal and the ordinary rule.** “Camps to the left” preserves the line's concrete direction. The commentators treat this as withdrawal or a secondary military position and explain why appropriate retreat need not violate the constant rule. Their judgment depends on the circumstances and capacity of the fourth line; it is not a universal instruction to turn left in dangerous situations.

[^j11-army-rewards]: **Rewarding achievement is not the same as granting power.** The source distinguishes recognizing service, conferring a domain, and entrusting a person with office. Shao Bao explicitly allows reward without enfeoffment or enfeoffment without appointment. The warning against petty people thus concerns the use made of success and the authority granted afterward, not a requirement to deny that an unsuitable person ever achieved anything.

[^j11-army-elder-variant]: **An intelligible base reading retained.** Hu Bingwen's concluding contrast is 大人小人 in the base, “great persons and petty people.” The parallel transcription reads 丈人小人, “the seasoned elder and petty people,” recalling the Army's Judgment. Both fit the discussion; the English retains the base's 大人. This is a meaningful variant, not a correction justified merely because the parallel text differs.

[^j11-bi-states]: **Establishing states and drawing lords close.** 萬國 and 諸侯 describe the historical political world of domains and their lords, not modern sovereign nation-states. The Great Image is interpreted as the ruler going out to establish closeness, whereas the Judgment chiefly concerns others coming to the governing line. Zhu expressly points out this difference of direction.

[^j11-bi-text]: **An attested correction: 天下 → 天子.** In Zhu's recorded conversation, the base says that those whom “the world” draws close are the lords. The parallel transcription reads 天子, the Son of Heaven. The English adopts this reading: the ruler draws the lords close, and they in turn bring him into relation with the people. It preserves the precise chain of the political argument.

[^j11-bi-summons]: **Cultivation is not humiliating solicitation.** Cheng answers the objection that waiting for an invitation would amount to the latecoming condemned by the Judgment. Cultivating oneself is already a way of seeking service; lowering one's purpose and humiliating oneself are another matter. His examples of Yi Yin and Marquis Wu remain historical exempla, not rules that every reader must await a formal summons before acting.

[^j11-bi-hunt]: **Different hunting logics are deliberately contrasted.** Cheng distinguishes an ordinary ritual rule about taking disobedient game from this hexagram's image of accepting those who come and not pursuing those who leave. The terms 順 and 逆 depend on the direction of approach or departure in his explanation. Turning both discussions into the same hunting procedure would erase the distinction he is making.

[^j11-bi-head]: **Head, beginning, and ending.** 首 can name a head spatially and a beginning temporally. Zhu uses both dimensions; other commentators emphasize joining from the outset. This must not be conflated with Qian's “none at their head,” whose commentaries pursue a different problem. The same graph need not carry a single English equivalent in every hexagram.

[^j11-small-cultivation]: **Cultivated accomplishment on a smaller scale.** 文德 here includes literary and cultural accomplishments, contrasted by Cheng with the larger work of the Way and ordering the world. The English retains his distinction rather than making both kinds of cultivation indistinguishable. It is a comparison within this hexagram, not a claim that the compilation always regards 文 as mere ornament or learning as trivial.

[^j11-small-return]: **The direction of returning remains disputed.** The earlier line commentary offers competing accounts of return, advancement, and restraint. Here the Image emphasizes return by the proper Way and the second line's preservation of centeredness. “Return” is therefore kept where the source uses 復; a single physical direction is not supplied wherever the wording leaves it open.

[^j11-small-household]: **The husband's failure in the historical analogy.** The line's domestic hierarchy is retained. Cheng and Xiang place responsibility on the husband's failure to order his own conduct and household, rather than treating the wife's opposition as inexplicable malice. This is the source's patriarchal analogy, not a modern prescription for marital authority, and it has not been replaced with an inward masculine–feminine allegory.

[^j11-small-doubt]: **Anxiety or rivalry.** Cheng reads 疑 through apprehension of yin's growing opposition. Yang Jian connects it with Kun's 陰疑於陽 and gives it the force of rivalry or resistance. The primary English uses “grounds for doubt”; the comments make their different constructions visible. The discussion is interpretive, not evidence that a corrected Chinese graph has been recovered.

[^j11-treading-order]: **A historically ranked social order.** Cheng contrasts stable offices and occupations with an age in which everyone pursues higher rank and greater wealth. The social assumptions are not modernized into a general argument for equal opportunities or individual self-actualization. At the same time, his account allows promotion when rank fails to match virtue; “settling people's purposes” is not simply a ban on every change of station.

[^j11-treading-warrior]: **A warrior acting as ruler, or acting in royal service.** Cheng treats the martial figure as a warning about inadequate ability combined with an overbold purpose. Wang Shenzi allows a favorable use of martial determination in the king's service. Their different readings of 武人為于大君 are preserved within their own voices, rather than forcing either comment to confirm the other's construction.

[^j11-treading-correct-danger]: **Danger can arise from what one relies upon.** Zhu's 傷於所恃 identifies the danger in reliance on one's position or capacity. Cheng warns that occupying the highest proper place can encourage unilateral decisiveness without apprehension. Thus the Image's reference to a correct position does not reverse the line's warning of danger; it can explain the conditions that give rise to it.

[^j11-peace-assisting]: **Shaping and assisting the natural course.** 裁成 includes cutting, delimiting, and bringing to a usable form; 輔相 means assisting. The commentators exemplify these through seasons, directions, sowing, harvesting, and fitting crops to terrain. Their argument gives human institutions an active role without saying that the ruler creates Heaven and Earth's productive powers from nothing.

[^j11-peace-embracing]: **Why only “embracing the uncultivated” is repeated.** Cheng treats the quoted phrase as standing for the line's several qualities. The compilers instead argue that 包荒 is their governing foundation, made fitting by decisiveness, far-reaching discernment, and impartiality. 荒 has a broad range—roughness, uncultivated extent, and what lies outside polished order—so the English avoids narrowing it exclusively to one class of people or one terrain.

[^j11-peace-substance]: **“Losing substance” is not a single settled doctrine.** Zhu and Cheng connect it with yin's location above its ordinary place. Other commentators take the yin lines as lacking the substance on which they depend, or as inwardly empty and unpossessive. The compilers expressly reject deriving solidity and emptiness solely from high and low positions. The English leaves that rejection intact rather than treating every explanation as a synonym for weakness.

[^j11-peace-commands]: **Commands in disorder; an awkward connective.** 命 is interpreted here as commands or administrative direction, not automatically as fate. Cheng's base sentence contains 雖其命之亂, an awkward construction in context. The English renders the evident connection between disordered commands and collapse without claiming to have recovered a corrected wording for that connective. The reading remains unresolved; the preceding Zhu gloss also explicitly connects disordered commands with the return of Obstruction.

[^j11-obstruction-withdrawal]: **Withdrawing the display of virtue.** 儉德 is explained by Zhu as drawing virtue inward so that it does not appear externally. It does not mean deliberately becoming less virtuous. “Cannot be honored with salary” also preserves the possibility of refusing an inducement, while Cheng stresses the danger of occupying conspicuous office at such a time. These are related but not identical emphases.

[^j11-obstruction-allusions]: **Service, withdrawal, and inherited quotations.** Guo Yong presents an account handed down through his father and an earlier official; that layered attribution is retained. The paired concern for people in office and for the ruler in retirement illustrates the passage's argument, without making every retiree's motive identical. The basket-bearing recluse is invoked as a contrast to preserving the duties of ruler and minister; the English does not invent a personal name where this passage supplies only an allusive description.

[^j11-obstruction-action]: **Reversal does not remove human responsibility.** Cheng says that extremity reverses, but also requires the ability to turn danger into security. He Kai reads the wording as a demand that someone undertake the overturning of Obstruction. The translation therefore preserves both the cyclical claim and the practical requirement, not an assurance that doing nothing will inevitably restore order.

[^j11-fellowship-difference]: **Union without indiscriminate sameness.** 類族辨物 involves grouping by kind and discerning differences. Zhu explicitly denies that everything under Heaven can be made the same. The Great Image's discrimination is a condition of appropriate fellowship, not a repudiation of fellowship and not permission to erase moral or practical distinctions for the sake of unity.

[^j11-fellowship-clan]: **A proper correspondent can become an exclusive attachment.** The second and fifth occupy the usual corresponding places. Yet the compilers distinguish joining through private feeling from joining through correct principle. They argue that the hexagram's general account and the particular line's warning need not contradict one another. The English preserves this account alongside Cheng's stronger distinction between the whole figure and the line.

[^j11-fellowship-opponent]: **“Opponent” and “correspondent” are not seamlessly interchangeable here.** Cheng identifies the third line's formidable opponent as the fifth. The compilers gloss 敵 through 應 and invoke other hexagrams' corresponding partners. A strict first–fourth, second–fifth, third–top positional scheme does not by itself resolve that difference. The English reports the gloss rather than silently supplying a line number that would make both accounts agree. This interpretive tension remains open.

[^j11-fellowship-outskirts]: **The outskirts are not yet the open country.** 郊 and 野 are kept distinct. The compilers contrast the top line's freedom from regret in the outskirts with the Judgment's fuller success in the open country. Their distinction concerns the still-unfulfilled breadth of fellowship, not merely a recommended travel distance.

[^j11-great-possession-mandate]: **Heaven's beneficent mandate.** 休 carries goodness or beneficence here. The commentators interpret suppressing evil and promoting good as serving Heaven's mandate, and also apply the argument to one's own person. The normative claim that Heaven's mandate contains goodness rather than evil belongs to them; it is not inserted as an independently demonstrated proposition about providence.

[^j11-great-possession-offering]: **The lord's presentation to the Son of Heaven.** 亨 is read in the offering or presentation sense developed by the earlier commentary, not as an unrelated promise of success. Cheng extends the image through a lord's people, revenues, and obligations to the ruler. The contrast concerns public service versus appropriating these as private wealth and power.

[^j11-great-possession-peng]: **An uncertain expression remains uncertain.** 彭 is translated here through Cheng's explanation of abundance or flourishing, but its wider lexical and textual problems are not thereby resolved. “Discerning clearly” renders the Image's explanation, which warns against remaining at the extreme of prosperity. Readers should not infer that the English supplies an uncontested dictionary meaning of 彭 for all occurrences in the Changes.

[^j11-great-possession-authority]: **The fifth Image is split; its final clause has rival readings.** The base gives “sincerity in exchange” and “the good fortune of authority” in two separate primary-text blocks, both retained. Zhu and Cheng understand 易而無備 as being taken lightly and failing to prepare respectfully if authority is absent. Kong Yingda and the compilers understand ease without defensive precautions as the source or character of genuine authority. The primary translation leaves the compact clause visible; the Supplement preserves the latter interpretation as an explicitly separate voice.

[^j11-great-possession-top]: **The top line and the whole figure.** Xiang Anshi reads “Great Possession's good fortune at the top” as concerning the completion of the whole hexagram, not merely one individual's position. Zhao Yansu locates the governing cause in the fifth line's honoring the worthy above it. Cheng emphasizes the top line's conduct. The English retains these different subjects rather than converting every blessing into a prediction for the same actor.

[^j11-modesty-leveling]: **Personal humility and public redistribution.** Several commentators understand reducing excess and supplying deficiency as balancing one's estimation of self and others. Feng Yi instead reads it as governing a society so that differing things receive a fitting distribution. The compilers allow his extension to stand alongside the personal reading. “Level” therefore does not mean making every thing identical, nor does the entire discussion concern self-abasement alone.

[^j11-modesty-self-nurture]: **牧: tending, conducting, or nurturing oneself.** Cheng explains 自牧 as one's manner of placing or conducting oneself; Kong and Zhang Shi emphasize nurturing and tending. The latter compares it with taming animals. Cheng's citation from the Odes also contains 牧 in a concrete pastoral setting, so the quoted verse is not rewritten as though it directly concerned psychological self-cultivation. These are distinct uses of the graph within the source's argument.

[^j11-modesty-position]: **Preserving a position is an effect, not the motive.** Cheng explicitly rejects the inference that the noble person practices modesty merely to retain office, just as a good reputation need not be the motive for doing good. 存位 is therefore translated without making the whole argument an instrumental technique for career security. The third line's labor and achievement remain real; modesty does not require denying that the work was done.

[^j11-modesty-force]: **Modesty is not the same as never using force.** The fifth-line commentators permit a ruler to campaign against those whom cultivated virtue has not brought into compliance. At the top, the scope is limited to one's own domain; the compilers connect this with continuing self-correction. These are historical political arguments, not present-day instructions to coerce dissenters. The compilers also read the top line's unfulfilled purpose favorably, as refusal to be satisfied with one's own modesty, rather than only as failure.

[^j11-enthusiasm-music]: **Music, achievement, and sacrificial association.** 樂 here is music and ritual performance as well as delight. The source connects civil and martial achievements with different dances and associates ancestors with offerings to the Lord on High. 配 does not simply mean that ancestors are interchangeable with that deity. The translation retains the ritual institutions and attributions instead of replacing the passage with a generic claim that music promotes positive feelings.

[^j11-enthusiasm-illness]: **A chronic affliction as a political warning.** The commentators compare the fifth line's restrictions with an illness that keeps someone vigilant. Yang Shi also invokes corrective officials and external adversaries. Their assertion about prolonged life is part of a historical moral analogy, not medical evidence that illness is beneficial or should remain untreated. Centeredness, formal positional correctness, pleasure, and survival are given different roles in the argument.

[^j11-following-rest]: **Does the lake move, or does thunder retire?** Cheng emphasizes the lake following thunder's movement and generalizes to acting with the time. Zhu challenges that reading because the prescribed conduct is rest, and emphasizes thunder's seasonal concealment. The English keeps the objection explicit. The daily example of retiring at dusk is not made into proof that both commentators explain the natural image in precisely the same way.

[^j11-following-readings]: **An evident contextual repair in the quotation about dusk.** The base's Cheng paragraph contains 君子以向嚮入宴息, where the repeated direction-words disrupt the quotation. The English follows the primary passage's 嚮晦, “toward dusk,” supported by the paragraph's ensuing explanation of approaching darkness. This is an internal contextual correction; a corrected reading at this exact place in the parallel Zhezhong transcription was not verified. 向 and 嚮 themselves need not be treated as different concepts in this setting.

[^j11-following-choice]: **An exclusive choice of attachment.** The second cannot bind itself to both the first and fifth. The compilers explain why the hexagram's meaning changes what would otherwise be expected from the usual second–fifth correspondence. Their claim concerns the configuration and situation, not a universal rule that all relationships with more than one person are inherently improper.

[^j11-following-man]: **夫 restored for 失.** The base primary text has 係丈失, an evident disruption of 係丈夫, “bound to the grown man.” The accompanying comments, the contrast with the young boy, and the earlier line statement all establish the intended expression. The English corrects it; this is documented as a contextual correction, not as a newly verified facsimile reading. The correction does not alter which line the commentators identify as the person followed.

[^j11-following-binding]: **Tenacious following or an elusive person brought into relation.** Cheng and Zhu emphasize the utmost persistence of attachment. The compilers emphasize the top line's elevated separation from the world, explaining why it is exceptionally difficult to bind. The former describes the strength of following; the latter helps identify whom the binding addresses. Neither has been silently discarded in favor of a single contemporary relationship metaphor.

[^j11-decay-nurture]: **Whose virtue is nurtured?** Zhu and Cheng distinguish cultivating oneself from aiding the people. Several collected commentators take both 振民 and 育德 as work upon the people and their character; the compilers prefer that reading partly because of word order. The English consequently uses different possessives where the different voices require them. This is not an accidental inconsistency to be harmonized in revision.

[^j11-decay-intention]: **Succeeding to intention can require correcting the inherited action.** The source distinguishes preserving a parent's loyal or benevolent intention from continuing practices that have become defective. Some commentators locate the intention in the child; Yang Qixin also allows the predecessor's own possible desire to repent. The English retains those nuances. “Filial” conduct here need not mean simply reproducing what the parent did.

[^j11-decay-virtue]: **The minister's virtue or the father's virtue?** Cheng reads 承以德 as the worthy person below supporting the fifth with firm, centered virtue. Xiang and the compilers relate it to succeeding to and making manifest the father's virtue. The compilers explicitly compare this with the first line's succession in intention. Their disagreement concerns both the subject of 承 and the way praise is obtained.

[^j11-decay-nonservice]: **Withdrawal with an exemplary purpose.** The top line's refusal to serve kings and lords is praised in relation to its purpose and timing. Cheng does not treat every absence from office as virtuous, and the passage does not prescribe permanent withdrawal for all readers. The ideal of advancing or retiring according to the Way remains part of the explanation.

[^j11-approach-teaching]: **An intention to educate, not teaching alone.** 教思 includes the sustained concern that someone should learn and become accomplished. 容保 adds capacious acceptance to protection. Cai's language of the people as one's siblings and of those without help expands the practical scope of that concern. The translation preserves both the ethical breadth and the historical ruler–people framework within which it is expressed.

[^j11-approach-command]: **“Not following the command”: an acknowledged difficulty.** Zhu says 未詳, “not yet clear.” Cheng understands 未 as denying mere compliance with a superior's command, rather than always meaning “not yet.” The compilers take 命 in relation to Heaven's waxing and waning and distinguish following the Way from passively relying on favorable circumstances. These are alternative explanations. The primary translation leaves the negative clause intact instead of supplying one interpretation as the recovered original meaning.

[^j11-approach-inward]: **Inside and outside depend on where one stands.** The compilers compare Approach's top line, whose purpose is within, with Peace's first line, whose purpose is outside. Both concern the world and the state: the spatial description changes with the line's position. Thus “inward” here need not mean withdrawal into private introspection, nor “outward” mere pursuit of external advantage.

[^j11-viewing-two-directions]: **Observing and being observed.** 觀 moves between looking at something and presenting an example for others to view. Touring the regions lets the ruler observe the people; establishing teaching gives the people something to observe. The English makes the changes of subject explicit where the comments require them, rather than translating every occurrence as inward contemplation.

[^j11-viewing-woman]: **A gendered limit in the historical argument.** The commentators regard a restricted view as tolerable within the woman's assigned role but shameful for the noble man expected to comprehend the greater Way. This asymmetry is preserved, not endorsed, and not rewritten as a timeless distinction between inward feminine and outward masculine energies. The comparison with Constancy belongs to the commentators themselves.

[^j11-viewing-guest]: **Seeking guest status or honoring a worthy guest.** Cheng reads 尚 as the observer's aspiration to be a guest at court and serve the Way. Yang Jian instead takes the state to honor worthy guests, making advancement appropriate. The primary expression “esteeming the guest” keeps the ambiguity visible; the comments specify their different subjects.

[^j11-viewing-self-people]: **One's conduct is assessed through its effects on the people.** 生 here concerns what proceeds from oneself, not merely one's biological life. Zhu's base gloss has 現己所行, where the English reads “observe one's conduct” in line with 觀我生 and the entire explanation. Taking 現 as an apparent error for 觀 is a contextual intervention, not a reading independently confirmed from the parallel transcription. The argument goes beyond self-inspection to the character and condition of the governed.

[^j11-biting-order]: **Thunder–lightning or lightning–thunder?** The base primary text reads 雷電. Zhu proposes 電雷, and Cheng suspects an inversion because the usual order of the two images would place the upper trigram first. Zhang Qingzi reports the latter reading in Cai Yong's Stone Classics. That report is translated as textual evidence cited by the commentator; the stone inscription itself was not inspected here. The primary English retains the base's order, while the proposed correction remains in the commentary.

[^j11-biting-toes]: **Concealment and injury must not be conflated.** 滅 can describe being covered or obliterated in these bodily images. Cheng explicitly speaks of injury from the wooden restraint; Zhu adds the image of being unable to advance into wrongdoing. The English retains the physical device and Cheng's harsher language. It does not present historical corporal punishment as appropriate contemporary practice or turn the whole scene into a purely inward exercise.

[^j11-biting-position]: **A difficult situation need not mean wrongful action.** Cheng connects the third line's improper position with resistance to its punishments. The compilers insist that the line image indicates the difficulty of its situation, not that its conduct is itself improper; otherwise “no blame” would be untenable. Similarly, the fifth line's “attains what is fitting” is explained through how it judges, rather than its formal position alone.

[^j11-biting-ears]: **Hearing and understanding.** 聰 concerns acute hearing and receptive discernment; 明 concerns clarity. Lin Xiyuan distinguishes their relationship when used separately or together. The neck restraint and the covered or injured ears remain concrete, but the commentary also charges a failure to heed warnings. The translation does not reduce 聰 throughout to the modern category of intellectual ability.

[^j11-adornment-judging]: **Administrative clarity is not a license for hasty judgment.** The commentaries contrast illuminating ordinary government with deciding cases that demand particular caution. Zhu further distinguishes deciding too early from leaving a case unresolved after the proper stages are complete. “Does not dare adjudicate” therefore needs its surrounding explanations; it is not a timeless prohibition against any exercise of judgment by a cultivated person.

[^j11-adornment-legal-words]: **The darker possibilities of 文.** He Kai exploits 文's range across patterning, textual elaboration, and manipulation of legal language. His sequence of legal expressions warns that ingenuity can conceal the actual circumstances and produce injustice. The wordplay cannot be reproduced by a single English noun. The individual expressions are rendered according to his argument, while the note identifies the shared Chinese term.

[^j11-adornment-beard]: **Which line is “above”?** Several voices identify the beard's support with the third nine. Xiang Anshi's later concluding discussion takes “rising with above” in relation to the top nine and his account of the hexagram's formation. The compilers reject that identification in favor of the third. The English preserves the final objection; it is not merely an alternative name for the same line.

[^j11-adornment-white-horse]: **The suspected raider and the marriage partner.** Cheng treats an intervening third line as preventing reunion with the proper partner; Guo identifies the arriving first line as the non-raiding marriage-seeker. Zhu's explanation remains conditional on preserving correctness and not joining improperly. These different assignments are not silently merged. 白 and 皤 preserve the white or unadorned image without adding a modern symbolic system of color.

[^j11-adornment-white]: **Plainness can precede and complete adornment.** Xiang's argument is that plain substance lies both before the emergence of colors and after their full development. Zhu's simpler account concerns a person beyond affairs who needs no applied decoration. The two are retained together. White adornment is not rendered “no culture”: the source can distinguish natural pattern from ornament added for display.

[^j11-splitting-foundation]: **Nourishing those below preserves the one above.** The mountain–earth image links the security of the ruler with the strength of the people who support him. 厚下 is therefore translated as strengthening or nourishing below, not merely securing obedience. The statement that the people are the state's root is quoted and explained within the historical political argument.

[^j11-splitting-ally]: **Who lacks an ally?** Cheng emphasizes yang or the noble person lacking support against encroachment. Cui, Wu, and Gong instead emphasize the second yin line's lack of a yang partner: with such a partner it might follow and assist rather than strip away. The compilers prefer the latter construction. This is a real difference of subject, and the English does not conceal it behind an unspecified “support.”

[^j11-splitting-frame]: **An uncertain bed-fitting and a corrupt-looking graph.** In Cui Jing's gloss, the base reads 辨當在第足之間. 第 is difficult here; the English renders the evidently intended space between the bed's surface and its feet, rather than translating “rank” or “sequence.” A reading such as 簀, a bed surface or mat, is contextually plausible but was not verified in a consulted witness at this place. The intervention remains provisional; the further identification with the bed's supporting frame is the commentator's own.

[^j11-splitting-palace]: **The queenly and palace-women image.** The compilers take yin in the honored place as a queenly figure who orders the palace women, rather than destroys the ruler through jealousy and disorder. This preserves the actual institutional metaphor. The top line subsequently contrasts what the noble person and petty person do at the same extremity; it need not mean that the top nine has suddenly become a petty person by definition.

[^j11-return-solstice]: **Protecting a beginning that has already stirred.** The commentators identify the 至日 here with the winter solstice. Zhu distinguishes stillness before the first yang from the Great Image's quiet protection after it has begun. This is important to the ethical analogy: a nascent good impulse is nurtured rather than forced. The historical closing of passes and suspension of travel is retained as the text's ritual practice, not prescribed for a modern reader's calendar.

[^j11-return-wang-bi]: **A transmitted criticism, not an editorial correction.** Su Shunqin challenges Wang Bi's account of return through stillness and the absence of things, including the seasonal wording attributed to him. The translation preserves what Su quotes and argues rather than silently repairing the quotation to make his opponent agree. His references to “firmness returns” and “firmness grows” occur in the Judgment Commentary; the base labels them 象 more broadly. “Commentary” in the English avoids falsely locating them among these Small Images.

[^j11-return-benevolence]: **A correction supported by a separate transmission.** Cheng's base gloss reads 為復之休美而占者. The English follows 吉, “auspicious,” as transmitted in [Dong Kai's *Zhouyi zhuanyi fulu*, juan 5, upper part](https://zh.wikisource.org/zh-hant/周易傳義附録_(四庫全書本)/卷05上): 為復之休美而吉者. This is evidence from another transmission of Cheng, not a claimed facsimile correction in this Zhezhong edition. Zhang Shi's ensuing remark about 仁 distinguishes the oracle's line statements from their later explanation here; it is not contradicted merely by the occurrence of 仁 in the Small Image.

[^j11-return-examining]: **Completing oneself or examining oneself.** Zhu and Cheng explain 考 as completion. Liang Yin emphasizes examining the goodness or otherwise of one's conduct. The translation retains these different senses rather than replacing both with “self-awareness.” The fifth line's 自 also distinguishes its return from those readings in which the second and fourth depend directly upon the first.

[^j11-return-heart-ruler]: **The ruler and the heart as ruler.** Cheng explains 君道 politically and then extends the warning to anyone who fails to return to the Way. Yang Qixin reads the heart as 天君, the governing lord of the person. The English preserves both levels. This is a historical ethical account of self-government, not an anatomical or psychological model newly supplied by the translator.

[^j11-without-falsity-nature]: **Giving each being its own nature.** The source connects 无妄 with each thing's 性命, its nature and Heaven-given allotment. The former kings nurture beings in accord with their seasons and natures rather than imposing private contrivance. “Without Falsity” therefore has a wider scope than telling the truth. The commentators' parallel with sincerity in the Doctrine of the Mean is retained as their explanation.

[^j11-without-falsity-harvest]: **Work and calculation of its reward.** The earlier oracle's compressed plowing-and-harvesting expression admits competing readings. Here Cheng and Feng emphasize not undertaking the work merely to calculate its eventual profit. “Not plowing for the harvest” makes that contextual reading legible, without asserting that the text promises a crop without cultivation. 未 is also explained as a negative that need not mean only “not yet.”

[^j11-without-falsity-calamity]: **Innocence does not guarantee immunity.** Cheng emphasizes that one person's gain is another's loss; Feng discusses the person wrongfully accused who finds no shame on examining himself. Both preserve the possibility of undeserved disaster. The English does not turn 无妄 into a promise that a morally upright person will never suffer external harm.

[^j11-without-falsity-medicine]: **A classical illness oracle, not treatment advice.** The source argues against altering what already accords with principle merely because an unexpected affliction appears. Its comments retain the literal medicinal image, while also applying it to moral and practical conduct. These statements are not present-day medical guidance and should not be used to avoid diagnosis, prescribed treatment, or other appropriate care. The distinction between an ethical analogy and a medical decision is the translator's caution, not a qualification put into the commentators' mouths.

[^j11-great-restraint-learning]: **Remembering in order to embody, not merely accumulate.** 識 here includes learning and retaining earlier words and deeds. Cheng asks the reader to infer the speakers' intentions and the practical use of their actions; Yang Shi rejects a contest in quantities of information and display. The Great Image's apparently impossible “Heaven within the mountain” is also explicitly allowed by Zhu as an image without a corresponding literal event.

[^j11-great-restraint-wagon]: **Deliberate immobilization.** 輹 concerns the carriage's fastening, not simply a wheel spoke. The second line's removal or release of it is understood as refraining from movement after judging the circumstances. The English preserves that agency where the commentary gives it, instead of making every occurrence an accidental breakdown. The precise ancient construction is not reconstructed from the phrase alone.

[^j11-great-restraint-animals]: **Restraint before and after strength develops.** The young ox's horn-board prevents a dangerous habit before it matures; the castrated boar illustrates reducing violent force after it has developed. The source distinguishes the ease of early prevention from the wider public benefit available to a ruler. These concrete historical images remain, without becoming instructions for animal handling or a claim that all strength should be suppressed.

[^j11-great-restraint-highway]: **A disputed 何.** Cheng takes the Image's 何 as introducing a question—why call this Heaven's highway?—and argues that it was mistakenly carried into the line statement. Other voices read an exclamatory or otherwise continuous phrase. The primary translation retains the base's wording, while Cheng's proposal is reported in full. No historical leaf was examined here to adjudicate his emendation.

[^j11-nourishment-speech]: **What leaves and enters the mouth, then the polity.** Speech and food first concern the person. Cheng and Yu extend them to commands, teaching, resources, and taxation. This extension is in the source, not a modern organizational analogy added to it. The discussion of moderation remains an ethical and political argument; the translation is not intended as a nutritional regimen.

[^j11-nourishment-gen]: **A contextual correction: 良 → 艮.** Cheng's base paragraph reads 良止而震動. The paired trigrams, the Great Image, and the rest of the explanation make 艮, Gen, the intended term: Gen stops and Zhen moves. The English corrects the graph. This is a direct contextual repair, not a reading independently verified from the parallel Zhezhong transcription at this location.

[^j11-nourishment-steadfastness]: **Where 貞 belongs in the sentence.** Xiang joins 拂頤貞, “opposing nourishment's correctness,” instead of treating 貞 simply as the person's persistence in the conduct. His comparison with the Judgment makes the disagreement explicit. The English retains his construction without forcing all other occurrences of 貞 into the same syntax.

[^j11-nourishment-cooperation]: **The ruler and the worthy above him.** The fifth depends upon the top nine to nourish the world. Xiang also makes the top's success depend upon the fifth's commission. This reciprocal account is not identical to a claim that either line succeeds entirely through self-sufficiency. The Small Images are being used to specify both the relation and the meaning of steadfastness in it.

[^j11-great-excess-alone]: **Standing alone and withdrawing from the age.** The source distinguishes public action that withstands opposition from retirement that does not become resentful when unrecognized. Other commentators align these with being employed or set aside. The language is not a claim that isolation proves one's correctness; it describes the extraordinary steadiness required of the figure under discussion.

[^j11-great-excess-lines]: **Qualitative excess is not a mechanical count.** Xiang calls the second uniquely free of excess and the third uniquely excessive in his explanation of the bent ridgepole. He Kai later contrasts the second–first pair with the fifth–top pair more broadly. Their arguments select different relationships and emphases. The English does not turn either into a universal arithmetic rule that would erase the other lines' stated meanings.

[^j11-great-excess-flowering]: **Renewal at the root and expenditure at the tip.** 稊 is explained as a new shoot from the root; 華 as flowering. The source uses this contrast to interpret an old tree and the possibility of generation in different pairings. Its historical judgments about age, marriage, and reproduction are retained as its judgments, not offered as a complete modern biological account or as advice about an individual relationship.

[^j11-great-excess-blame]: **No one else to blame, or no moral blame.** Cheng construes the loss as self-incurred and therefore leaving no one else to reproach. Su Shi instead allows a disastrous attempt to rescue others that nevertheless cannot be morally blamed. The same 無咎/不可咎 language thus receives materially different explanations. The English does not conceal this under a single formula of “no blame.”

[^j11-danger-practice]: **Repetition, instruction, and trained response.** 習 is both recurrence and practice. Cheng applies 習教事 chiefly to making commands and teaching familiar enough for people to follow; Su Shi includes matters that must be learned before one can respond competently to danger. “Practices the work of instruction” preserves the common phrase, while the commentaries establish their distinct practical applications.

[^j11-danger-noble-person]: **An evident contextual correction: 予 → 子.** The base Great Image reads 君予. The English restores 君子, “the noble person,” consistently with the formula throughout the Great Images and with the commentaries immediately following. This is documented as a contextual correction. It is not claimed as the result of inspecting a historical printed page at this point.

[^j11-danger-center]: **The middle of danger or centered virtue.** “Not yet out of the center” can refer to remaining within the pit of danger. Guo Yong also emphasizes not departing from the ethical center, while Xu Wenzhi warns against demanding immediate, total escape. The English leaves their different emphases visible: an inwardly steady response and an outwardly unchanged predicament are not the same claim.

[^j11-danger-two-bowls]: **Whether 貳 belongs to the quoted phrase.** The base Small Image includes 樽酒簋貳. Zhu, reporting Chao and Lu's *Explanations of Texts*, favors a text without 貳 here. Jiang Bao appeals to the Small Image's inclusion of 貳 against attaching it to 用缶 in the earlier line. The translation preserves the base phrase and their disagreement. The vessel names are ritual terms, not interchangeable references to an ordinary drinking party.

[^j11-danger-fullness]: **An unfilled pit, or water that does not overflow.** Cheng treats the fifth line's “not full” as an unfinished overcoming of danger and an achievement not yet great. Zhu and several others emphasize remaining level without excess; Xiang distinguishes the continuity of flowing water from the filling of a particular hollow. These explanations cannot all be reduced to one physical description. Their differences are retained rather than resolved by replacing every occurrence with “balanced.”

[^j11-danger-three-years]: **A term of confinement and a moral conditional.** Cheng stresses prolonged danger, explaining three years as an extreme duration for imprisonment. Zhu Zhen contrasts patient self-examination with rash attempts to escape and gives a different account of the possible ending. Their historical claims about confinement are translated as such, not verified here as the law of any particular dynasty and not represented as current legal guidance.

[^j11-clinging-successive-light]: **One brightness, successive manifestations.** Zhu emphasizes the same brightness arising again on the following day. Cheng gives a major political example: luminous virtue continued across generations. Both are preserved. 明兩 does not require imagining two physical suns, and successive illumination need not everywhere mean dynastic succession.

[^j11-clinging-decline]: **A setting sun or an obscured heart.** Cheng uses the afternoon sun to discuss decline, succession, and retirement. The compilers instead take it as the obscuring of the heart's virtue. Their short objection is consequential: it moves the explanation from an external life-stage to the quality of understanding. The translation does not silently make either interpretation control the other.

[^j11-clinging-royal-position]: **The fifth place need not mean one universal emperor.** Cai Qing argues from 王公 that a lord ruling his own state can also occupy the figure's honored fifth position. This is the commentator's own qualification of the usual ruler-image. The English consequently retains “kings and lords” where required, rather than making every fifth line refer exclusively to the Son of Heaven.
